# Daily Summary: 2025-04-17

### Video Summarization with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11199v1)
- **Authors**: Min Jung Lee, Dayoung Gong, Minsu Cho
- **Abstract**: The exponential increase in video content poses significant challenges in terms of efficient navigation, search, and retrieval, thus requiring advanced video summarization techniques. Existing video summarization methods, which heavily rely on visual features and temporal dynamics, often fail to capture the semantics of video content, resulting in incomplete or incoherent summaries. To tackle the challenge, we propose a new video summarization framework that leverages the capabilities of recent Large Language Models (LLMs), expecting that the knowledge learned from massive data enables LLMs to evaluate video frames in a manner that better aligns with diverse semantics and human judgments, effectively addressing the inherent subjectivity in defining keyframes. Our method, dubbed LLM-based Video Summarization (LLMVS), translates video frames into a sequence of captions using a Muti-modal Large Language Model (M-LLM) and then assesses the importance of each frame using an LLM, based on the captions in its local context. These local importance scores are refined through a global attention mechanism in the entire context of video captions, ensuring that our summaries effectively reflect both the details and the overarching narrative. Our experimental results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method over existing ones in standard benchmarks, highlighting the potential of LLMs in the processing of multimedia content.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLMVS, a novel video summarization framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) for keyframe selection.  Unlike traditional methods that rely heavily on visual features, LLMVS uses LLMs to evaluate frames based on their semantic content and contextual relevance. The framework first generates textual captions for video frames using a Multi-modal Large Language Model (M-LLM).  Then, an LLM assesses the importance of each frame by considering its captions within a local temporal window. These local importance scores are then refined using a global attention mechanism that accounts for the entire video context, ensuring both detail and narrative coherence. The approach is evaluated on standard video summarization benchmarks (SumMe and TVSum), demonstrating superior performance compared to existing methods. The paper highlights the potential of LLMs for processing multimedia content beyond visual features alone.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its shift from visual-centric to semantics-driven video summarization using LLMs.  While M-LLMs have been used in other video understanding tasks, their application as central "frame selectors" for summarization, guided by textual data and global context aggregation, is a distinctive contribution.  The use of in-context learning to leverage the LLM's pre-trained knowledge is well-executed. *However, the core components - captioning, importance scoring, and attention - are not entirely new individually, but the combination and the specific application of LLMs to video summarization makes the approach novel.*

*   **Significance:** The results show state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks. The reported improvement over existing methods is significant. Importantly, the paper challenges the traditional emphasis on visual saliency, suggesting that semantic understanding is crucial for high-quality video summaries. This could shift future research toward more sophisticated, language-aware approaches to multimedia processing. The identified challenges in capturing diverse human preferences, especially on TVSum, point to areas for improvement. The paper demonstrates effective use of LLMs (specifically LLaVA and Llama-2), providing a strong basis for further exploration of the framework.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong experimental results demonstrating state-of-the-art performance.
    *   Clear description of the framework and its components.
    *   Well-motivated approach based on semantic understanding.
    *   Insightful analysis of the role of different LLM components.
    *   Comprehensive ablation studies validate design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the method beats prior SOTA, the experimental setup is somewhat dependent on the "frozen" pre-trained models. Finetuning these pre-trained models may have given higher performance in general, though the paper mentions why this was avoided.
    *   The study could benefit from more extensive qualitative analysis. While qualitative results are provided, a more in-depth discussion of failure cases and the types of errors the model makes would be valuable.
    *   The paper highlights TVSum benchmark's subjectivity. Future iterations can improve performance or focus on datasets where diversity in summarization preference is more clearly noted, or less pronounced.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field of video summarization by encouraging researchers to explore the potential of LLMs for multimedia understanding. The LLMVS framework provides a strong foundation for future work, and the insights gained from the experiments will be valuable for designing more effective video summarization systems. Specifically, this will push for the usage of language understanding for more complex video tasks, where visual features are only one component of the decision.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to video summarization using LLMs, achieving state-of-the-art results on standard benchmarks. The shift from visual-centric methods to semantic understanding is a significant contribution. While the components themselves are not all fundamentally new, the combination and application within video summarization, specifically leveraging LLMs pre-trained knowledge and global context aggregation, is novel. The limitations of this study, specifically that the pre-trained models were frozen and the qualitative analysis could have been more thorough, are the main reasons the score is not higher. It is a strong research contribution likely to influence the direction of the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### VEXP: A Low-Cost RISC-V ISA Extension for Accelerated Softmax Computation in Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11227v1)
- **Authors**: Run Wang, Gamze Islamoglu, Andrea Belano, Viviane Potocnik, Francesco Conti, Angelo Garofalo, Luca Benini
- **Abstract**: While Transformers are dominated by Floating-Point (FP) Matrix-Multiplications, their aggressive acceleration through dedicated hardware or many-core programmable systems has shifted the performance bottleneck to non-linear functions like Softmax. Accelerating Softmax is challenging due to its non-pointwise, non-linear nature, with exponentiation as the most demanding step. To address this, we design a custom arithmetic block for Bfloat16 exponentiation leveraging a novel approximation algorithm based on Schraudolph's method, and we integrate it into the Floating-Point Unit (FPU) of the RISC-V cores of a compute cluster, through custom Instruction Set Architecture (ISA) extensions, with a negligible area overhead of 1\%. By optimizing the software kernels to leverage the extension, we execute Softmax with 162.7$\times$ less latency and 74.3$\times$ less energy compared to the baseline cluster, achieving an 8.2$\times$ performance improvement and 4.1$\times$ higher energy efficiency for the FlashAttention-2 kernel in GPT-2 configuration. Moreover, the proposed approach enables a multi-cluster system to efficiently execute end-to-end inference of pre-trained Transformer models, such as GPT-2, GPT-3 and ViT, achieving up to 5.8$\times$ and 3.6$\times$ reduction in latency and energy consumption, respectively, without requiring re-training and with negligible accuracy loss.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VEXP: A Low-Cost RISC-V ISA Extension for Accelerated Softmax Computation in Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper presents VEXP, a hardware acceleration technique focused on improving the efficiency of Softmax computation in Transformer models. Recognizing that Softmax has become a bottleneck after aggressive GEMM acceleration, the authors design a custom arithmetic block for Bfloat16 exponentiation, integrated into the Floating-Point Unit (FPU) of RISC-V cores within a compute cluster. This is achieved through custom ISA extensions, incurring minimal area overhead. The VEXP extension utilizes a Schraudolph's method-based approximation algorithm for exponentiation.  By optimizing software kernels to leverage VEXP, the authors demonstrate significant improvements in latency, energy efficiency, and overall performance for Transformer inference, particularly in FlashAttention-2. The proposed approach aims to avoid retraining, maintain accuracy, and minimize the area/power impact on the system. It's also scalable for multi-cluster configurations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific approach to accelerating Softmax using a low-cost, tightly integrated RISC-V ISA extension. While previous works have explored hardware acceleration for Softmax and exponentiation, the combination of a Schraudolph's-based exponentiation approximation, an FPU-integrated ISA extension, the focus on Bfloat16 precision, and its integration into a RISC-V multi-cluster system is relatively novel. The detail of implementation and integration into a full system, the benchmarking, and the detailed results also add to the novelty. The use of software optimizations alongside hardware acceleration is also well handled. The enhancement of Schraudolph's method in the paper is also a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical bottleneck in Transformer inference. As GEMM operations are increasingly accelerated, the non-linear functions like Softmax become the limiting factor. This paper provides a practical, low-overhead solution to alleviate this bottleneck, making Transformer deployment more efficient on resource-constrained devices or systems. The fact that the design doesn't require retraining is also a significant practical benefit. The multi-cluster setup also makes this design relevant to realistic AI applications. The extensive evaluation, including accuracy, performance, energy consumption, and scalability analysis, strengthens the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Low Overhead:** The solution's key strength is its minimal area overhead (1%) at the cluster level, making it a practical and cost-effective solution.
    *   **Native Precision:** The focus on Bfloat16, a widely used precision in Transformers, is a strength.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** Thorough evaluation on multiple Transformer models (GPT-2, GPT-3, ViT) demonstrates the effectiveness and generalizability of the approach.  The evaluation considers both performance and energy consumption, which is essential.
    *   **Scalability:** The scalability analysis indicates the suitability of the approach for larger systems.
    *   **Software Co-optimization:** Optimization of the kernels to best utilize the extension and the existing system is a strength.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Comparison:** While the paper compares against other Softmax accelerators, a more detailed comparison against other *general* approximation methods for exponentiation (e.g., different forms of LUTs or more sophisticated piecewise approximations) could further strengthen the argument. However, the comparison with accelerators for Softmax compensates for this.
    *   **Specific Architecture:** The solution is tightly coupled with the Snitch RISC-V architecture. While this allows for deep integration and low overhead, it might limit its direct applicability to other platforms. A more generalized description of the core idea might improve potential wider adoption. However, focusing on a concrete platform and evaluating this in detail allows for a convincing demonstration of the concept.

*   **Impact:** The paper's impact is likely to be in informing the design of future RISC-V-based AI accelerators. The VEXP approach offers a viable path toward accelerating Softmax without significant hardware costs or retraining efforts. It demonstrates the value of ISA extensions for addressing specific computational bottlenecks in emerging workloads.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated approach to a significant problem in Transformer acceleration. The focus on low overhead, native precision, and thorough evaluation makes it a valuable contribution. While the architecture-specific nature is a potential limitation, the demonstrated results and the lessons learned from the integration into the RISC-V platform are highly relevant to the field.

### Nondeterministic Polynomial-time Problem Challenge: An Ever-Scaling Reasoning Benchmark for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11239v1)
- **Authors**: Chang Yang, Ruiyu Wang, Junzhe Jiang, Qi Jiang, Qinggang Zhang, Yanchen Deng, Shuxin Li, Shuyue Hu, Bo Li, Florian T. Pokorny, Xiao Huang, Xinrun Wang
- **Abstract**: Reasoning is the fundamental capability of large language models (LLMs). Due to the rapid progress of LLMs, there are two main issues of current benchmarks: i) these benchmarks can be crushed in a short time (less than 1 year), and ii) these benchmarks may be easily hacked. To handle these issues, we propose the ever-scalingness for building the benchmarks which are uncrushable, unhackable, auto-verifiable and general. This paper presents Nondeterministic Polynomial-time Problem Challenge (NPPC), an ever-scaling reasoning benchmark for LLMs. Specifically, the NPPC has three main modules: i) npgym, which provides a unified interface of 25 well-known NP-complete problems and can generate any number of instances with any levels of complexities, ii) npsolver: which provides a unified interface to evaluate the problem instances with both online and offline models via APIs and local deployments, respectively, and iii) npeval: which provides the comprehensive and ready-to-use tools to analyze the performances of LLMs over different problems, the number of tokens, the aha moments, the reasoning errors and the solution errors. Extensive experiments over widely-used LLMs demonstrate: i) NPPC can successfully decrease the performances of advanced LLMs' performances to below 10%, demonstrating that NPPC is uncrushable, ii) DeepSeek-R1, Claude-3.7-Sonnet, and o1/o3-mini are the most powerful LLMs, where DeepSeek-R1 outperforms Claude-3.7-Sonnet and o1/o3-mini in most NP-complete problems considered, and iii) the numbers of tokens, aha moments in the advanced LLMs, e.g., Claude-3.7-Sonnet and DeepSeek-R1, are observed first to increase and then decrease when the problem instances become more and more difficult. We believe that NPPC is the first ever-scaling reasoning benchmark, serving as the uncrushable and unhackable testbed for LLMs toward artificial general intelligence (AGI).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Nondeterministic Polynomial-time Problem Challenge: An Ever-Scaling Reasoning Benchmark for LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Nondeterministic Polynomial-time Problem Challenge (NPPC), a new benchmark designed to evaluate and push the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  NPPC aims to address the limitations of existing benchmarks which are often quickly "crushed" or easily "hacked." The key idea is to leverage NP-complete (NPC) problems, which are inherently hard to solve but easy to verify, providing a scalable and robust assessment framework.  The NPPC consists of three main modules: `npgym` (a unified interface for 25 well-known NPC problems allowing for instance generation and verification), `npsolver` (a unified interface for evaluating LLMs through APIs or local deployments), and `npeval` (tools for comprehensive performance analysis, including token usage, "aha moments", and error analysis). The authors perform extensive experiments with popular LLMs, demonstrating NPPC's ability to differentiate model performance and reveal limitations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects. First, the focus on NPC problems as a foundation for a reasoning benchmark is a compelling approach.  While previous benchmarks have used reasoning tasks, the systematic use of complexity theory to ensure scalability and verifiability is a significant departure.  Second, the design of the NPPC with its three interconnected modules provides a comprehensive framework for generating, solving, and evaluating reasoning tasks.  The inclusion of "aha moment" analysis is a particularly interesting feature. The framework offers a modular structure for expansion as well.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical issue in the LLM evaluation landscape: the rapid obsolescence and exploitability of static benchmarks. The "ever-scalingness" concept, realized through the use of NP-complete problems, offers a potential solution to this problem. If adopted and refined, NPPC could serve as a more enduring measure of LLM reasoning capabilities, pushing models towards more genuine problem-solving rather than memorization or shortcutting.  The focus on problems that are reducible to NP, making it more relevant to real world problems (navigation and planning), also enhances its potential impact. The comprehensive analysis provided by `npeval` facilitates a deeper understanding of LLM reasoning strengths and weaknesses, driving future research directions.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong theoretical foundation:**  The reliance on NP-completeness provides a principled approach to benchmark design, ensuring both difficulty and verifiability.
    * **Scalability:**  The framework's ability to generate instances with varying complexity levels makes it potentially resistant to being "crushed" by future LLM advancements.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The inclusion of `npeval` allows for detailed analysis beyond simple accuracy metrics, offering valuable insights into LLM reasoning processes.
    * **Modular and extensible design:** The NPPC design allows for expansion by including more problem types, and incorporating new complexity measures.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited "real-world" relevance demonstrated:** While many real-world problems *can* be reduced to NPC problems, the paper doesn't provide extensive examples of how specific real-world reasoning tasks directly map to the included NPC problems.  A more compelling demonstration of relevance would strengthen the argument for NPPC's significance.
    * **Prompt engineering dependence:**  The performance of LLMs is known to be highly sensitive to prompt design.  While the paper mentions a consistent prompt template, a more thorough discussion of prompt engineering choices and their potential impact on results would be beneficial. It would be useful to analyze the robustness of results against prompt variations.
    * **Focus on decision problems:** While NP problems are decision problems, where a proposed solution can be easily verified, some real-world problems are optimization problems, where finding an optimal solution is difficult. It could be beneficial to include optimization problems as well.

* **Potential Influence:**  NPPC has the potential to become a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working on LLM reasoning.  It provides a challenging and well-defined framework for evaluating and comparing different models. It is also potentially useful as a means to detect cheating within the machine learning community, and to promote progress on AI safety.

* **Justification for Score:** The NPPC benchmark makes several innovative contributions to the design of a more robust benchmark for LLMs. The approach using NP-complete problems and providing comprehensive error analysis, goes beyond existing approaches. In particular, the uncrushable nature of NPPC is well justified. However, weaknesses such as dependence on prompt engineering, make its influence harder to predict.

### Distillation-Supervised Convolutional Low-Rank Adaptation for Efficient Image Super-Resolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11271v1)
- **Authors**: Xinning Chai, Yao Zhang, Yuxuan Zhang, Zhengxue Cheng, Yingsheng Qin, Yucai Yang, Li Song
- **Abstract**: Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) have been widely used in efficient image super-resolution. However, for CNN-based methods, performance gains often require deeper networks and larger feature maps, which increase complexity and inference costs. Inspired by LoRA's success in fine-tuning large language models, we explore its application to lightweight models and propose Distillation-Supervised Convolutional Low-Rank Adaptation (DSCLoRA), which improves model performance without increasing architectural complexity or inference costs. Specifically, we integrate ConvLoRA into the efficient SR network SPAN by replacing the SPAB module with the proposed SConvLB module and incorporating ConvLoRA layers into both the pixel shuffle block and its preceding convolutional layer. DSCLoRA leverages low-rank decomposition for parameter updates and employs a spatial feature affinity-based knowledge distillation strategy to transfer second-order statistical information from teacher models (pre-trained SPAN) to student models (ours). This method preserves the core knowledge of lightweight models and facilitates optimal solution discovery under certain conditions. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that DSCLoRA improves PSNR and SSIM over SPAN while maintaining its efficiency and competitive image quality. Notably, DSCLoRA ranked first in the Overall Performance Track of the NTIRE 2025 Efficient Super-Resolution Challenge. Our code and models are made publicly available at https://github.com/Yaozzz666/DSCF-SR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Distillation-Supervised Convolutional Low-Rank Adaptation (DSCLORA), a method for improving the performance of efficient image super-resolution (SR) models. DSCLORA integrates ConvLoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation for convolutional layers) into the SPAN (Swift Parameter-free Attention Network) architecture. It replaces the SPAB module of SPAN with a new SConvLB module and incorporates ConvLoRA layers into the pixel shuffle block.  A spatial feature affinity-based knowledge distillation strategy transfers knowledge from a pre-trained teacher model (SPAN) to the student model (DSCLORA). Experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that DSCLORA improves PSNR and SSIM compared to SPAN while maintaining efficiency. The paper highlights its first-place ranking in the NTIRE 2025 Efficient Super-Resolution Challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of applying LoRA to CNN-based super-resolution is interesting and potentially impactful for efficient SR. Using spatial affinity-based distillation is also a good approach.  The SConvLB module seems to be the key innovation, however, its specific design and benefits need to be clarified further. LoRA is already well-established, so the core novelty lies in *how* it's integrated specifically into the SPAN architecture with distillation.  The specific modifications to SPAN are incremental, not fundamentally revolutionary.

*   **Significance:**  Efficient SR is crucial for deploying SR models on resource-constrained devices.  DSCLORA appears to offer a good balance between performance and efficiency, making it a practically relevant contribution.  Ranking first in a NTIRE challenge provides strong evidence of its effectiveness. However, detailed comparisons showing performance-parameter trade-offs versus other efficient methods are critical for assessing the overall significance. Demonstrating generalizability to other lightweight SR architectures beyond SPAN would further enhance its impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Demonstrated performance gains without significantly increasing computational cost.
    *   Effective use of knowledge distillation to improve image quality.
    *   Strong empirical results on benchmark datasets and in a competitive challenge.
    *   Clear and concise writing.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The design of SConvLB module and how it leverages LoRA isn't explained clearly enough, as are the reasons for replacing the SPAB. There is no ablation studies for the specific components of SConvLB.
    *   The performance gains are incremental. While ranking first in the NTIRE challenge is good, it's important to quantify *how much* better the results are than other top contenders.
    *   The evaluation is limited to PSNR/SSIM. While these metrics are commonly used, it would be valuable to include perceptual metrics (e.g., LPIPS) or user studies to assess the subjective quality of the results.
    *   Limited exploration of LoRA configuration (different ranks, different layers). The reported ablation studies could have included more variation to demonstrate what leads to the best performing setting.

*   **Potential Influence:** DSCLORA could influence the development of more efficient SR models, especially for deployment on mobile devices or other resource-constrained platforms. It provides a viable way of fine-tuning existing SR models, potentially leading to better performance without large cost increases.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of efficient image super-resolution. DSCLORA delivers on its promise of improving SR performance without major increases to model complexity or computational burden. The NTIRE challenge success is a significant factor in its impact. However, the improvements are incremental, and the underlying mechanism needs better explanation. The ablations should have delved more deeply into critical design choices. Also, the significance and novelty should be more evident against the already well-established SPAN. Therefore, while the paper offers some practical benefits, its fundamental contributions are limited in novelty and scope.

### From Misleading Queries to Accurate Answers: A Three-Stage Fine-Tuning Method for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11277v1)
- **Authors**: Guocong Li, Weize Liu, Yihang Wu, Ping Wang, Shuaihan Huang, Hongxia Xu, Jian Wu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit excellent performance in natural language processing (NLP), but remain highly sensitive to the quality of input queries, especially when these queries contain misleading or inaccurate information. Existing methods focus on correcting the output, but they often overlook the potential of improving the ability of LLMs to detect and correct misleading content in the input itself. In this paper, we propose a novel three-stage fine-tuning method that enhances the ability of LLMs to detect and correct misleading information in the input, further improving response accuracy and reducing hallucinations. Specifically, the three stages include (1) training LLMs to identify misleading information, (2) training LLMs to correct the misleading information using built-in or external knowledge, and (3) training LLMs to generate accurate answers based on the corrected queries. To evaluate our method, we conducted experiments on three datasets for the hallucination detection task and the question answering (QA) task, as well as two datasets containing misleading information that we constructed. The experimental results demonstrate that our method significantly improves the accuracy and factuality of LLM responses, while also enhancing the ability to detect hallucinations and reducing the generation of hallucinations in the output, particularly when the query contains misleading information. We will publicly release our code upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "From Misleading Queries to Accurate Answers: A Three-Stage Fine-Tuning Method for LLMs":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the problem of large language models (LLMs) being sensitive to misleading or inaccurate information in input queries. The authors propose a three-stage fine-tuning method to enhance LLMs' ability to detect and correct misleading information *before* generating a response. The three stages are: (1) training LLMs to identify misleading information, (2) training LLMs to correct the misleading information using built-in or external knowledge, and (3) training LLMs to generate accurate answers based on the corrected queries. The method is evaluated on hallucination detection and question answering tasks using existing datasets and two newly constructed datasets containing misleading information. The results demonstrate that the proposed method significantly improves the accuracy and factuality of LLM responses, reduces hallucinations, and enhances the ability to detect misleading information in the input. The authors will release their code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its proactive approach to handling misleading information. Most existing methods focus on correcting the output *after* it has been generated.  By addressing the problem at the input level, the paper offers a potentially more efficient and effective solution. The three-stage fine-tuning approach is well-defined and intuitively sound.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it tackles a major limitation of LLMs: their vulnerability to "garbage in, garbage out." In many real-world applications, especially in domains like medicine or law, the accuracy of responses is critical, and users may inadvertently provide misleading information. Improving the robustness of LLMs to such scenarios is essential for their reliable deployment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Method:** The three-stage fine-tuning approach is clearly described and logically structured.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are conducted on a variety of datasets, including both existing benchmarks and newly created datasets designed to specifically test robustness to misleading information.
    *   **Empirical Results:** The results consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method across different LLMs and datasets. The ablation study further reinforces the importance of each stage in the pipeline.
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and the motivation for the proposed solution.
    *   **Case Studies:** The case studies provide concrete examples of how the method works in practice, enhancing the reader's understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Knowledge:** The method's performance depends on the availability of reliable knowledge for detecting and correcting misleading information. If the LLM's internal knowledge or the external knowledge base is incomplete or inaccurate, the method may fail. This is a key limitation, especially in niche or rapidly evolving domains. Future work should focus on addressing this weakness explicitly.
    *   **Dataset Construction:** While the creation of the HaluEval-QAmis and CQAmis datasets is a strength, the description of the misleading information's type and complexity in relation to real-world misinformation is minimal. There is a need to examine how these artificial misinformation types compare and contrast with real-world misinformation.
    *   **Scalability:** While the paper shows some scalability across different model sizes, it does not address resource constraints. It might also be useful to perform this study to see whether the framework can be distilled into a simpler model.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It highlights the importance of input-level correction for robust LLM performance.
    *   It provides a practical framework for enhancing LLMs' ability to handle misleading information.
    *   It establishes a benchmark for evaluating LLMs' robustness to misleading information, through the creation of new datasets.

*   **Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a solid and well-executed approach, the reliance on high-quality knowledge and the somewhat limited analysis of scalability are drawbacks. The creation of new datasets strengthens the paper's contribution, but further examination of these datasets would enhance their importance. While proactively fixing the errors in the prompt has significance, this paper does not do much to move the space further beyond standard LLM development.

### Automated Python Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11290v2)
- **Authors**: Joshua Otten, Antonios Anastasopoulos, Kevin Moran
- **Abstract**: Python is one of the most commonly used programming languages in industry and education. Its English keywords and built-in functions/modules allow it to come close to pseudo-code in terms of its readability and ease of writing. However, those who do not speak English may not experience these advantages. In fact, they may even be hindered in their ability to understand Python code, as the English nature of its terms creates an additional layer of overhead. To that end, we introduce the task of automatically translating Python's natural modality (keywords, error types, identifiers, etc.) into other human languages. This presents a unique challenge, considering the abbreviated nature of these forms, as well as potential untranslatability of advanced mathematical/programming concepts across languages. We therefore create an automated pipeline to translate Python into other human languages, comparing strategies using machine translation and large language models. We then use this pipeline to acquire translations from five common Python libraries (pytorch, pandas, tensorflow, numpy, and random) in seven languages, and do a quality test on a subset of these terms in French, Greek, and Bengali. We hope this will provide a clearer path forward towards creating a universal Python, accessible to anyone regardless of nationality or language background.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Automated Python Translation," along with a justified novelty/significance score:

**Summary**

The paper introduces the task of automatically translating Python's "natural modality" (keywords, error types, identifiers, etc.) into other human languages. The authors argue that while Python's English-based vocabulary aids readability for English speakers, it can hinder non-English speakers.  They propose a pipeline involving Python term expansion (unabbreviating), translation, and (optionally) abbreviation. They compare different strategies for term expansion using GPT-4 Turbo and for translation using Google Translate, ChatGPT, and Llama2. They evaluate these strategies on Python's standard library and then extend their pipeline to translate terms from five additional Python libraries (pytorch, pandas, tensorflow, numpy, and random) into seven languages, performing a quality assessment on a subset of translations into French, Greek, and Bengali.  Finally, they explore fine-tuning a large language model (LLM) to translate entire code blocks.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of translating programming language *keywords* is novel.  Most prior work on multilingual coding focuses on comments, variable names, and documentation.  Translating core language elements presents unique challenges related to abbreviation, context, and grammatical function (as the authors point out).

*   **Significance:**  The potential impact is significant.  A truly universal Python could lower the barrier to entry for programming, making it accessible to a broader global audience.  This could lead to increased diversity in the field and wider adoption of Python. The UNIPY project is a great step.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly defines the problem and its challenges.
    *   The pipeline approach is well-structured and modular.
    *   The use of multiple evaluation metrics (exact match accuracy and chrF) provides a comprehensive assessment.
    *   The experiments are reasonably thorough, comparing different models and prompting strategies.
    *   The downstream evaluation provides valuable insights into the pipeline's performance in a more realistic setting.
    *   The exploration of fine-tuning an LLM for code block translation, while not yielding perfect results, is a worthwhile direction.
    *   The inclusion of analysis on the ambiguities created by english language that are hard to represent in other languages.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The quality of the evaluation relies heavily on automated metrics and single annotator evaluations, especially for the downstream pipeline test.  While the authors acknowledge this, more thorough human evaluations (with multiple annotators and inter-annotator agreement scores) would strengthen the findings.
    *   The analysis of error cases could be more detailed. The few examples in the paper are helpful, but a more systematic analysis of common error patterns would be valuable.
    *   The abbreviation scheme is rudimentary and doesn't account for the nuances of different languages. As the authors acknowledge, it may not even be applicable to languages like Mandarin.
    *   The performance of LLMs is significantly lower for low-resource languages, highlighting a persistent problem in machine translation.  While this is a known issue, it limits the practical applicability of the proposed approach for many languages.
    *   The paper emphasizes the limitations of CodeInternational compared to UNIPY, but doesn't do a direct comparison of these approaches.

*   **Impact on the field:** The paper introduces a new research area, paving the way for future work on multilingual programming languages. The automated pipeline for translating Python terms offers a practical tool for expanding the UNIPY project and other similar initiatives. The comparison of different translation models provides useful insights for future research.

*   **Why not a higher score?** While the task is novel and significant, the current implementation has limitations. The reliance on Google Translate (while yielding the best results in some experiments) makes the pipeline susceptible to the biases and inconsistencies of that system. The performance gap between high- and low-resource languages is a major obstacle. Furthermore, the evaluations are not as rigorous as they could be. This is still a relatively early stage in exploring this problem, and substantial further research is needed.

**Score: 6**

*Reasoning:* The paper presents a novel and potentially significant research direction. The approach is well-defined, and the experiments provide valuable insights. However, the limitations related to evaluation quality, handling of low-resource languages, and the somewhat rudimentary abbreviation scheme prevent a higher score. The contribution is a solid step forward but requires further refinement to have a truly transformative impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Autoregressive Distillation of Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11295v1)
- **Authors**: Yeongmin Kim, Sotiris Anagnostidis, Yuming Du, Edgar Schönfeld, Jonas Kohler, Markos Georgopoulos, Albert Pumarola, Ali Thabet, Artsiom Sanakoyeu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models with transformer architectures have demonstrated promising capabilities in generating high-fidelity images and scalability for high resolution. However, iterative sampling process required for synthesis is very resource-intensive. A line of work has focused on distilling solutions to probability flow ODEs into few-step student models. Nevertheless, existing methods have been limited by their reliance on the most recent denoised samples as input, rendering them susceptible to exposure bias. To address this limitation, we propose AutoRegressive Distillation (ARD), a novel approach that leverages the historical trajectory of the ODE to predict future steps. ARD offers two key benefits: 1) it mitigates exposure bias by utilizing a predicted historical trajectory that is less susceptible to accumulated errors, and 2) it leverages the previous history of the ODE trajectory as a more effective source of coarse-grained information. ARD modifies the teacher transformer architecture by adding token-wise time embedding to mark each input from the trajectory history and employs a block-wise causal attention mask for training. Furthermore, incorporating historical inputs only in lower transformer layers enhances performance and efficiency. We validate the effectiveness of ARD in a class-conditioned generation on ImageNet and T2I synthesis. Our model achieves a $5\times$ reduction in FID degradation compared to the baseline methods while requiring only 1.1\% extra FLOPs on ImageNet-256. Moreover, ARD reaches FID of 1.84 on ImageNet-256 in merely 4 steps and outperforms the publicly available 1024p text-to-image distilled models in prompt adherence score with a minimal drop in FID compared to the teacher. Project page: https://github.com/alsdudrla10/ARD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Autoregressive Distillation of Diffusion Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel distillation technique called AutoRegressive Distillation (ARD) for diffusion transformer models. ARD addresses the exposure bias issue present in existing few-step distillation methods by leveraging the historical trajectory of the probability flow ODE during sampling.  Instead of relying solely on the most recently denoised sample as input, ARD uses a predicted history of denoised samples. This provides a more informative input for predicting future steps. The method modifies the teacher transformer architecture by adding token-wise time embeddings and employing a block-wise causal attention mask to capture dependencies between samples at different time steps.  The paper validates ARD on class-conditional ImageNet generation and text-to-image synthesis, demonstrating a reduction in FID degradation compared to baseline distillation methods with minimal computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *autoregressive* approach to distillation.  Prior works have focused on direct distillation, which makes student models heavily reliant on imperfect "current" samples. The idea of incorporating the historical trajectory is a valid and potentially significant improvement.  The transformer architecture modifications (token-wise time embeddings and block-wise causal attention) seem like a natural adaptation to leverage the historical information.  The hierarchical usage of historical data in lower layers for capturing coarse-grained information is also a good inductive bias.
*   **Significance:** Reducing the computational cost of diffusion models is a highly relevant and impactful problem. Distillation is a well-explored avenue, but the exposure bias remains a significant challenge. ARD offers a plausible solution that demonstrably improves performance relative to existing distillation techniques, as demonstrated by the FID score improvements. The ability to distill down to 3-4 steps with minimal performance degradation is significant for practical deployment. The results on ImageNet and text-to-image synthesis provides solid evidence for the generalizability of the proposed approach. Additionally, the claim that it outperforms existing T2I distilled models further underscores its usefulness.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a clear and important problem (exposure bias in distillation).
    *   The autoregressive approach is well-motivated and theoretically sound.
    *   Empirical results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing methods in terms of FID score and prompt adherence.
    *   Minimal computational overhead compared to baselines.
    *   Comprehensive experimentation including ablation studies to validate architectural choices.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the results are impressive, the method builds on existing techniques (diffusion transformers, distillation). The individual components (token-wise embeddings, attention masking) are not ground-breaking in themselves; novelty lies in *how* they are combined to leverage the trajectory.
    *   The implementation relies on pre-computed ODE trajectories which can limit the flexibility of the method with new tasks and data sets (although online calculation might also be feasible).
    *   The ablation studies, while comprehensive, could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* certain configurations are optimal (e.g., why N=6 is the sweet spot). A deeper dive into analyzing the learned attention maps or representation might provide more insight.
    *   The paper could discuss the possible limitations of the method better. For instance, the effect of teacher model's error accumulation on the trajectory.
    *   While the results are good, some details on hyperparameter choices/robustness would make the experiments stronger.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the code and pre-trained models are released, ARD could become a standard technique for distilling diffusion transformers, especially in resource-constrained environments. It has the potential to become a building block in future generative model architectures.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper offers a sound solution for a current bottleneck in diffusion models with solid empirical support. The concept is clever in the way the method leverages the history and introduces the autoregressive aspect for distillation. Although the novelty is incremental (as the individual components are not very novel on their own), the integrated system demonstrably improves on what already existed. Its practical significance and potential to lower computation cost for diffusion models justify a high score. However, the incremental novelty of the component techniques and the reliance on pre-computed trajectories prevent it from being in the very top tier.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Big Brother is Watching: Proactive Deepfake Detection via Learnable Hidden Face
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11309v1)
- **Authors**: Hongbo Li, Shangchao Yang, Ruiyang Xia, Lin Yuan, Xinbo Gao
- **Abstract**: As deepfake technologies continue to advance, passive detection methods struggle to generalize with various forgery manipulations and datasets. Proactive defense techniques have been actively studied with the primary aim of preventing deepfake operation effectively working. In this paper, we aim to bridge the gap between passive detection and proactive defense, and seek to solve the detection problem utilizing a proactive methodology. Inspired by several watermarking-based forensic methods, we explore a novel detection framework based on the concept of ``hiding a learnable face within a face''. Specifically, relying on a semi-fragile invertible steganography network, a secret template image is embedded into a host image imperceptibly, acting as an indicator monitoring for any malicious image forgery when being restored by the inverse steganography process. Instead of being manually specified, the secret template is optimized during training to resemble a neutral facial appearance, just like a ``big brother'' hidden in the image to be protected. By incorporating a self-blending mechanism and robustness learning strategy with a simulative transmission channel, a robust detector is built to accurately distinguish if the steganographic image is maliciously tampered or benignly processed. Finally, extensive experiments conducted on multiple datasets demonstrate the superiority of the proposed approach over competing passive and proactive detection methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel proactive deepfake detection method called "Learnable Hidden Face".  It embeds a learnable template image, resembling a neutral face, into a host face image using a semi-fragile invertible steganography network.  The embedded template acts as an "inspector," and any tampering or forgery attempts on the host face will be reflected in the restored template. A deepfake detector then analyzes the difference between the original and restored hidden face to determine authenticity.  The template is optimized during training and is designed to be robust to common image processing operations but sensitive to malicious manipulations like deepfakes. A specialized transmission channel is simulated during training for robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of "hiding a learnable face within a face" is relatively novel. While watermarking and steganography have been used in deepfake detection before, the concept of optimizing a hidden *face* as a template, rather than a random pattern or explicit watermark, is interesting. The use of a semi-fragile invertible network to reflect manipulations on the host image into the restored template is also a unique aspect.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from attempting to bridge the gap between passive and proactive deepfake detection. Proactive methods are appealing because they directly address the problem at the source, but often struggle with imperceptibility, robustness and deployment. If this method can effectively prevent deepfakes *and* be robust to normal usage, it would be a substantial contribution.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty:** The concept of a "learnable hidden face" provides a fresh perspective on proactive deepfake detection.
    * **Performance:** The experimental results seem strong, outperforming several existing passive and proactive methods across various datasets and deepfake techniques.
    * **Robustness:** The paper demonstrates robustness to common image processing techniques.
    * **Complete Approach:** The paper proposes a well-rounded framework, including a training strategy with a simulative transmission channel and multi-task loss functions.
    * **Visual Interpretation:** The visualization of the masked steganographic images and recovered faces provides an intuitive understanding of the approach.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Practical Deployment Concerns:** The paper doesn't address the practical challenges of deploying such a system.  For example, how would you ensure that all images online are protected by this embedding scheme? What would be the incentive for users to adopt this proactively? The paper lacks a discussion of this crucial aspect.
    * **Scalability:** How does this approach scale to large datasets and high-resolution images/videos? This needs to be addressed.
    * **Sensitivity to Unforeseen Attacks:**  While the approach is robust against specific attacks evaluated, there's a risk of new, more sophisticated deepfake techniques that can circumvent the detection mechanism.
    * **Overfitting the Learnable Template:**  The learnable template is derived from training data. Is it possible that this approach is somewhat overfitting to datasets like FFHQ and generalizes poorly to images that deviate significantly from the characteristics of FFHQ-trained faces (e.g., different ethnicities, ages, or image styles)?  Although there are experiments on different datasets, a more rigorous exploration of template overfitting on different image types is important.
    * **Semi-Fragility vs. Security:** The choice of a semi-fragile watermark implies that any tampering will affect the restored image. It needs to be explored further whether this approach might be tricked with adversarial attacks (attacks designed to bypass detection methods while maintaining visual fidelity).

* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to stimulate further research in proactive deepfake detection, particularly in the development of learned watermarking and template optimization techniques. The idea of using a learned template that represents facial features rather than arbitrary noise or pre-defined images may have wider implications for secure media transmission.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach that achieves strong results in deepfake detection. The concept of "hiding a learnable face within a face" is a creative idea, and the experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness and robustness. However, the paper does have some weaknesses, particularly in addressing practical deployment concerns, potential overfitting of the learnable template, and vulnerabilities to future unforeseen attacks. Therefore, while representing a valuable contribution, the approach is not quite ready for direct deployment in real-world scenarios. Taking everything into consideration, a score of **7** is justified.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Optimizing LLM Inference: Fluid-Guided Online Scheduling with Memory Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11320v1)
- **Authors**: Ruicheng Ao, Gan Luo, David Simchi-Levi, Xinshang Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are indispensable in today's applications, but their inference procedure -- generating responses by processing text in segments and using a memory-heavy Key-Value (KV) cache -- demands significant computational resources, particularly under memory constraints. This paper formulates LLM inference optimization as a multi-stage online scheduling problem where sequential prompt arrivals and KV cache growth render conventional scheduling ineffective. We develop a fluid dynamics approximation to provide a tractable benchmark that guides algorithm design. Building on this, we propose the Waiting for Accumulated Inference Threshold (WAIT) algorithm, which uses multiple thresholds to schedule incoming prompts optimally when output lengths are known, and extend it to Nested WAIT for cases with unknown output lengths. Theoretical analysis shows that both algorithms achieve near-optimal performance against the fluid benchmark in heavy traffic conditions, balancing throughput, latency, and Time to First Token (TTFT). Experiments with the Llama-7B model on an A100 GPU using both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate improved throughput and reduced latency relative to established baselines like vLLM and Sarathi. This work bridges operations research and machine learning, offering a rigorous framework for the efficient deployment of LLMs under memory constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper "Optimizing LLM Inference: Fluid-Guided Online Scheduling with Memory Constraints":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of optimizing Large Language Model (LLM) inference, particularly when memory resources are limited. It models LLM inference as a multi-stage online scheduling problem, where the Key-Value (KV) cache dynamically expands during the process, rendering traditional scheduling algorithms ineffective.  The authors develop a fluid dynamics approximation to create a tractable benchmark.  Building on this, they propose two algorithms: WAIT (Waiting for Accumulated Inference Threshold) for cases where output lengths are known, and Nested WAIT for unknown output lengths.  Theoretical analysis demonstrates near-optimal performance under heavy traffic conditions, balancing throughput, latency, and Time to First Token (TTFT).  Experimental results using the Llama-7B model on an A100 GPU, with both synthetic and real-world datasets, show improved throughput and reduced latency compared to VLLM and Sarathi.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a novel contribution by applying queueing theory and fluid dynamics approximations to the LLM inference scheduling problem, offering a theoretically grounded approach. While system-level optimizations for LLM inference are abundant, they often lack mathematical rigor. The development of the WAIT and Nested WAIT algorithms, guided by the fluid dynamics benchmark, and the provision of heavy-traffic optimality guarantees, offer a unique perspective. The Nested WAIT algorithm's adaptation to unknown output lengths is a valuable extension.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because efficient LLM inference is a crucial factor in making LLMs accessible and deployable in real-world applications. The demonstrated improvements in throughput and latency have practical implications for reducing costs and improving user experience. Furthermore, bridging operations research and machine learning in this context offers a valuable framework for future research and development in LLM deployment. The theoretical analysis helps to understand the limits of non-predictive policies and guides the design of more efficient scheduling strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper is well-grounded in queueing theory and fluid dynamics, providing a solid mathematical framework for analysis and algorithm design.
    *   **Practical Algorithms:** The WAIT and Nested WAIT algorithms are practical and implementable, as demonstrated by the experimental results.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments use both synthetic and real-world datasets and compare the proposed algorithms against established baselines.
    *   **Clear Performance Guarantees:** The paper provides asymptotic optimality guarantees for the algorithms under heavy traffic conditions.
    *   Adaptation to time-varying arrival rates and extending the segment design is a clear strength to improving practical applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Fluid Approximation:** The fluid dynamics approximation, while useful for analysis, simplifies the stochastic nature of the problem. The actual performance may deviate from the theoretical benchmark.
    *   **Memory usage M^*:** The assumption of equilibrium with static batching means that the practical dynamic utilization of the models may be underutilized or overutilized. The algorithms adapt but the theory is not adaptable for this setting.
    *   **Simplified GPU Model:** The GPU model used in the paper is simplified and assumes a linear relationship between iteration time and memory consumption. This may not fully capture the complexities of real-world GPU architectures.
    *   **Tuning required:** The algorithms rely on properly tuning certain thresholds and coefficients which are not explicitly defined and must be found empirically.
    *   **Comparison scope:** The results would be improved by comparison to other more sophisticated LLM optimization methods or models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a theoretical foundation for LLM inference scheduling and inspiring the development of new, more efficient algorithms. The framework can be extended to incorporate more realistic GPU models, address more complex scheduling constraints, and analyze the performance of other scheduling algorithms.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper presents a significant contribution by applying queueing theory to LLM inference scheduling. The proposed algorithms offer improved performance compared to widely used baselines, and the theoretical analysis provides valuable insights. However, the reliance on fluid dynamics approximations, simplified GPU model, and lack of a practical method to handle threshold tuning, and lack of comparison to a greater breadth of models, limit the overall impact of the work. While valuable, more research is needed to validate its applicability across diverse real-world deployment settings to be rated higher.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Minimalist Approach to LLM Reasoning: from Rejection Sampling to Reinforce
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11343v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Xiong, Jiarui Yao, Yuhui Xu, Bo Pang, Lei Wang, Doyen Sahoo, Junnan Li, Nan Jiang, Tong Zhang, Caiming Xiong, Hanze Dong
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become a prevailing approach for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks. Among recent methods, GRPO stands out for its empirical success in training models such as DeepSeek-R1, yet the sources of its effectiveness remain poorly understood. In this work, we revisit GRPO from a reinforce-like algorithm perspective and analyze its core components. Surprisingly, we find that a simple rejection sampling baseline, RAFT, which trains only on positively rewarded samples, yields competitive performance than GRPO and PPO. Our ablation studies reveal that GRPO's main advantage arises from discarding prompts with entirely incorrect responses, rather than from its reward normalization. Motivated by this insight, we propose Reinforce-Rej, a minimal extension of policy gradient that filters both entirely incorrect and entirely correct samples. Reinforce-Rej improves KL efficiency and stability, serving as a lightweight yet effective alternative to more complex RL algorithms. We advocate RAFT as a robust and interpretable baseline, and suggest that future advances should focus on more principled designs for incorporating negative samples, rather than relying on them indiscriminately. Our findings provide guidance for future work in reward-based LLM post-training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on mathematical reasoning tasks. The authors revisit several RL approaches, including RAFT (rejection sampling fine-tuning), Vanilla Reinforce, and GRPO (a Reinforce variant).  Surprisingly, they find that RAFT, a very simple baseline using only positively rewarded samples, achieves performance competitive with GRPO, a more complex RL method. Ablation studies reveal that GRPO's primary advantage stems from implicitly filtering out prompts with entirely incorrect responses, rather than reward normalization techniques.  Based on this insight, the authors propose Reinforce-Rej, a minimal extension of policy gradient that filters both entirely incorrect and entirely correct samples. Reinforce-Rej demonstrates improved KL efficiency and stability. The authors advocate for RAFT as a robust baseline and suggest focusing future research on principled designs for incorporating negative samples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Insightful Analysis of GRPO:** The paper's key strength lies in its in-depth analysis of GRPO.  Deconstructing GRPO and identifying the core contributor to its performance (filtering incorrect prompts) is a significant finding. The ablation studies are well-designed and provide strong evidence for their claims. The paper addresses the practical consideration of post-training LLMs, a burgeoning area of interest.
    *   **RAFT as a Strong Baseline:**  Demonstrating the effectiveness of RAFT as a competitive baseline is valuable. This simplification allows for easier implementation and interpretability, which is beneficial for the research community. The paper advocates for RAFT's simplicity and effectiveness, offering it as a robust alternative to more complex RL techniques.
    *   **Reinforce-Rej Proposal:** The proposal of Reinforce-Rej is a logical consequence of their findings. The improved KL efficiency is promising.
    *   **Emphasis on Sample Selection:** The paper rightly emphasizes the importance of sample selection over complex algorithmic design in reward-based LLM post-training. This shift in focus is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Reproducibility**: The code availability is an important factor in fostering future research into reward-based LLM fine-tuning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Task Domain:** The analysis is heavily focused on mathematical reasoning. While this is a relevant domain, the generalizability of the findings to other LLM tasks (e.g., creative writing, code generation) is not explicitly addressed.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While Reinforce-Rej shows improvement, its performance is comparable to GRPO. While this can be a matter of implementation, it is still a valid consideration of the relative impact of Reinforce-Rej.
    *   **Why correct samples are harmful is not fully explained:** While the paper provides evidence of the importance of removing correct and wrong samples from the dataset, the paper fails to fully explain why training on perfectly-correct samples might be harmful for performance.
    *   **Lack of ablation with different N responses**. In the experimental design, the number of sampled responses (N) is constant, but there is no discussion of how sensitive the method is to choosing optimal N.
    *   **Average@16 Evaluation**: While average@16 is more thorough, it may still be worth considering more basic metrics like accuracy@1 and accuracy@4.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The deconstruction of GRPO's effectiveness is novel and significant. Identifying prompt filtering as a critical component challenges the assumption that reward normalization is essential.
    *   The focus on simpler algorithms and sample selection offers a practical direction for LLM post-training research.
    *   Reinforce-Rej, while an incremental improvement, demonstrates the potential of carefully designed sample filtering strategies.
    *   The finding that negative feedback in training the LLaMA models had little effect goes against intuition.

**Overall:**

The paper provides valuable insights into the components that contribute to the performance of reward-based LLM training methods.  The shift in focus towards sample selection is a potentially significant contribution. The experimental analysis is thorough and well-justified. However, limitations in task domain and the incremental nature of the Reinforce-Rej improvement slightly temper the overall impact. Considering all of these factors, I'm giving the paper a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Seedream 3.0 Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11346v2)
- **Authors**: Yu Gao, Lixue Gong, Qiushan Guo, Xiaoxia Hou, Zhichao Lai, Fanshi Li, Liang Li, Xiaochen Lian, Chao Liao, Liyang Liu, Wei Liu, Yichun Shi, Shiqi Sun, Yu Tian, Zhi Tian, Peng Wang, Rui Wang, Xuanda Wang, Xun Wang, Ye Wang, Guofeng Wu, Jie Wu, Xin Xia, Xuefeng Xiao, Zhonghua Zhai, Xinyu Zhang, Qi Zhang, Yuwei Zhang, Shijia Zhao, Jianchao Yang, Weilin Huang
- **Abstract**: We present Seedream 3.0, a high-performance Chinese-English bilingual image generation foundation model. We develop several technical improvements to address existing challenges in Seedream 2.0, including alignment with complicated prompts, fine-grained typography generation, suboptimal visual aesthetics and fidelity, and limited image resolutions. Specifically, the advancements of Seedream 3.0 stem from improvements across the entire pipeline, from data construction to model deployment. At the data stratum, we double the dataset using a defect-aware training paradigm and a dual-axis collaborative data-sampling framework. Furthermore, we adopt several effective techniques such as mixed-resolution training, cross-modality RoPE, representation alignment loss, and resolution-aware timestep sampling in the pre-training phase. During the post-training stage, we utilize diversified aesthetic captions in SFT, and a VLM-based reward model with scaling, thereby achieving outputs that well align with human preferences. Furthermore, Seedream 3.0 pioneers a novel acceleration paradigm. By employing consistent noise expectation and importance-aware timestep sampling, we achieve a 4 to 8 times speedup while maintaining image quality. Seedream 3.0 demonstrates significant improvements over Seedream 2.0: it enhances overall capabilities, in particular for text-rendering in complicated Chinese characters which is important to professional typography generation. In addition, it provides native high-resolution output (up to 2K), allowing it to generate images with high visual quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the Seedream 3.0 Technical Report:

**Summary:**

The paper presents Seedream 3.0, a Chinese-English bilingual image generation foundation model. It details technical improvements over Seedream 2.0 to address limitations in prompt alignment, typography, visual aesthetics, fidelity, and resolution. The core improvements include: (1) doubling the training dataset with defect-aware training and dual-axis data sampling; (2) efficient pre-training techniques like mixed-resolution training, cross-modality RoPE, and representation alignment loss; (3) using diversified aesthetic captions and a VLM-based reward model in post-training; and (4) a novel acceleration paradigm leveraging consistent noise expectation and importance-aware timestep sampling for faster inference. Seedream 3.0 demonstrates improvements in text rendering, aesthetic quality, and supports native high-resolution output (up to 2K). Benchmarking shows Seedream 3.0 achieving state-of-the-art results in both objective and subjective evaluations, outperforming other popular models such as GPT-4o, Imagen 3, and Midjourney v6.1.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Improvement:** The paper addresses several limitations of previous models in a holistic manner, covering data, pre-training, post-training, and inference acceleration.
    *   **Technical Novelty:** The proposed techniques like defect-aware training, dual-axis data sampling, cross-modality RoPE, and the acceleration paradigm show genuine novelty.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents thorough experimental evaluations, using both automatic metrics and human evaluations. Seedream 3.0 consistently achieves superior performance compared to strong baselines.
    *   **Practical Impact:** The integration into commercial platforms like Doubao and Jimeng and the focus on inference efficiency highlight the practical value of the work.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to understand the technical details and experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper details several improvements, it lacks comprehensive ablation studies to isolate the impact of each individual technique.  It's hard to tell which specific component contributes the most to the overall performance gain.
    *   **Dependency on External Data:** The defect-aware training relies on a manually annotated dataset. The creation of this dataset adds to the overall complexity and requires a substantial annotation effort.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** Some techniques, such as the resolution-aware timestep sampling or the particular design of the reward model scaling, would benefit from a more solid theoretical justification and connection to existing literature.
    *   **Black-box Nature:** The use of proprietary datasets, evaluation metrics, and models limits the ability to reproduce and independently verify some of the claims. The paper is necessarily somewhat opaque about the exact nature of the data and internal implementations.

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The combination of data filtering and augmentation with defect-aware training is novel.
    *   Cross-modality RoPE for improved visual-text alignment is a useful modification of prior techniques.
    *   The instance-aware flow acceleration (HyperSD/RayFlow) with the consistent noise estimation may have more specific implementation details.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The work contributes to the progress of text-to-image generation, particularly in the context of bilingual models and Chinese typography.
    *   It addresses practical limitations of previous models, such as resolution constraints and inference speed, making it more suitable for real-world applications.
    *   The focus on controllable generation and high aesthetic quality is also valuable for downstream tasks.

**Justification:**

Seedream 3.0 makes several significant technical contributions to text-to-image generation.  The comprehensive approach, combining innovations in data handling, model architecture, training strategies, and inference acceleration, sets it apart from incremental improvements focusing on a single aspect.  The strong performance on benchmark datasets and integration into real-world platforms indicates a tangible impact.  However, the lack of detailed ablation studies and some theoretical depth, coupled with the reliance on proprietary resources, slightly diminishes the overall impact.
