### Teaching Large Language Models to Reason through Learning and Forgetting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11364v1)
- **Authors**: Tianwei Ni, Allen Nie, Sapana Chaudhary, Yao Liu, Huzefa Rangwala, Rasool Fakoor
- **Abstract**: Leveraging inference-time search in large language models has proven effective in further enhancing a trained model's capability to solve complex mathematical and reasoning problems. However, this approach significantly increases computational costs and inference time, as the model must generate and evaluate multiple candidate solutions to identify a viable reasoning path. To address this, we propose an effective approach that integrates search capabilities directly into the model by fine-tuning it using both successful (learning) and failed reasoning paths (forgetting) derived from diverse search methods. While fine-tuning the model with these data might seem straightforward, we identify a critical issue: the model's search capability tends to degrade rapidly if fine-tuning is performed naively. We show that this degradation can be substantially mitigated by employing a smaller learning rate. Extensive experiments on the challenging Game-of-24 and Countdown mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that our approach not only outperforms both standard fine-tuning and inference-time search baselines but also significantly reduces inference time by 180$\times$.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the high computational cost associated with inference-time search methods in large language models (LLMs) for solving complex reasoning problems. It proposes a method called "Unlikelihood Fine-Tuning (UFT)" that integrates search capabilities directly into the model during fine-tuning. UFT utilizes both successful and failed reasoning paths derived from diverse search methods. The paper identifies and tackles the problem of rapid degradation in the model's search capability during naive fine-tuning by employing a smaller learning rate. The approach is evaluated on the Game-of-24 and Countdown mathematical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating improved performance compared to standard fine-tuning and inference-time search baselines, along with a significant reduction in inference time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution is the UFT method which combines supervised fine-tuning on correct reasoning paths with unlikelihood training on failed paths *and* emphasizes the importance of a small learning rate to prevent the forgetting of search capabilities. While individual components like unlikelihood training are not entirely new, the combination of these aspects and the specific application to CoT-style fine-tuning to maintain search ability are novel. Prior work primarily focused on distilling knowledge from successful paths, or creating preference pairs. The idea to use a smaller learning rate to prevent search degradation is simple but important and not well-documented elsewhere.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically important problem: reducing the inference cost of LLM reasoning. If UFT is readily reproducible and applicable to other reasoning tasks or even other model types, it can significantly impact the deployment of LLMs in resource-constrained environments or where low latency is crucial. The experimental results demonstrating improved performance and reduced inference time are compelling. The finding that data quality (using classical search data) matters more than simply using CoT is also a significant point.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of high inference costs associated with search-based reasoning methods in LLMs.
    * **Well-Defined Approach:** The UFT method is clearly explained, with a focus on the rationale behind using both successful and failed reasoning paths and the smaller learning rate.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents convincing experimental results on two challenging benchmarks, demonstrating improved performance and reduced inference time.
    * **Important Insights:** The finding about training data quality driving performance is a valuable contribution.  Also, the focus on maintaining search capability is important.
    * **Reproducibility:** The paper describes the implementation details, including hyperparameter settings, which promotes reproducibility.  The authors built upon existing libraries, making it easier for other researchers.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generalization:** The evaluation is primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks.  While these are challenging, it's unclear how well UFT would generalize to other types of reasoning or knowledge-intensive tasks.  The authors note limitations for the 1.5B models. It is limited to mathematical specific LLMs.
    * **Limited Ablation Studies:**  More ablation experiments could have isolated the impact of individual components of UFT more clearly. It is still unclear precisely the benefits of the *diversity* of data (CoT vs classical search).
    * **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a theoretical analysis of why UFT works. While the empirical results are strong, a theoretical understanding would strengthen the contribution.  There are some brief allusions to a planning-theoretic perspective, but this remains quite high-level.
    * **The effectiveness of the technique is shown on a Qwen2.5 model.** It's unknown how effective it will be on recent models like GPT-4.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective method for reducing the inference cost of LLM reasoning. The insights about training data quality and the importance of a small learning rate could also guide future research in this area.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to improving the efficiency of LLM reasoning. The empirical results are strong, and the insights about training data quality and learning rate are valuable. However, the limited generalization, lack of ablation studies, and absence of theoretical analysis detract from the overall contribution. Therefore, the paper merits a score that reflects its strong empirical results and practical relevance, while also acknowledging its limitations in terms of scope and theoretical understanding.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### OpenTuringBench: An Open-Model-based Benchmark and Framework for Machine-Generated Text Detection and Attribution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11369v1)
- **Authors**: Lucio La Cava, Andrea Tagarelli
- **Abstract**: Open Large Language Models (OLLMs) are increasingly leveraged in generative AI applications, posing new challenges for detecting their outputs. We propose OpenTuringBench, a new benchmark based on OLLMs, designed to train and evaluate machine-generated text detectors on the Turing Test and Authorship Attribution problems. OpenTuringBench focuses on a representative set of OLLMs, and features a number of challenging evaluation tasks, including human/machine-manipulated texts, out-of-domain texts, and texts from previously unseen models. We also provide OTBDetector, a contrastive learning framework to detect and attribute OLLM-based machine-generated texts. Results highlight the relevance and varying degrees of difficulty of the OpenTuringBench tasks, with our detector achieving remarkable capabilities across the various tasks and outperforming most existing detectors. Resources are available on the OpenTuringBench Hugging Face repository at https://huggingface.co/datasets/MLNTeam-Unical/OpenTuringBench
- **Summary**: This paper introduces OpenTuringBench, a new benchmark dataset and evaluation framework for detecting and attributing machine-generated text (MGT) produced by open large language models (OLLMs). The benchmark includes a diverse set of OLLMs, challenging evaluation tasks involving human/machine-manipulated text, out-of-domain texts, and previously unseen models. The paper also presents OTBDetector, a contrastive learning framework specifically designed for this benchmark to detect and attribute MGT. Experimental results demonstrate the relevance and varying difficulty of the proposed tasks, and highlight the competitive performance of OTBDetector compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The primary novelty lies in the comprehensive focus on OLLMs, which, while increasingly prevalent, haven't been thoroughly explored in the MGT detection literature, particularly with recently released, diverse models. The introduction of challenging tasks like mixed human-machine text and out-of-domain evaluation adds to the dataset's unique aspects. The release of the OTBDetector also contribute to the novelty of this submission.
*   **Weakness:** While the combination of OLLMs, varied tasks, and detector framework is novel, individual components like contrastive learning for MGT detection are not entirely new. The core concepts of the OTBDetector, while adapted for longer sequences, is inspired and build upon existing methods.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** The work addresses a timely and significant problem. The proliferation of MGT from OLLMs poses considerable risks to information integrity, and reliable detection methods are crucial. The benchmark's focus on OLLMs, emphasizing ethical considerations and practical accessibility, is a strength. The benchmark dataset is substantially larger than existing solutions.
*   **Weakness:** While the benchmark covers a wider range of OLLMs compared to previous works, the rate at which new models emerge is rapid. Therefore, the benchmark dataset might become outdated, needing periodic updates. It is also unclear if the evaluation tasks designed are truly capturing the complexity of new-generation MGT. Despite competitive results against existing benchmarks, the absolute performance in certain challenging tasks (especially Human-Machine Mixing, and Out-of-Domain tests) is not high, thus calling for improved detectors and a thorough exploration of MGT properties.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the under-explored area of MGT detection from OLLMs with a large-scale benchmark. The creation of challenging evaluation tasks like human-content revision or out-of-domain testing are relevant and useful for future research. The release of the OTBDetector model can serve as a good baseline for future development in this area. The paper's weaknesses are in limited absolute performance, and the quickly evolving LLM landscape, which may render the benchmark rapidly outdated. The performance improvement in challenging evaluation tasks are modest and requires future improvement to tackle new scenarios that exploit human and machine text combinations.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cancer-Myth: Evaluating AI Chatbot on Patient Questions with False Presuppositions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11373v1)
- **Authors**: Wang Bill Zhu, Tianqi Chen, Ching Ying Lin, Jade Law, Mazen Jizzini, Jorge J. Nieva, Ruishan Liu, Robin Jia
- **Abstract**: Cancer patients are increasingly turning to large language models (LLMs) as a new form of internet search for medical information, making it critical to assess how well these models handle complex, personalized questions. However, current medical benchmarks focus on medical exams or consumer-searched questions and do not evaluate LLMs on real patient questions with detailed clinical contexts. In this paper, we first evaluate LLMs on cancer-related questions drawn from real patients, reviewed by three hematology oncology physicians. While responses are generally accurate, with GPT-4-Turbo scoring 4.13 out of 5, the models frequently fail to recognize or address false presuppositions in the questions-posing risks to safe medical decision-making. To study this limitation systematically, we introduce Cancer-Myth, an expert-verified adversarial dataset of 585 cancer-related questions with false presuppositions. On this benchmark, no frontier LLM -- including GPT-4o, Gemini-1.Pro, and Claude-3.5-Sonnet -- corrects these false presuppositions more than 30% of the time. Even advanced medical agentic methods do not prevent LLMs from ignoring false presuppositions. These findings expose a critical gap in the clinical reliability of LLMs and underscore the need for more robust safeguards in medical AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Cancer-Myth: Evaluating Large Language Models on Patient Questions with False Presuppositions" addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of Large Language Models (LLMs) for medical advice.  It argues that existing benchmarks don't adequately assess LLMs' ability to recognize and correct false presuppositions embedded in real-world patient questions, a failure that could lead to unsafe medical decision-making. The authors introduce a new, expert-verified adversarial dataset, Cancer-Myth, consisting of cancer-related questions with deliberately planted false presuppositions.  They then evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs and a medical agentic system on this benchmark, demonstrating that these models consistently fail to identify and correct these misconceptions. The paper concludes that more robust safeguards are needed to ensure the clinical reliability of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core contribution, the Cancer-Myth dataset, is novel and addresses a crucial, under-explored area in LLM evaluation for medical applications.  Current benchmarks primarily focus on knowledge recall or question answering accuracy on medical exam-style questions, neglecting the nuanced ability to understand and respond to user misconceptions in natural language. While there has been prior work on adversarial examples and sycophancy in LLMs, this paper specifically targets the medical domain and focuses on the correction of false beliefs held by patients. The focus on false presuppositions, a specific linguistic phenomenon, adds another layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is high.  As LLMs become increasingly integrated into healthcare, the ability to identify and correct patient misconceptions is paramount for safe and effective medical communication.  The paper convincingly demonstrates that current LLMs are deficient in this crucial capability, highlighting a significant risk associated with their use as medical advisors. The insights gained from this paper could inform the development of more robust training methodologies, model architectures, and safety mechanisms for medical AI systems. By demonstrating the failure of even advanced LLMs and agentic systems, it underscores the need for careful consideration and dedicated research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of false presuppositions in patient questions and its potential impact on medical decision-making.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors use a carefully designed adversarial dataset and a thorough evaluation protocol involving expert physicians.  The process of dataset creation, involving generation, verification, and categorization, adds to the rigor.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence of LLM failure on the Cancer-Myth dataset, across multiple models and an agentic system.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper's findings offer actionable insights for future research, suggesting the need for improved training, model architectures, and safety mechanisms.
    *   **Analysis of Failure Modes:**  The paper's category-wise analysis reveals specific types of misconceptions that LLMs struggle with, providing a granular understanding of the problem.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  The dataset focuses exclusively on cancer-related questions. While cancer is a significant area, the generalizability of the findings to other medical domains could be explored further.
    *   **Dataset Size:** The size of the Cancer-Myth dataset (585 examples) is reasonable but not exceptionally large. A larger and more diverse dataset could potentially reveal more subtle patterns and challenges.
    *   **Limited Intervention Strategies Explored:** The paper demonstrates the failure of existing models and a basic agentic system, but doesn't propose or test specific interventions to improve performance. Showing at least some direction in improving model performance would be highly valuable.
    *   **The complexity of evaluating false presuppositions:** Identifying what constitutes a false presupposition and how to address it is a subjective and difficult task, even for experts. The dataset creation and annotation processes could be further detailed.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The Cancer-Myth dataset has the potential to become a valuable resource for researchers working on medical AI safety and trustworthiness. The paper's findings are likely to stimulate further research into methods for improving LLMs' ability to identify and correct patient misconceptions. The insights gleaned from this research could influence the development of more reliable and safer medical AI systems.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5**. The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting a critical, previously under-emphasized problem in LLM evaluation for medical applications and by providing a novel benchmark for assessing this capability. The methodology is sound, and the empirical results are compelling. The paper is well-written and clearly articulates its arguments. While the scope is somewhat limited to cancer, the findings have broader implications for the development of safe and reliable medical AI. The paper primarily *identifies* a problem rather than *solving* it and the dataset size is not huge. Testing intervention strategies would significantly increase the score.

### Omni$^2$: Unifying Omnidirectional Image Generation and Editing in an Omni Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11379v1)
- **Authors**: Liu Yang, Huiyu Duan, Yucheng Zhu, Xiaohong Liu, Lu Liu, Zitong Xu, Guangji Ma, Xiongkuo Min, Guangtao Zhai, Patrick Le Callet
- **Abstract**: $360^{\circ}$ omnidirectional images (ODIs) have gained considerable attention recently, and are widely used in various virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR) applications. However, capturing such images is expensive and requires specialized equipment, making ODI synthesis increasingly important. While common 2D image generation and editing methods are rapidly advancing, these models struggle to deliver satisfactory results when generating or editing ODIs due to the unique format and broad 360$^{\circ}$ Field-of-View (FoV) of ODIs. To bridge this gap, we construct \textbf{\textit{Any2Omni}}, the first comprehensive ODI generation-editing dataset comprises 60,000+ training data covering diverse input conditions and up to 9 ODI generation and editing tasks. Built upon Any2Omni, we propose an \textbf{\underline{Omni}} model for \textbf{\underline{Omni}}-directional image generation and editing (\textbf{\textit{Omni$^2$}}), with the capability of handling various ODI generation and editing tasks under diverse input conditions using one model. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority and effectiveness of the proposed Omni$^2$ model for both the ODI generation and editing tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Omni²: Unifying Omnidirectional Image Generation and Editing in an Omni Model":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Omni², a unified model for both generating and editing omnidirectional (360°) images (ODIs).  It addresses the gap in research on ODIs compared to standard 2D image generation, which has seen significant recent advances.  The authors highlight the challenges of working with the unique format and wide field-of-view of ODIs.  To facilitate research, they construct Any2Omni, a new comprehensive dataset with over 60,000 training samples covering nine ODI generation and editing tasks.  Omni² leverages a transformer-based architecture and introduces a novel viewport-based bidirectional attention mechanism for maintaining consistency across different views of the 360° image. Experimental results demonstrate Omni²'s state-of-the-art performance in ODI generation and show promising results in ODI editing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel contributions.  The creation of the Any2Omni dataset is significant, as it provides a much-needed resource for training and evaluating ODI generation and editing models.  The introduction of the viewport-based bidirectional attention mechanism is also a novel approach to enforce consistency in ODIs. Unifying multiple ODI generation and editing tasks into a single model is a notable achievement.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses an important and relatively underexplored area within generative modeling.  ODIs are critical for virtual reality (VR) and augmented reality (AR) applications, and improving the quality and accessibility of ODI generation and editing tools has a high impact.  The model demonstrates the feasibility of creating a versatile model for omnidirectional image tasks, serving as a solid baseline for future research and development.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The Any2Omni dataset is a major strength, filling a critical gap in the field.
    *   **Unified Approach:** The model’s ability to handle multiple ODI generation and editing tasks is an appealing design choice.
    *   **Technical Contribution:** The viewport-based bidirectional attention mechanism provides a novel and effective solution for ensuring consistency in generated ODIs.
    *   **Strong Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the model's superior performance compared to existing methods for ODI generation, and show good potential in editing applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation of Editing:** While the paper demonstrates the capability of ODI editing, the focus is largely on generation. A more thorough evaluation of its editing capabilities through more diverse tasks and comparisons is needed.
    *   **Dependency on Pretrained Models:** The use of pretrained models (VAE from SDXL and Transformer) may limit the model's potential for innovation at a fundamental level and its generalization capability across datasets. This is a commonly adopted strategy, but it should be acknowledged.
    *   **Editing Pipeline Simplicity:** The editing pipeline, while effective, is acknowledged to be simple. More advanced editing techniques, especially in the viewport selection and blending, might offer significant improvements.
    *   **Scope Limitation:** The Object Editing section is limited to removal/addition tasks only.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence research in ODI generation and editing.  The Any2Omni dataset could become a standard benchmark, and the Omni² model provides a strong foundation for future research in this area. It may spur the development of more advanced models that can handle complex editing operations and generate more realistic and immersive ODIs.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:** A critical point to consider is that, while the authors make good contributions, the fundamental architecture of the model relies heavily on previously established methods and pre-trained models in the 2D image domain. Therefore, the innovation relative to advancements in 2D image generative models is incremental.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of omnidirectional image generation and editing, particularly through the creation of the Any2Omni dataset and the introduction of the Omni² model.  It demonstrates the potential for unifying various ODI tasks and provides a strong baseline for future research. However, while the integration of existing techniques is well-executed, the core innovation in the architectural design is only moderate, and certain aspects of the model, particularly in the editing domain, require further development. It also makes good uses of current model like GroundedSAM. Therefore, the paper warrants a score of 7, reflecting its significant contribution, but also acknowledging its limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### RankAlign: A Ranking View of the Generator-Validator Gap in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11381v1)
- **Authors**: Juan Diego Rodriguez, Wenxuan Ding, Katrin Erk, Greg Durrett
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) have become generally more capable and accurate across many tasks, some fundamental sources of unreliability remain in their behavior. One key limitation is their inconsistency at reporting the the same information when prompts are changed. In this paper, we consider the discrepancy between a model's generated answer and their own verification of that answer, the generator-validator gap. We define this gap in a more stringent way than prior work: we expect correlation of scores from a generator and a validator over the entire set of candidate answers. We show that according to this measure, a large gap exists in various settings, including question answering, lexical semantics tasks, and next-word prediction. We then propose RankAlign, a ranking-based training method, and show that it significantly closes the gap by 31.8% on average, surpassing all baseline methods. Moreover, this approach generalizes well to out-of-domain tasks and lexical items.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel perspective on the generator-validator (G-V) gap in Large Language Models (LLMs), focusing on the correlation between generator log-odds and validator log-odds over the *entire* set of candidate answers, rather than just top-ranked answers. It argues that consistent model behavior requires strong correlation across the entire answer space. The paper then proposes a new fine-tuning method called "RankAlign," which uses a pairwise ranking loss to align validator rankings with rankings derived from generator log probabilities. Experiments across several tasks (question answering, lexical semantics, next-word prediction) demonstrate that RankAlign significantly reduces the G-V gap compared to baselines, while maintaining or slightly degrading task-specific accuracy. The approach is also shown to generalize reasonably well to out-of-domain tasks and prompt variations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a more comprehensive measure of the G-V gap. Prior work focused on agreement between the generator and validator, often only considering the top-generated answer. Shifting the focus to *correlation* across *all* candidate answers and using log-odds to capture confidence is a valuable contribution. RankAlign itself, while utilizing a ranking loss (a common technique), is novel in its application to the G-V gap problem and in aligning *rankings* of generator and validator. This differs fundamentally from approaches that merely try to align the *outputs*.
* **Significance:** The G-V gap is a significant problem that undermines trust and reliability in LLMs. By demonstrating a method that closes this gap, the paper contributes to making LLMs more consistent and therefore more useful in various downstream tasks. The empirical results are compelling, showing consistent improvements across different models, tasks, and even demonstrating some out-of-domain generalization. The practical implications are considerable, especially for applications like LLMs as judges or in scenarios where consistent assessment of multiple options is crucial.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper provides a precise definition of the G-V gap, moving beyond simple agreement metrics.
    * **Novel method:** RankAlign addresses the G-V gap in a unique way by aligning the rankings produced by both generator and validator.
    * **Strong empirical results:** Experiments demonstrate significant improvements in G-V correlation across several tasks and models.
    * **Generalization:** Evidence of out-of-domain and prompt variation robustness is a valuable finding.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Accuracy Degradation:** While the paper states mild degradation, in some tasks there is a relatively large drop in accuracy with RankAlign (e.g. TriviaQA). The trade-off between consistency and accuracy warrants further investigation. Future work needs to explore ways to minimize, or eliminate the accuracy drop.
    * **Limited Mechanistic Understanding:** While the paper demonstrates *that* RankAlign works, it offers less insight into *why* it works. Further research into the mechanistic reasons for G-V gaps and how RankAlign addresses them could be very valuable.
    * **Reliance on Hyperparameter Tuning:** The reliance on careful hyperparameter tuning, (e.g., the margin *d*), makes the method somewhat less practical to implement in new problem domains.
    * **DPO Comparison:** While the relationship to DPO is addressed, the theoretical connections could be more deeply explored.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways. First, it motivates the importance of considering the entire answer space when evaluating LLM consistency. Second, the RankAlign method provides a practical approach for improving G-V consistency that can be built upon.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable advancement in addressing a crucial limitation of LLMs. The improved definition of the G-V gap, the novel RankAlign method, the comprehensive empirical results, and the demonstration of some generalization capabilities justify a relatively high score. However, the accuracy degradation in some tasks and the limited mechanistic understanding hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. While the trade-off is acknowledged, it could be further explored.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### VideoPanda: Video Panoramic Diffusion with Multi-view Attention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11389v1)
- **Authors**: Kevin Xie, Amirmojtaba Sabour, Jiahui Huang, Despoina Paschalidou, Greg Klar, Umar Iqbal, Sanja Fidler, Xiaohui Zeng
- **Abstract**: High resolution panoramic video content is paramount for immersive experiences in Virtual Reality, but is non-trivial to collect as it requires specialized equipment and intricate camera setups. In this work, we introduce VideoPanda, a novel approach for synthesizing 360$^\circ$ videos conditioned on text or single-view video data. VideoPanda leverages multi-view attention layers to augment a video diffusion model, enabling it to generate consistent multi-view videos that can be combined into immersive panoramic content. VideoPanda is trained jointly using two conditions: text-only and single-view video, and supports autoregressive generation of long-videos. To overcome the computational burden of multi-view video generation, we randomly subsample the duration and camera views used during training and show that the model is able to gracefully generalize to generating more frames during inference. Extensive evaluations on both real-world and synthetic video datasets demonstrate that VideoPanda generates more realistic and coherent 360$^\circ$ panoramas across all input conditions compared to existing methods. Visit the project website at https://research-staging.nvidia.com/labs/toronto-ai/VideoPanda/ for results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the VideoPanda paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VideoPanda, a novel approach for generating panoramic (360°) videos conditioned on either text prompts or single-view video inputs. It leverages multi-view attention layers within a video diffusion model to synthesize consistent multi-view videos that can be stitched together to create immersive panoramic content. The model is trained jointly using both text and video conditioning, supports autoregressive generation for longer videos, and employs a random subsampling strategy during training to reduce computational burden and generalize to longer videos with more views during inference. The authors claim state-of-the-art results compared to existing methods in terms of realism and coherence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:**  Generating high-resolution panoramic video content is a challenging and important problem for virtual reality and immersive experiences. The paper directly tackles the limited availability and cost associated with capturing such content.
*   **Multi-View Attention for Consistency:** The core idea of using multi-view attention to enforce consistency between different views is sound and makes intuitive sense for panoramic video generation.  This is likely a key enabler for creating coherent 360° scenes.
*   **Combined Conditioning:**  The ability to condition on both text and single-view video provides flexibility and broadens the applicability of the model.  Video-to-panoramic generation is particularly useful for extending existing video content.
*   **Autoregressive Generation and Scalability:** The approach to autoregressive generation to create longer videos is a standard technique, but its inclusion here, combined with the random sub-sampling training, helps address the practical issue of generating long, high-resolution panoramic videos without excessive computational costs.
*   **Quantitative and Qualitative Results:** The paper presents both quantitative metrics (FID, FVD, Clip score) and qualitative results, which is essential for evaluating generative models.  The user study adds further weight to the subjective assessment of quality.
*   **CogVLM Integration**: The incorporation of cogVLM is a nice feature to have because it enhances the model's ability to understand and generate based on text prompts.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:**  While the combination of techniques is effective, each individual component (diffusion models, multi-view attention, autoregressive generation) is well-established. The novelty lies primarily in the specific combination and application to panoramic video generation.

*   **Limited Comparison:**  The paper primarily compares against 360DVD. While this is a relevant baseline, a more comprehensive comparison against other recent video generation methods (especially if they could be adapted or extended to the panoramic setting) would strengthen the claims.
*   **Dataset Reliance:** The paper heavily relies on the WEB360 dataset. While it's understandable due to the scarcity of panoramic video data, it's important to acknowledge any potential biases or limitations of this dataset and how they might affect the generalizability of the model.
*   **Lack of Detail on Network Architecture:** The paper mentions adding "3D multi-view self-attention layers", but the implementation details for these layers and how they interact with the existing 2D self-attention layers is not very clear. This lack of specificity makes it hard to implement or verify the proposed architecture
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** Some ablation studies are incomplete, such as the one on shifting the noise schedule. It is not clear whether a better noise schedule cannot be used if the noise augmentations are implemented.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of video generation by specifically addressing the challenges of panoramic video creation. VideoPanda provides a practical and effective approach for generating 360° videos, potentially lowering the barrier to entry for creating immersive experiences. While the individual components are not revolutionary, their combination and adaptation to this specific problem domain is well-executed.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, a score of **7** is appropriate.

*   The paper addresses a relevant and challenging problem.
*   The multi-view attention mechanism is a valuable contribution to panoramic video generation.
*   The combination of techniques is effective, and the results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance.
*   However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, and the comparison against other methods could be more comprehensive.
*   The dataset reliance and potential biases should be acknowledged.
*   Several aspects of the model and the experiments could be described in more detail for better clarity.

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research in panoramic video generation. However, further improvements and more extensive evaluations are needed to solidify its significance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### DataDecide: How to Predict Best Pretraining Data with Small Experiments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11393v1)
- **Authors**: Ian Magnusson, Nguyen Tai, Ben Bogin, David Heineman, Jena D. Hwang, Luca Soldaini, Akshita Bhagia, Jiacheng Liu, Dirk Groeneveld, Oyvind Tafjord, Noah A. Smith, Pang Wei Koh, Jesse Dodge
- **Abstract**: Because large language models are expensive to pretrain on different datasets, using smaller-scale experiments to decide on data is crucial for reducing costs. Which benchmarks and methods of making decisions from observed performance at small scale most accurately predict the datasets that yield the best large models? To empower open exploration of this question, we release models, data, and evaluations in DataDecide -- the most extensive open suite of models over differences in data and scale. We conduct controlled pretraining experiments across 25 corpora with differing sources, deduplication, and filtering up to 100B tokens, model sizes up to 1B parameters, and 3 random seeds. We find that the ranking of models at a single, small size (e.g., 150M parameters) is a strong baseline for predicting best models at our larger target scale (1B) (~80% of com parisons correct). No scaling law methods among 8 baselines exceed the compute-decision frontier of single-scale predictions, but DataDecide can measure improvement in future scaling laws. We also identify that using continuous likelihood metrics as proxies in small experiments makes benchmarks including MMLU, ARC, HellaSwag, MBPP, and HumanEval >80% predictable at the target 1B scale with just 0.01% of the compute.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "How to Predict Best Pretraining Data with Small Experiments":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the crucial problem of efficiently selecting the best pretraining data for large language models (LLMs) without incurring the prohibitively high cost of training multiple full-scale models. The authors introduce DATADECIDE, a comprehensive suite of pretrained models, datasets, and evaluations. It involves pretraining models on 25 different data recipes (corpora with varying sources, deduplication, and filtering) across a range of model sizes (4M to 1B parameters) with three random seeds. This allows for a controlled empirical study of which small-scale experiments and decision-making methods can accurately predict the performance of larger models trained on those datasets. The paper benchmarks various prediction methods, including single-scale experiments and extrapolating scaling laws, and measures their accuracy in predicting pairwise dataset comparisons at the 1B parameter scale. The key findings include the effectiveness of single-scale experiments (using a 150M parameter model) as a baseline and the observation that tasks like MMLU and ARC are more easily predictable than others. The paper also explores the use of continuous likelihood metrics as proxies for discrete accuracy metrics to improve predictability at smaller scales.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main strength lies in its **creation and release of the DATADECIDE suite.** This dataset with pretrained models across many data recipes, model sizes, and random seeds, provides a standardized and controlled environment for studying pretraining data selection. This is a **valuable resource** for the community and addresses a critical gap in empirical research on data scaling. While the methods explored (single-scale training, scaling law extrapolation) are not entirely novel, the **thorough and systematic comparison within the DATADECIDE framework** is.
*   **Significance:** The research question is highly relevant, given the increasing cost of LLM training. Being able to reliably predict the performance of a data recipe from smaller experiments has great practical and economical importance. The paper also offers **valuable practical recommendations** about benchmarks, metrics, and prediction methods. In particular, The finding of high single scale performance has a big impact on research community, providing a strong and cheap baseline. 

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The large-scale, controlled experimental setup of DATADECIDE is a significant accomplishment.
    *   The comprehensive comparison of prediction methods offers actionable insights for practitioners.
    *   The identification of task-specific predictability differences is important for resource allocation.
    *   The exploration of proxy metrics contributes to developing more efficient data selection strategies.
    *   Releasing the models, data, and evaluation code promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the variety of corpora considered is significant, focusing on "data recipes" (variations of existing corpora) might limit the exploration of entirely new data sources.
    *   The scaling law extrapolation methods used may not be state-of-the-art. While the result that no tested scaling law can perform as well as a simple single scale, these scaling laws might have parameters/hyper parameters unoptimized for the task.
    *   The study focuses exclusively on the OLMES benchmark suite. Expanding to other downstream tasks could strengthen the generality of the findings.
    *   The authors make a simplifying assumption that FLOPs adequately represents the costs of training, which assumes equal accelerator utilization.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has strong potential to influence future research directions in LLM pretraining. It provides a concrete framework and dataset for evaluating data selection strategies, which can accelerate the development of more efficient and cost-effective training methods.

*   **Justification for Score:**  The paper is not groundbreaking with totally novel methods; but the contribution lies in the scale and rigor of the experiments and the practical insights. DATADECIDE is a useful resource, and the findings have the potential to impact the resource allocation of the community when deciding to pretrain.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging Point Transformers for Detecting Anatomical Landmarks in Digital Dentistry
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11418v1)
- **Authors**: Tibor Kubík, Oldřich Kodym, Petr Šilling, Kateřina Trávníčková, Tomáš Mojžiš, Jan Matula
- **Abstract**: The increasing availability of intraoral scanning devices has heightened their importance in modern clinical orthodontics. Clinicians utilize advanced Computer-Aided Design techniques to create patient-specific treatment plans that include laboriously identifying crucial landmarks such as cusps, mesial-distal locations, facial axis points, and tooth-gingiva boundaries. Detecting such landmarks automatically presents challenges, including limited dataset sizes, significant anatomical variability among subjects, and the geometric nature of the data. We present our experiments from the 3DTeethLand Grand Challenge at MICCAI 2024. Our method leverages recent advancements in point cloud learning through transformer architectures. We designed a Point Transformer v3 inspired module to capture meaningful geometric and anatomical features, which are processed by a lightweight decoder to predict per-point distances, further processed by graph-based non-minima suppression. We report promising results and discuss insights on learned feature interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a justified score:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Point Transformer v3 (PTv3) architecture for automatic anatomical landmark detection in 3D digital dentistry scans. The approach involves using PTv3 to extract geometric and anatomical features from point clouds representing teeth, followed by an MLP-based decoder to predict per-point distances (distance maps) to landmarks.  A novel topology-driven graph-based non-minima suppression (CTD-NMS) algorithm is then applied to refine the landmark positions. The authors participate in the 3DTeethLand Grand Challenge at MICCAI 2024 and report promising results in terms of precision and recall.  The paper also investigates the interpretability of the learned features and the influence of distance map transformations. The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its application of the recent Point Transformer v3 architecture, known for its speed and simplicity in general 3D vision tasks, to the specific domain of dental landmark detection. While PTv3 itself isn't a new invention, its use within this context, combined with the CTD-NMS algorithm, represents a novel combination. The CTD-NMS algorithm is also a novel approach that incorporates topological information into the post-processing stage.

*   **Significance:** Automated landmark detection in dentistry is a practically significant problem, as it streamlines treatment planning and orthodontics. Applying a state-of-the-art point cloud learning technique like PTv3 can potentially improve the accuracy and efficiency of this process. The paper also emphasizes the interpretability of features. A significant strength of this paper is its practical focus, aiming to address limitations of previous approaches. Publicly releasing the code further increases the significance of the work by enabling reproducibility and follow-up research. The paper presents some results in terms of precision and recall, demonstrating the potential of the proposed method. However, a more detailed comparison against existing techniques would strengthen the claim of improved performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Use of a recent and powerful point cloud learning architecture (PTv3).
    *   Novel CTD-NMS algorithm that leverages topological information.
    *   Analysis of feature interpretability.
    *   Public availability of code.
    *   Addresses the limitation of previous methods needing the pre-definition of number of landmarks.
    *   Versatility and applicability of framework across different anatomical contexts within orthodontics.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited quantitative comparison to existing state-of-the-art methods in dental landmark detection. The paper primarily focuses on describing the method and reporting results, rather than rigorously comparing it against baselines.
    *   The dataset used is relatively small (200 annotated cases), which can limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   Some implementation details are not fully elaborated, making it potentially challenging to reproduce the results exactly.
    *   There is limited depth in analysing the interpretability, beyond PCA plotting. More specific analysis related to how the model weights reflect actual dental anatomy would add more.
    *   The performance results are only given on the validation set, which is not as convincing as performing the evaluation on the withheld test set.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating the effectiveness of transformer-based architectures for dental landmark detection and by introducing a novel topology-aware post-processing technique.  The open-source code will likely facilitate further research and development in this area. However, its real impact depends on how the approach fares in more comprehensive comparisons and real-world clinical applications.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by applying a cutting-edge deep learning technique to a relevant and practically important problem in digital dentistry. The PTv3-based approach, along with the CTD-NMS algorithm, shows promise for accurate landmark detection. The code availability is a major plus. However, the lack of extensive comparative results, the limited dataset size, and the relative lack of in-depth feature interpretability analysis, limit the overall impact and prevent a higher score. Furthermore, results on the official test dataset would strengthen the paper. A score of 7 reflects the novelty and potential of the work, while acknowledging its limitations in experimental validation and analysis depth.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reinforcing Compositional Retrieval: Retrieving Step-by-Step for Composing Informative Contexts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11420v1)
- **Authors**: Quanyu Long, Jianda Chen, Zhengyuan Liu, Nancy F. Chen, Wenya Wang, Sinno Jialin Pan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across numerous tasks, yet they often rely on external context to handle complex tasks. While retrieval-augmented frameworks traditionally focus on selecting top-ranked documents in a single pass, many real-world scenarios demand compositional retrieval, where multiple sources must be combined in a coordinated manner. In this work, we propose a tri-encoder sequential retriever that models this process as a Markov Decision Process (MDP), decomposing the probability of retrieving a set of elements into a sequence of conditional probabilities and allowing each retrieval step to be conditioned on previously selected examples. We train the retriever in two stages: first, we efficiently construct supervised sequential data for initial policy training; we then refine the policy to align with the LLM's preferences using a reward grounded in the structural correspondence of generated programs. Experimental results show that our method consistently and significantly outperforms baselines, underscoring the importance of explicitly modeling inter-example dependencies. These findings highlight the potential of compositional retrieval for tasks requiring multiple pieces of evidence or examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new approach to retrieval-augmented large language models (LLMs) called Reinforcing Compositional Retrieval (RCR). Unlike conventional retrieval methods that select context in a single pass, RCR models retrieval as a Markov Decision Process (MDP), sequentially selecting examples where each step is conditioned on previously selected items. This allows the system to capture inter-example dependencies and assemble more informative contexts for the LLM.  The retriever is trained in two stages: first with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) using a novel data construction method that maximizes sub-structure coverage, and then with reinforcement learning (RL) using a reward based on local structural similarity between the generated and ground truth programs.  Experiments on compositional generalization semantic parsing benchmarks demonstrate that RCR consistently outperforms top-k and sequential retrieval baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its formulation of compositional retrieval as an MDP and the development of a tri-encoder architecture to explicitly model inter-example dependencies. The two-stage training process, specifically the data construction method for SFT, and reward function for RL, are also novel contributions.  While sequential retrieval has been explored before, the explicit modeling of inter-example dependencies and the efficient data construction method distinguish this work. The use of GRPO as the optimization method is not novel by itself, but its application in the context of compositional retrieval contributes to overall novelty. The most novel aspect is the framework of explicitly modeling the sequential retrieval process as an MDP.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial limitation of existing retrieval-augmented LLMs, namely their inability to effectively combine multiple pieces of evidence or examples with diverse semantics. Compositional retrieval is vital for complex tasks that require a coordinated understanding of different sources.  The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of RCR in improving program generation quality, suggesting its potential impact on other tasks that require multiple pieces of evidence, such as multi-hop reasoning or knowledge graph completion.
The significance is well-demonstrated in the semantic parsing domain, a suitable initial testbed to illustrate the importance of structured, compositional retrieval. The potential future extension of this work to areas like multi-hop QA makes the method significantly important.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of compositional retrieval and highlights the limitations of existing methods.
    * **Well-Designed Approach:**  The MDP formulation and the tri-encoder architecture provide a sound framework for modeling inter-example dependencies.
    * **Efficient Training:**  The data construction method for SFT avoids the computational overhead of scoring LMs while maintaining a more interpretable selection process. The use of the tri-encoder helps to mitigate computational costs associated with the MDP formulation.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experimental results consistently show that RCR outperforms baselines across different compositional generalization benchmarks. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components of the system.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct various experiments to analyze the impact of different factors, such as the number of training samples, advantage estimation methods, and group size.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily focused on semantic parsing tasks. While this is a good starting point, it would be beneficial to demonstrate the effectiveness of RCR on other tasks that require compositional retrieval, such as multi-hop question answering or document summarization. It remains to be demonstrated whether the local structure-based reward transfers easily to other complex reasoning domains.
    * **Limited Model Diversity**: It uses bert-base-uncased embeddings which, while standard, are relatively small and does not make explicit considerations of model diversity during the pre-training steps (e.g. training on code generation or scientific domains).

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on retrieval-augmented LLMs by highlighting the importance of modeling inter-example dependencies and providing a practical framework for compositional retrieval. It may also inspire the development of new training techniques and reward functions that are tailored to specific tasks.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of retrieval-augmented language models. The formulation of compositional retrieval as an MDP and the development of the RCR architecture are novel and well-executed. The efficient training process and strong empirical results further strengthen the paper. However, the limited scope of evaluation and areas for additional model diversity prevent it from achieving a higher score. Despite these minor limitations, the paper is a valuable contribution that is likely to influence future research on compositional retrieval and context augmentation for LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ADT: Tuning Diffusion Models with Adversarial Supervision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11423v1)
- **Authors**: Dazhong Shen, Guanglu Song, Yi Zhang, Bingqi Ma, Lujundong Li, Dongzhi Jiang, Zhuofan Zong, Yu Liu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved outstanding image generation by reversing a forward noising process to approximate true data distributions. During training, these models predict diffusion scores from noised versions of true samples in a single forward pass, while inference requires iterative denoising starting from white noise. This training-inference divergences hinder the alignment between inference and training data distributions, due to potential prediction biases and cumulative error accumulation. To address this problem, we propose an intuitive but effective fine-tuning framework, called Adversarial Diffusion Tuning (ADT), by stimulating the inference process during optimization and aligning the final outputs with training data by adversarial supervision. Specifically, to achieve robust adversarial training, ADT features a siamese-network discriminator with a fixed pre-trained backbone and lightweight trainable parameters, incorporates an image-to-image sampling strategy to smooth discriminative difficulties, and preserves the original diffusion loss to prevent discriminator hacking. In addition, we carefully constrain the backward-flowing path for back-propagating gradients along the inference path without incurring memory overload or gradient explosion. Finally, extensive experiments on Stable Diffusion models (v1.5, XL, and v3), demonstrate that ADT significantly improves both distribution alignment and image quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adversarial Diffusion Tuning (ADT), a novel fine-tuning framework designed to improve diffusion models by addressing the training-inference discrepancy. ADT stimulates the inference process during optimization and aligns final outputs with training data through adversarial supervision. Key components of ADT include a Siamese-network discriminator with a fixed pre-trained backbone, an image-to-image sampling strategy to stabilize adversarial training, preservation of the original diffusion loss to prevent discriminator hacking, and constrained backward propagation of gradients along the inference path to avoid memory overload and gradient explosion. The authors validate ADT's effectiveness on Stable Diffusion models (v1.5, XL, and v3), demonstrating improvements in distribution alignment and image quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of addressing the training-inference divergence in diffusion models using adversarial fine-tuning is not entirely new; however, the specific combination of techniques in ADT shows reasonable novelty. The paper combines elements of GANs, VAEs, and existing diffusion model fine-tuning approaches (like DRTune) in a unique way. Specifically, the contributions related to the Siamese discriminator with a frozen backbone, the image-to-image sampling strategy, and the backward-flowing path constraint are interesting and show incremental improvements over previous methods. However, the individual components have precedents in the literature. The main novelty lies in the specific combination and adaptation of these components in the ADT framework.

* **Significance:** The paper presents a practical and effective approach to fine-tuning diffusion models. The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements in image quality and distribution alignment across different Stable Diffusion variants. The benefits of improved distribution alignment are practically important for real-world applications, ensuring that the generated images are more representative of the training data and avoid the introduction of biases. The paper shows that it achieves better results than the original FT fine-tuning strategy.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper addresses multiple aspects of diffusion model fine-tuning, including adversarial training stability, memory efficiency, and gradient control.
    * **Strong Experimental Validation:** The experiments are thorough, covering different Stable Diffusion models, samplers, and datasets. The use of multiple metrics (FID, HPS, AES) and human evaluation strengthens the claims.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of ADT.
    * **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is generally clear and well-structured, making it easy to follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Innovation:** While the ADT framework is effective, its individual components build upon existing techniques. The level of innovation is incremental rather than groundbreaking.
    * **Computational Cost:** Although the authors address memory efficiency, the adversarial training process still adds to the overall computational cost compared to vanilla fine-tuning. While not fully described, the paper claims that ADT is able to reach similar results in less steps than regular FT strategies.
    * **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** The paper focuses primarily on Stable Diffusion models. While this is a reasonable scope, it would be beneficial to discuss the potential generalizability of ADT to other types of diffusion models or generative tasks.
    * **Dependence on Pre-trained Discriminator:**  Performance is directly linked to the performance of the pre-trained discriminator, potentially limiting overall performance.

* **Potential Impact:** ADT could have a moderate impact on the field by providing a practical and effective fine-tuning strategy for diffusion models. It is likely to be of interest to researchers and practitioners working on image generation and related tasks. However, because of the incremental innovation of the method, its overall impact might be limited.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. While the paper presents a well-executed and effective fine-tuning framework for diffusion models, the level of novelty is incremental rather than transformative.  The combination of techniques is clever, and the experimental results are convincing. However, the individual components have precedents in the literature. I believe that it can have some impact on downstream applications.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Dual-Space Framework for General Knowledge Distillation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11426v1)
- **Authors**: Xue Zhang, Songming Zhang, Yunlong Liang, Fandong Meng, Yufeng Chen, Jinan Xu, Jie Zhou
- **Abstract**: Knowledge distillation (KD) is a promising solution to compress large language models (LLMs) by transferring their knowledge to smaller models. During this process, white-box KD methods usually minimize the distance between the output distributions of the teacher model and the student model to transfer more information. However, we reveal that the current white-box KD framework exhibits two limitations: a) bridging probability distributions from different output spaces will limit the similarity between the teacher model and the student model; b) this framework cannot be applied to LLMs with different vocabularies. One of the root causes for these limitations is that the distributions from the teacher and the student for KD are output by different prediction heads, which yield distributions in different output spaces and dimensions. Therefore, in this paper, we propose a dual-space knowledge distillation (DSKD) framework that unifies the prediction heads of the teacher and the student models for KD. Specifically, we first introduce two projectors with ideal initialization to project the teacher/student hidden states into the student/teacher representation spaces. After this, the hidden states from different models can share the same head and unify the output spaces of the distributions. Furthermore, we develop an exact token alignment (ETA) algorithm to align the same tokens in two differently-tokenized sequences. Based on the above, our DSKD framework is a general KD framework that supports both off-policy and on-policy KD, and KD between any two LLMs regardless of their vocabularies. Extensive experiments on instruction-following, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks show that DSKD significantly outperforms existing methods based on the current white-box KD framework and surpasses other cross-tokenizer KD methods for LLMs with different vocabularies.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces Dual-Space Knowledge Distillation (DSKD), a novel framework for knowledge distillation of Large Language Models (LLMs). The key idea behind DSKD is to unify the output spaces of the teacher and student models, by using projectors to map hidden states of the teacher and student models into shared representation spaces. This allows the models, even with differing vocabularies, to learn from each other more efficiently by sharing prediction heads. The paper also introduces an Exact Token Alignment (ETA) algorithm to facilitate distillation between models with different tokenizers. The authors demonstrate through extensive experiments on various instruction-following, reasoning, and code generation benchmarks that DSKD outperforms existing white-box knowledge distillation methods and surpasses cross-tokenizer KD techniques. The paper also extends DSKD to the on-policy KD setting.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

While the paper presents a technically sound approach, the novelty and significance require a critical assessment. Here's a breakdown:

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addressing a clear limitation: The paper directly tackles the problems of vocabulary mismatch and limited teacher-student similarity in existing white-box KD for LLMs, which is a relevant issue given the proliferation of LLMs with varying architectures.
    *   Technical Soundness: The proposed DSKD framework and ETA algorithm are well-defined, and the implementation details seem thorough. The experiments are comprehensive, covering a range of benchmarks and model sizes.
    *   Strong Empirical Results: The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of DSKD over existing methods across different tasks and settings, which contributes a tangible improvement.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental Novelty: While the combination of the projectors and the ETA algorithm is presented as novel, projector based method has been proposed in other knowledge distillation area, and existing cross-tokenizer knowledge distillation methods can solve the vocabulary issue. The core idea of unifying output spaces or feature spaces through linear transformations isn't entirely new to the field of knowledge distillation or transfer learning.
    *   Complexity: The introduction of projectors and an alignment algorithm adds complexity to the KD process, which may increase training time. This is a potential trade-off that needs to be carefully considered.
    * Limited theoretical analysis: While empirical results are provided, the paper does not thoroughly analyze the underlying theoretical justifications for why DSKD works beyond unifying the output spaces. A more formal understanding of how the projectors affect the knowledge transfer process would strengthen the paper.

*   **Significance:**
    *   Practical Impact: DSKD offers a practical solution to distill knowledge between LLMs with differing vocabularies, making it useful in real-world scenarios where access to a teacher model with the same vocabulary is not always possible.
    *   Community Inspiration: This work could inspire new directions in knowledge distillation, especially for handling heterogeneous LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper tackles a real-world problem within the KD field and shows improvement. However, the components themselves are not revolutionary, and the novelty primarily comes from combining existing ideas in a specific way for LLMs. More extensive theoretical analysis will improve the significance of the paper.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### NormalCrafter: Learning Temporally Consistent Normals from Video Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11427v1)
- **Authors**: Yanrui Bin, Wenbo Hu, Haoyuan Wang, Xinya Chen, Bing Wang
- **Abstract**: Surface normal estimation serves as a cornerstone for a spectrum of computer vision applications. While numerous efforts have been devoted to static image scenarios, ensuring temporal coherence in video-based normal estimation remains a formidable challenge. Instead of merely augmenting existing methods with temporal components, we present NormalCrafter to leverage the inherent temporal priors of video diffusion models. To secure high-fidelity normal estimation across sequences, we propose Semantic Feature Regularization (SFR), which aligns diffusion features with semantic cues, encouraging the model to concentrate on the intrinsic semantics of the scene. Moreover, we introduce a two-stage training protocol that leverages both latent and pixel space learning to preserve spatial accuracy while maintaining long temporal context. Extensive evaluations demonstrate the efficacy of our method, showcasing a superior performance in generating temporally consistent normal sequences with intricate details from diverse videos.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the NormalCrafter paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NormalCrafter, a novel video normal estimation model. The key idea is to leverage video diffusion models for generating temporally consistent normal sequences from open-world videos. The authors propose two main innovations: 1) Semantic Feature Regularization (SFR), which aligns diffusion features with semantic cues to encourage the model to focus on the intrinsic semantics of the scene; and 2) a two-stage training protocol that leverages both latent and pixel space learning to preserve spatial accuracy while maintaining long temporal context.  Experiments show improved spatial fidelity and temporal consistency compared to existing image-based normal estimation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in multiple aspects. The use of video diffusion models as priors for normal estimation, instead of simply augmenting existing image-based methods with temporal layers, is a good starting point. The SFR technique, which explicitly encourages the diffusion model to align with semantic features from a pre-trained encoder (DINO), is a clever way to address the over-smoothing issues that often arise when naively applying diffusion models. The two-stage training approach, designed to balance computational cost and accuracy, is another significant contribution. This careful balance ensures long sequence processing without excessive memory requirements.

*   **Significance:** The ability to generate temporally consistent and detailed normal maps from videos is significant for a wide range of computer vision applications like 3D reconstruction, relighting, video editing and mixed reality. The paper presents results that are noticeably better than existing approaches, particularly concerning temporal consistency. It tackles a challenging problem, which is to create normal map sequences that are stable without flicker artifacts, while maintaining high spatial accuracy.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:** Clearly identifies the problem of temporal inconsistency in video normal estimation.
    *   **Methodology:**  Well-motivated and technically sound. SFR and two-stage training address specific issues in a principled way.
    *   **Experiments:** Extensive evaluation on diverse datasets, including both single-image and video benchmarks. Qualitative results are also convincing.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  Thorough ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational cost:** The method relies on diffusion models, which can be computationally expensive, particularly during training. While the two-stage training reduces this, it is still not insignificant. While a future direction is model efficiency through model pruning and distillation techniques, this is an important limitation and a potential hinderance to widespread adoption.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** While the paper tries to use diverse datasets, performance may still be affected by biases in the training data. The generalizability to truly arbitrary, unpredictable video content remains to be seen.

*   **Potential Influence:** The NormalCrafter approach has the potential to influence future research in video normal estimation. The use of diffusion models as priors and the proposed SFR technique are promising directions. The careful consideration of temporal consistency and spatial accuracy is important for downstream applications. The release of the model will encourage further research and comparisons in this area.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper is not without its limitations, the technical contributions are substantial and represent a significant advancement in the field of video normal estimation. The methods address a challenging problem, and the proposed solutions are well-motivated and effective. The experiments are comprehensive and demonstrate superior performance compared to existing approaches. However, the computational cost and the reliance on large datasets are notable drawbacks. On the balance, the NormalCrafter approach addresses several challenges in the field of video normal estimation, offers innovative solutions, and has considerable potential for impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
