To address such an issue, we leverage the Finite State Machine (FSM) on LLMs, and propose a framework called FiSMiness. Our framework allows a single LLM to bootstrap the planning during ESC, and self-reason the seeker's emotion, support strategy and the final response upon each conversational turn. Substantial experiments on ESC datasets suggest that FiSMiness outperforms many baselines, including direct inference, self-refine, chain of thought, finetuning, and external-assisted methods, even those with many more parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FiSMiness: A Finite State Machine Based Paradigm for Emotional Support Conversations":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel framework, FiSMiness, for emotional support conversations (ESC) using a Finite State Machine (FSM) approach. The framework leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to model the different stages of an ESC, including recognizing the seeker's emotion, determining the appropriate support strategy, and generating a response. The FSM structure aims to overcome the myopic nature of existing LLM-based ESC systems by providing a lookahead strategy planning mechanism. The paper presents experimental results on the ESConv dataset, demonstrating that FiSMiness outperforms several baselines, including direct inference, self-refinement, and methods incorporating external knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the application of FSMs to emotional support conversations. While FSMs have been used in other NLP tasks like question answering and reasoning, their use in ESC is relatively unexplored. The paper convincingly argues that FSMs provide a structured approach for strategy planning and stage transitions, addressing a key limitation of existing LLM-based ESC systems. However, the individual components (emotion recognition, strategy selection, response generation) are standard LLM tasks, reducing the overall novelty somewhat.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the quality and coherence of emotional support conversations generated by LLMs. By providing a structured framework, FiSMiness can help ensure that the conversation progresses logically and addresses the seeker's needs effectively. The experimental results on ESConv provide empirical evidence for the effectiveness of the approach. However, the significance could be further strengthened by demonstrating the framework's applicability to other ESC datasets and real-world scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-structured paper.
    *   The FSM-based approach is intuitively appealing and well-motivated.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with several baselines.
    *   Demonstrates improved performance over existing methods on the ESConv dataset.
    * Addresses an important limitation of existing LLM based ESC solutions, which lack long-term strategy planning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual components of the FSM (emotion recognition, strategy selection, response generation) are not inherently novel.
    *   The evaluation is primarily focused on the ESConv dataset, which may not be representative of all ESC scenarios.
    *   The paper does not explore the limitations of the FSM-based approach in detail, such as handling unexpected user inputs or complex emotional states.
    * The state transitions in the proposed FSM appear relatively simple and lack a deeper exploration of how to manage complex emotional states or handle unexpected user input.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective and coherent emotional support systems based on LLMs. The FSM framework provides a valuable starting point for future research in this area. The paper may also encourage researchers to explore other structured approaches for improving the long-term planning capabilities of LLMs in conversational settings.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** While the paper's claims are supported by experiments, the improvements, while present, do not seem earth shattering. The novelty rests on a methodological application rather than a conceptual breakthrough. Further, the focus on a single, benchmark dataset limits how broadly the impact might be construed.

Score: 6

The paper presents a sound methodology and provides empirical support for its effectiveness within the confines of the ESConv dataset. The degree of innovation however, does not suggest that this is a *must-read* type of paper that will shape the future direction of ESC research, but it is still a valuable contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### GT-SVQ: A Linear-Time Graph Transformer for Node Classification Using Spiking Vector Quantization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11840v1)
- **Authors**: Huizhe Zhang, Jintang Li, Yuchang Zhu, Liang Chen, Zibin Zheng
- **Abstract**: Graph Transformers (GTs), which simultaneously integrate message-passing and self-attention mechanisms, have achieved promising empirical results in some graph prediction tasks. Although these approaches show the potential of Transformers in capturing long-range graph topology information, issues concerning the quadratic complexity and high computing energy consumption severely limit the scalability of GTs on large-scale graphs. Recently, as brain-inspired neural networks, Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs), facilitate the development of graph representation learning methods with lower computational and storage overhead through the unique event-driven spiking neurons. Inspired by these characteristics, we propose a linear-time Graph Transformer using Spiking Vector Quantization (GT-SVQ) for node classification. GT-SVQ reconstructs codebooks based on rate coding outputs from spiking neurons, and injects the codebooks into self-attention blocks to aggregate global information in linear complexity. Besides, spiking vector quantization effectively alleviates codebook collapse and the reliance on complex machinery (distance measure, auxiliary loss, etc.) present in previous vector quantization-based graph learning methods. In experiments, we compare GT-SVQ with other state-of-the-art baselines on node classification datasets ranging from small to large. Experimental results show that GT-SVQ has achieved competitive performances on most datasets while maintaining up to 130x faster inference speed compared to other GTs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GT-SVQ, a linear-time Graph Transformer designed for node classification. GT-SVQ leverages spiking vector quantization (SVQ) to create codebooks based on rate-coded outputs from spiking neurons. These codebooks are then integrated into self-attention mechanisms, enabling the aggregation of global information with linear complexity. This addresses scalability limitations present in traditional Graph Transformers. The authors claim GT-SVQ alleviates codebook collapse and reduces reliance on complex machinery often found in previous VQ-based methods.  Experimental results on various node classification datasets demonstrate GT-SVQ's competitive performance and improved inference speed compared to other Graph Transformers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Scalability:**  The primary strength of GT-SVQ is its claimed linear-time complexity, addressing a critical bottleneck in applying Graph Transformers to large-scale graphs.  The use of spiking vector quantization, a brain-inspired technique, presents a novel approach to achieving this efficiency.
    *   **Codebook Collapse Mitigation:** The paper presents a viable solution to the codebook collapse problem by using spiking neurons as quantizers, which alleviates the problem of pre-defined codebooks.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on a diverse set of datasets. The reported speedups (up to 130x) compared to other GTs are significant and compelling, assuming they are accurate. Also, performance is on-par or better than other high-precision GT methods.
    * **Inference Speed**: The results of the experiment in Table 3 show that GT-SVQ achieves the lowest inference latency across all datasets.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **True Novelty of SVQ:** The degree of novelty of SVQ within *this context* is questionable.  While SVQ itself might be established, its application within a Graph Transformer setting *and* integrated with spiking neurons constitutes a contribution.  However, the use of vector quantization in transformers is not entirely new. It would benefit the paper to clearly differentiate from *exactly* how GT-SVQ is different, and why those differences are critical.
    *   **Overclaiming Performance:**  While results are mostly on-par, the paper overstates the case that it is *better*. Table 2 only shows 2 results that are marked as *best*, and there is a lot of variance.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper could benefit from a more rigorous theoretical analysis of the linear complexity claim. While the intuition behind using SVQ for complexity reduction is clear, a more formal proof or analysis of the complexity would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Heterophily datasets:** The paper states that "GT-SVQ has comparable performances on two heterophily datasets". This needs more discussion, since in a lot of scenarios, performance is still sub-par to other methods.
    *   **Codebook usage:** the number of codewords is quite large - how efficient is the codebook usage in comparison to the number of nodes? Would it be feasible to store codewords corresponding to each node on neuromorphic hardware?
    * **Limited Scope of Novelty**: The use of surrogate gradients and Integrate-and-Fire neuron models is fairly standard in the SNN literature. The adaptation of these techniques to graph transformers, while effective, doesn't represent a radical innovation.

*   **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field by enabling the application of Graph Transformers to larger graphs.
    *   The integration of spiking neural networks with Graph Transformers opens up new avenues for research in efficient graph representation learning.
    *   The code release will likely encourage further exploration and adoption of the GT-SVQ approach.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper makes a solid contribution to the field, but some weaknesses need to be addressed. While the individual components (SNNs, Vector Quantization, Transformers) are established, the combination and application to graph representation learning *does* present a level of novelty. The experimental results are compelling, but a more rigorous theoretical analysis is needed.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.NE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating the Goal-Directedness of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11844v1)
- **Authors**: Tom Everitt, Cristina Garbacea, Alexis Bellot, Jonathan Richens, Henry Papadatos, Siméon Campos, Rohin Shah
- **Abstract**: To what extent do LLMs use their capabilities towards their given goal? We take this as a measure of their goal-directedness. We evaluate goal-directedness on tasks that require information gathering, cognitive effort, and plan execution, where we use subtasks to infer each model's relevant capabilities. Our evaluations of LLMs from Google DeepMind, OpenAI, and Anthropic show that goal-directedness is relatively consistent across tasks, differs from task performance, and is only moderately sensitive to motivational prompts. Notably, most models are not fully goal-directed. We hope our goal-directedness evaluations will enable better monitoring of LLM progress, and enable more deliberate design choices of agentic properties in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for evaluating the "goal-directedness" of Large Language Models (LLMs). Goal-directedness is defined as the propensity of an LLM to use available resources and capabilities to achieve a given goal, irrespective of its inherent capabilities. The authors construct a Blocksworld environment and design several composite tasks (Information Gathering, Cognitive Effort, Plan and Execute, and Combined Task) composed of independent subtasks. They measure LLMs' performance on both the composite tasks and the subtasks, inferring their capabilities from subtask performance. By comparing the actual performance on composite tasks with what would be expected if the LLM fully utilized its inferred capabilities, they quantify goal-directedness. They evaluate state-of-the-art models from Google DeepMind, OpenAI, and Anthropic, finding that most models are not fully goal-directed and that goal-directedness is relatively consistent across tasks but only moderately responsive to motivational prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central novelty of the paper lies in providing a *measurable* definition for goal-directedness that is distinctly separated from a model's overall task performance. This is a crucial distinction, as it allows for a more nuanced understanding of LLM agency. The use of subtasks to infer capabilities is a practical approach, given the "black box" nature of many LLMs. However, defining the 'correct' set of subtasks and ensuring they accurately reflect the underlying capabilities is not an easy problem, and the paper touches on it as an assumption. Prior work often equated goal-directedness with task completion, which this paper explicitly addresses.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key aspect of LLM safety and agentic behavior. Understanding the extent to which LLMs actively pursue their goals is vital for anticipating potential risks associated with increasingly autonomous systems. The framework could be instrumental in monitoring LLM progress, designing agents with specific agentic properties (including safety considerations) and even detecting subtle changes in model behavior. The findings that goal-directedness varies across models and is not always maximized is significant for AI Safety, indicating a spectrum of agentic behaviors.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The formal definition of goal-directedness is a key contribution, separating it from task performance.
    *   The Blocksworld environment provides a controlled setting for systematic evaluation.
    *   The use of subtasks is a practical approach for inferring model capabilities.
    *   The experimental results provide empirical insights into the goal-directedness of several state-of-the-art models.
    *   The code and data are open-sourced, enhancing reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The Blocksworld environment, while controlled, is a simplified environment, which might limit the generalizability of the results.
    *   The assumption that performance on subtasks accurately reflects underlying capabilities is crucial and may not always hold, particularly for complex reasoning. The choice of subtasks is therefore significant, but the authors offer less justification.
    *   The reliance on *interpreting* models' actions (measuring a block implies an attempt to understand its size) may be vulnerable to Clever Hans effects (LLMs performing actions that *appear* goal-directed but aren't).
    *   The definition of what constitutes "full use" of capabilities depends on the choice of the maxπεΠ. E[R] which may be difficult to approximate well.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence the field by:
    *   Providing a framework for evaluating and comparing the goal-directedness of different LLMs.
    *   Guiding the design of safer and more controllable AI systems.
    *   Stimulating further research on the properties and behavior of LLMs as agents.

*Justification:* While the paper presents a valuable framework and interesting findings, its impact is somewhat limited by the reliance on a simplified environment and the assumptions underpinning the subtask-based capability inference. These factors restrict its broader applicability and generalizability.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ACE: Attentional Concept Erasure in Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11850v1)
- **Authors**: Finn Carter
- **Abstract**: Large text-to-image diffusion models have demonstrated remarkable image synthesis capabilities, but their indiscriminate training on Internet-scale data has led to learned concepts that enable harmful, copyrighted, or otherwise undesirable content generation. We address the task of concept erasure in diffusion models, i.e., removing a specified concept from a pre-trained model such that prompting the concept (or related synonyms) no longer yields its depiction, while preserving the model's ability to generate other content. We propose a novel method, Attentional Concept Erasure (ACE), that integrates a closed-form attention manipulation with lightweight fine-tuning. Theoretically, we formulate concept erasure as aligning the model's conditional distribution on the target concept with a neutral distribution. Our approach identifies and nullifies concept-specific latent directions in the cross-attention modules via a gated low-rank adaptation, followed by adversarially augmented fine-tuning to ensure thorough erasure of the concept and its synonyms. Empirically, we demonstrate on multiple benchmarks, including object classes, celebrity faces, explicit content, and artistic styles, that ACE achieves state-of-the-art concept removal efficacy and robustness. Compared to prior methods, ACE better balances generality (erasing concept and related terms) and specificity (preserving unrelated content), scales to dozens of concepts, and is efficient, requiring only a few seconds of adaptation per concept. We will release our code to facilitate safer deployment of diffusion models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ACE: Attentional Concept Erasure in Diffusion Models" introduces a novel method, Attentional Concept Erasure (ACE), for removing specific concepts from pre-trained text-to-image diffusion models. ACE combines a closed-form attention manipulation with lightweight fine-tuning to erase undesired concepts while preserving the model's ability to generate other content. The core idea is to identify and nullify concept-specific latent directions within the cross-attention modules of the diffusion model via a gated low-rank adaptation, followed by adversarial fine-tuning to ensure robust erasure. Experiments on various benchmarks, including object classes, celebrity faces, explicit content, and artistic styles, demonstrate that ACE achieves state-of-the-art concept removal efficacy and robustness, with improved generality, specificity, efficiency, and robustness against adversarial attacks compared to previous methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques and the theoretical grounding. While closed-form attention manipulation and fine-tuning are not entirely new, the way ACE integrates these approaches, particularly with the adversarial fine-tuning and the closed-form derivation of the gating vectors based on attention differences, is a significant improvement. The authors provide a theoretically sound formulation of concept erasure as aligning the model's conditional distribution on the target concept with a neutral distribution. The adversarial augmentation strategy specifically addresses a crucial gap in prior work (robustness to adversarial prompts), which is a strong contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a highly significant problem in the field of generative AI: mitigating the risks associated with harmful, copyrighted, or otherwise undesirable content generated by diffusion models. By providing an efficient and robust concept erasure method, ACE contributes to making these models safer and more controllable for practical deployment.  The efficiency of ACE (requiring only a few seconds of adaptation per concept) is particularly important for scalability. The demonstrated improvement in robustness against adversarial attacks is also a key advantage. The reported results showcase the effectiveness of ACE on multiple benchmarks, further solidifying its practical value.  The release of the code, as mentioned in the abstract, would greatly enhance the impact and facilitate future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across diverse benchmarks, consistently demonstrating superior performance compared to existing methods.
    *   **Robustness:** The adversarial fine-tuning component significantly enhances robustness against prompt-based attacks, addressing a crucial vulnerability in prior work.
    *   **Efficiency:** The method is highly efficient, requiring minimal retraining and enabling the simultaneous removal of dozens of concepts.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The paper provides a theoretical justification for the proposed approach, linking concept erasure to the alignment of conditional distributions.
    *   **Well-Written and Clear:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method, experimental setup, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scalability Limitations (acknowledged):** While the paper demonstrates good performance with dozens of concepts, it acknowledges diminishing returns when scaling to hundreds of concepts, pointing to a capacity issue. This limitation is discussed, but further research is needed to address it.
    *   **Reliance on External Classifiers/Heuristics:**  The reliance on external classifiers or prompt heuristics to determine the presence of a concept during fine-tuning could be a limitation for abstract concepts, as acknowledged by the authors.  A more automated or intrinsic method for identifying concept presence would improve the robustness and applicability of ACE.
    *   **Implicit biases:** The authors acknowledged they did not address the issue of implicit biases.
    *   **Lack of Detail in Derivation:** The paper mentions that the derivation of the closed-form solution is omitted for brevity. While understandable, including it in supplementary material would improve the paper's completeness and allow for better verification of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** ACE has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a practical and robust method for concept erasure in diffusion models. Its efficiency and robustness against adversarial attacks make it a valuable tool for developers and practitioners seeking to deploy safer and more controllable generative AI systems. The code release will likely encourage further research and development in this area.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of concept erasure in diffusion models. The combination of closed-form attention gating and adversarial fine-tuning, along with the theoretical grounding, is a unique and effective approach. The comprehensive experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across diverse benchmarks, including significant improvements in robustness against adversarial attacks. The efficiency of the method is also a key advantage.

However, the acknowledged scalability limitations and reliance on external classifiers/heuristics for concept detection prevent it from achieving a higher score. Addressing these limitations in future work would further enhance the practical value and impact of ACE.  Furthermore, the lack of complete detail on the derivation of the closed-form solution is a minor drawback.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Finding Flawed Fictions: Evaluating Complex Reasoning in Language Models via Plot Hole Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11900v1)
- **Authors**: Kabir Ahuja, Melanie Sclar, Yulia Tsvetkov
- **Abstract**: Stories are a fundamental aspect of human experience. Engaging deeply with stories and spotting plot holes -- inconsistencies in a storyline that break the internal logic or rules of a story's world -- requires nuanced reasoning skills, including tracking entities and events and their interplay, abstract thinking, pragmatic narrative understanding, commonsense and social reasoning, and theory of mind. As Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly generate, interpret, and modify text, rigorously assessing their narrative consistency and deeper language understanding becomes critical. However, existing benchmarks focus mainly on surface-level comprehension. In this work, we propose plot hole detection in stories as a proxy to evaluate language understanding and reasoning in LLMs. We introduce FlawedFictionsMaker, a novel algorithm to controllably and carefully synthesize plot holes in human-written stories. Using this algorithm, we construct a benchmark to evaluate LLMs' plot hole detection abilities in stories -- FlawedFictions -- , which is robust to contamination, with human filtering ensuring high quality. We find that state-of-the-art LLMs struggle in accurately solving FlawedFictions regardless of the reasoning effort allowed, with performance significantly degrading as story length increases. Finally, we show that LLM-based story summarization and story generation are prone to introducing plot holes, with more than 50% and 100% increases in plot hole detection rates with respect to human-written originals.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Finding Flawed Fictions: Evaluating Complex Reasoning in Language Models via Plot Hole Detection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel task, plot hole detection in stories, to evaluate language understanding and reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that existing benchmarks primarily focus on surface-level comprehension, failing to capture the nuanced reasoning needed for narrative understanding. To this end, they present FLAWEDFICTIONSMAKER, an algorithm to controllably synthesize plot holes in human-written stories.  Using this algorithm, they create FLAWEDFICTIONS, a benchmark to test LLMs' ability to detect these plot holes. Experiments with state-of-the-art LLMs reveal significant challenges in accurately solving the task, with performance declining as story length increases. The authors also explore the use of plot hole detection for evaluating the consistency of LLM-generated stories, finding that both summarization and contemporary adaptation tasks can introduce more plot holes than the original, human-written narratives. They make their dataset and code publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers significant novelty. The idea of using plot hole detection as a proxy for deeper language understanding is clever and addresses a clear gap in existing benchmarks. The FLAWEDFICTIONSMAKER algorithm is a novel contribution, providing a systematic way to create challenging test cases.  Furthermore, applying plot hole detection to LLM-generated content is an interesting and practically relevant application.

*   **Significance:** Assessing narrative understanding is crucial as LLMs become increasingly involved in text generation and interpretation.  The paper's findings highlight that even the most advanced LLMs struggle with narrative consistency, indicating that further improvements in reasoning and comprehension are needed. The FLAWEDFICTIONS benchmark provides a valuable tool for researchers to evaluate and drive progress in these areas.  The case study showing increased plot holes in LLM-generated content emphasizes the need for quality control mechanisms in AI-driven creative writing.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the concept of a plot hole and its relationship to deeper language understanding.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors use a systematic approach to create their benchmark, combining algorithmic generation with human verification to ensure high quality.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper evaluates a wide range of LLMs and reasoning models, providing a comprehensive assessment of current capabilities.
    *   **Practical Application:** The exploration of plot hole detection for evaluating LLM-generated stories demonstrates the real-world applicability of the task.
    *   **Open Source Resource:**  The release of the dataset and code promotes further research and development in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data Limitations:** While the algorithm introduces controlled plot holes, one could argue that naturally occurring inconsistencies in professionally written stories might be qualitatively different and pose unique challenges for LLMs. A fully curated dataset of real-world plot holes could be more valuable, but also more challenging to create.
    *   **Story Complexity:** The analysis focuses on short stories, the findings and the ability to find plot holes may be more easily find due to their size. LLMs may continue to improve but could be challenging to evaluate due to longer and more complex storylines.
    *   **Annotation Subjectivity:** While human verification is conducted, the concept of a "plot hole" can be subjective, and different annotators might have varying interpretations. A more detailed analysis of inter-annotator agreement could strengthen the paper.
    *   **LLM Improvement:** With the rapid pace that AI is improving, future improvements could be much better and may outdate the results of this paper. This paper must have been reviewed before new LLM models came out, such as GPT4-Turbo

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Motivating the development of new LLM architectures and training methods specifically designed for narrative understanding.
    *   Inspiring researchers to create more challenging and realistic benchmarks for language understanding.
    *   Providing a framework for evaluating the quality and consistency of LLM-generated content.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed study with clear contributions to the field. The introduction of the plot hole detection task and the FLAWEDFICTIONS benchmark are significant advancements. While the synthetic nature of the data and some aspects of the methodology present limitations, the overall impact of the work warrants a high score. Furthermore, given the pace of change in the AI field, this paper may be outdated and need to be constantly refreshed.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Rethinking the Generation of High-Quality CoT Data from the Perspective of LLM-Adaptive Question Difficulty Grading
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11919v1)
- **Authors**: Qianjin Yu, Keyu Wu, Zihan Chen, Chushu Zhang, Manlin Mei, Lingjun Huang, Fang Tan, Yongsheng Du, Kunlin Liu, Yurui Zhu
- **Abstract**: Recently, DeepSeek-R1 (671B) (DeepSeek-AIet al., 2025) has demonstrated its excellent reasoning ability in complex tasks and has publiclyshared its methodology. This provides potentially high-quality chain-of-thought (CoT) data for stimulating the reasoning abilities of small-sized large language models (LLMs). To generate high-quality CoT data for different LLMs, we seek an efficient method for generating high-quality CoT data with LLM-Adaptive questiondifficulty levels. First, we grade the difficulty of the questions according to the reasoning ability of the LLMs themselves and construct a LLM-Adaptive question database. Second, we sample the problem database based on a distribution of difficulty levels of the questions and then use DeepSeek-R1 (671B) (DeepSeek-AI et al., 2025) to generate the corresponding high-quality CoT data with correct answers. Thanks to the construction of CoT data with LLM-Adaptive difficulty levels, we have significantly reduced the cost of data generation and enhanced the efficiency of model supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Finally, we have validated the effectiveness and generalizability of the proposed method in the fields of complex mathematical competitions and code generation tasks. Notably, with only 2k high-quality mathematical CoT data, our ZMath-32B surpasses DeepSeek-Distill-32B in math reasoning task. Similarly, with only 2k high-quality code CoT data, our ZCode-32B surpasses DeepSeek-Distill-32B in code reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method for generating high-quality chain-of-thought (CoT) data tailored to specific large language models (LLMs).  The approach, termed "LLM-Adaptive Question Difficulty Grading," involves: 1) grading question difficulty based on the reasoning capabilities of the target LLM; 2) constructing a question database categorized by these difficulty levels; 3) sampling questions from this database according to a defined distribution; and 4) leveraging a powerful LLM (DeepSeek-R1) to generate high-quality CoT data for the sampled questions. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach in enhancing the reasoning abilities of smaller LLMs in mathematical reasoning and code generation tasks. They show that fine-tuning smaller models on this adaptively generated CoT data significantly improves their performance compared to those trained on data without adaptive difficulty grading.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adapting CoT data difficulty to match the capabilities of the target LLM has some novelty.  While curriculum learning and difficulty-based sampling are established techniques, their specific application to CoT data generation with a focus on aligning data difficulty with *target* LLM capacity is a valuable extension.  It moves beyond static difficulty labeling or relying solely on proxy metrics (e.g., question length).

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in the field: efficiently training smaller LLMs to achieve strong reasoning performance.  The high computational cost of training very large models makes them impractical for many applications. Therefore, methods that enable smaller models to exhibit improved reasoning capabilities are highly valuable. The demonstrated improvements over existing baselines, even with limited data (2k samples), highlight the potential of this approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitation of existing CoT data generation methods, which often fail to account for the target LLM's specific reasoning abilities.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The proposed approach is well-structured and described in detail, making it relatively easy to understand and reproduce.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The authors provide experimental results in mathematical reasoning and code generation, demonstrating the effectiveness of their approach across different tasks.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The inclusion of ablation studies strengthens the claims by isolating the contribution of each component (difficulty grading method, distribution transfer, training data size). These are vital for understanding the impact of different choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Single Teacher Model:** The method relies heavily on DeepSeek-R1 for CoT data generation. While this allows for high-quality data, it also introduces potential bias from the DeepSeek-R1 model.  Exploring multiple teacher models or using ensemble methods could enhance the robustness of the approach.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Distribution Sampling:** Although the paper compares two distribution strategies, a more thorough investigation of different sampling techniques and their impact on performance would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Comparison to other CoT generation methods:** The paper does not provide direct comparison to current methods (like curriculum learning or rejection sampling methods specific to generating CoT) applied with the same budget. A direct comparison to alternative CoT generation methods, considering data generation cost and downstream task performance, would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Generalization to more tasks:** Testing the method on a more diverse set of tasks and datasets would help assess its generalizability beyond math and coding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in CoT data generation, particularly for scenarios where computational resources are limited. The LLM-Adaptive Difficulty Grading approach offers a promising direction for efficiently training smaller LLMs to achieve strong reasoning performance.  The results could encourage researchers to focus on more targeted and adaptive data generation strategies.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a valuable contribution with a clear methodology and empirical validation. However, the limitations regarding reliance on a single teacher model, limited exploration of distribution sampling, and narrow comparison to other CoT generation methods, and the lack of evidence for broad generalization, prevent it from receiving a higher score.

### SemDiff: Generating Natural Unrestricted Adversarial Examples via Semantic Attributes Optimization in Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11923v1)
- **Authors**: Zeyu Dai, Shengcai Liu, Rui He, Jiahao Wu, Ning Lu, Wenqi Fan, Qing Li, Ke Tang
- **Abstract**: Unrestricted adversarial examples (UAEs), allow the attacker to create non-constrained adversarial examples without given clean samples, posing a severe threat to the safety of deep learning models. Recent works utilize diffusion models to generate UAEs. However, these UAEs often lack naturalness and imperceptibility due to simply optimizing in intermediate latent noises. In light of this, we propose SemDiff, a novel unrestricted adversarial attack that explores the semantic latent space of diffusion models for meaningful attributes, and devises a multi-attributes optimization approach to ensure attack success while maintaining the naturalness and imperceptibility of generated UAEs. We perform extensive experiments on four tasks on three high-resolution datasets, including CelebA-HQ, AFHQ and ImageNet. The results demonstrate that SemDiff outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of attack success rate and imperceptibility. The generated UAEs are natural and exhibit semantically meaningful changes, in accord with the attributes' weights. In addition, SemDiff is found capable of evading different defenses, which further validates its effectiveness and threatening.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SemDiff: Generating Natural Unrestricted Adversarial Examples via Semantic Attributes Optimization in Diffusion Models" addresses the problem of generating unrestricted adversarial examples (UAEs) – adversarial examples that don't rely on clean input samples and have no specific perturbation constraints.  The paper argues that existing diffusion model-based UAE generation methods suffer from unnatural-looking artifacts because they directly optimize intermediate latent noises that lack high-level semantic information. SemDiff proposes a novel approach that explores the semantic latent space within diffusion models to identify meaningful attributes and then optimizes the weights of these attributes to generate adversarial examples that are both effective at fooling target models and visually realistic with meaningful semantic changes. They achieve this by training semantic functions to learn class-sensitive adversarial attributes. A multi-attribute optimization approach is proposed to ensure attack success while preserving naturalness.  Extensive experiments on multiple high-resolution datasets and tasks demonstrate that SemDiff outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of attack success rate and imperceptibility (measured by metrics like BRISQUE, FID, and KID), while also showing resilience against common defenses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach by focusing on the semantic latent space within diffusion models rather than directly manipulating the intermediate latent noises used in prior work.  The idea of learning and optimizing adversarial *attributes* is a significant departure and a potential improvement for generating more realistic and controllable adversarial examples. The multi-attribute optimization with a penalty term is also a good addition to prevent excessive changes. However, the underlying diffusion model techniques are not new, and the use of CLIP guidance, while effective, is becoming a fairly standard technique in this domain. The innovation resides in the *specific application and combination* of these elements for the task of UAE generation.

*   **Significance:**  The generation of high-quality, unrestricted adversarial examples is a relevant problem, as it exposes vulnerabilities in deep learning models that are not captured by traditional, perturbation-based attacks. If the proposed method does produce more realistic and semantic meaningful UAEs, then it poses a higher threat to the safety of deep learning models in real-world scenarios.  Furthermore, the ability to generate UAEs without needing clean samples is valuable in data-scarce or sensitive domains (e.g., medical imaging). However, the practical significance may be limited as most existing and potential adversarial attacks can be mitigated through ensemble adversarial training and other techniques. The method can further provide insights into the robustness and explainability of DNNs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The method is well-motivated, clearly explaining the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets, tasks, and evaluation metrics.
    *   The results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of SemDiff over baseline methods, particularly in terms of imperceptibility and naturalness.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of different components of the proposed method.
    *   The analysis of hyperparameters offers guidance for practical application.
    * The work is open-sourced.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper relies on established techniques like diffusion models and CLIP guidance, diminishing the overall technical depth and core novelty.
    *   Although the paper provides visual comparisons, a more rigorous perceptual study with human evaluators could further strengthen the claims regarding naturalness and imperceptibility.
    *   The computational cost of training semantic functions and performing multi-attribute optimization is not thoroughly discussed.  This could be a limiting factor for applications on large datasets or complex models.
    * While they show some defense evasions, a more systematic study of different defense techniques would add more weight to the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's focus on semantic manipulation for UAE generation could influence future research in adversarial attacks and defenses.  It could inspire the development of more robust defenses that are less susceptible to semantic-level perturbations.  The method itself could be used as a tool for evaluating and improving the robustness of deep learning models in realistic scenarios.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of adversarial machine learning by offering a novel approach to generating unrestricted adversarial examples that overcomes some of the limitations of previous methods. While it relies on established techniques to a certain extent, the specific combination and application of these elements to address the problem of UAE generation is novel. The experimental results are convincing and support the claims made in the paper. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses. Therefore, a score of 7 is assigned.

**Score: 7**

- **Score**: 7/10

### An LLM-as-a-judge Approach for Scalable Gender-Neutral Translation Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11934v1)
- **Authors**: Andrea Piergentili, Beatrice Savoldi, Matteo Negri, Luisa Bentivogli
- **Abstract**: Gender-neutral translation (GNT) aims to avoid expressing the gender of human referents when the source text lacks explicit cues about the gender of those referents. Evaluating GNT automatically is particularly challenging, with current solutions being limited to monolingual classifiers. Such solutions are not ideal because they do not factor in the source sentence and require dedicated data and fine-tuning to scale to new languages. In this work, we address such limitations by investigating the use of large language models (LLMs) as evaluators of GNT. Specifically, we explore two prompting approaches: one in which LLMs generate sentence-level assessments only, and another, akin to a chain-of-thought approach, where they first produce detailed phrase-level annotations before a sentence-level judgment. Through extensive experiments on multiple languages with five models, both open and proprietary, we show that LLMs can serve as evaluators of GNT. Moreover, we find that prompting for phrase-level annotations before sentence-level assessments consistently improves the accuracy of all models, providing a better and more scalable alternative to current solutions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, incorporating the requested rigorousness and justification:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as judges for evaluating gender-neutral translation (GNT). It addresses the challenge of automatically evaluating GNT, which is complex due to the varied valid neutralization strategies. The authors investigate two prompting approaches: (1) direct sentence-level assessment and (2) a chain-of-thought approach involving phrase-level annotations followed by sentence-level judgement. They experiment with several open and proprietary LLMs in Italian, Spanish, and German. The key finding is that LLMs can effectively evaluate GNT, and that incorporating phrase-level annotations before sentence-level judgements significantly improves evaluation accuracy. This offers a more scalable alternative to existing solutions which often rely on dedicated, monolingual classifiers trained with task-specific data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of using LLMs as evaluators (LLM-as-a-Judge) isn't entirely new, its application to the specific and nuanced task of GNT evaluation is novel. Previous work has explored LLMs for general MT evaluation and gender bias identification, but this paper directly addresses the unique challenges of *evaluating* the effectiveness of neutralization strategies in translation, a task that requires understanding subtle semantic and grammatical cues. The distinction between using LLMs to evaluate general translation quality *versus* evaluating if gender bias has been successfully neutralized is significant.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the practical limitations of existing GNT evaluation methods. Current approaches often require dedicated training data for each language and don't adequately consider the source sentence context, which makes scaling them difficult.  The LLM-based approach presented here offers a more scalable and adaptable solution, potentially accelerating research and development in GNT by enabling faster and cheaper evaluation.  It also provides a crucial bridge between complex neutralization strategies, from single word substitutions to complex restructurings, and more traditional methods of translation evaluation. This is an area of critical importance for improving MT systems and fostering fairer, more inclusive technologies.  As MT becomes increasingly integrated into sensitive domains, evaluation strategies that don't assess biases will perpetuate harms.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Research Questions:**  The paper clearly articulates research questions (RQ1 and RQ2) related to LLM applicability and the impact of intermediate annotation steps.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimentation:** The study involves multiple languages, several LLMs (both open and closed source), and a carefully designed set of prompts. The comparison of different prompting strategies is a strength.
    *   **Clear Results and Analysis:** The results are presented in a clear and well-organized manner, with supporting figures and tables. The analysis and discussion effectively interpret the findings and link them back to the research questions.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The proposed method has direct practical implications for GNT research and system development.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors release their code and data (including prompt examples) enabling reproducibility and extension of their work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Bias Considered:** The paper primarily focuses on binary gender representation and doesn't delve into more nuanced aspects of gender inclusivity, such as non-binary identities, which are crucial considerations in broader inclusivity efforts. The paper acknowledges this in its bias statement and limitations.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Acceptability:** While the evaluation focuses on gender neutrality, it could benefit from a deeper assessment of the *acceptability* and fluency of the generated neutral translations. A neutral translation is only useful if it is also natural and grammatically correct. The acceptability is particularly important since some neutral strategies can be complex reformulations, potentially impacting the naturalness of the sentence.
    *   **Limited Comparison with State-of-the-Art in General MT Evaluation:** While the paper rightly highlights the limitations of standard MT metrics in the context of GNT, it would be stronger with a more detailed comparison against a broader range of state-of-the-art MT evaluation metrics. Even though these metrics penalize gendered output when not needed, it’s important to contextualize the LLM-based evaluation within the wider evaluation landscape.
    *   **Dependence on LLM Performance:** The approach's performance is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. Future work needs to investigate the robustness of the approach across different LLM architectures, fine-tuning strategies, and potential biases within the LLMs themselves.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of gender-inclusive natural language processing, specifically machine translation. Its practical focus on evaluation is important, as reliable evaluation is necessary for further advancement. The scalability and cross-linguality of the LLM-based approach are significant advantages. Although there are some weaknesses, these are clearly identified and discussed in the paper as directions for future research. While it leverages the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm, it applies it in a novel way, and offers a significant advantage over previously employed methods. It is important and well-executed.

### Mind2Matter: Creating 3D Models from EEG Signals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11936v1)
- **Authors**: Xia Deng, Shen Chen, Jiale Zhou, Lei Li
- **Abstract**: The reconstruction of 3D objects from brain signals has gained significant attention in brain-computer interface (BCI) research. Current research predominantly utilizes functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) for 3D reconstruction tasks due to its excellent spatial resolution. Nevertheless, the clinical utility of fMRI is limited by its prohibitive costs and inability to support real-time operations. In comparison, electroencephalography (EEG) presents distinct advantages as an affordable, non-invasive, and mobile solution for real-time brain-computer interaction systems. While recent advances in deep learning have enabled remarkable progress in image generation from neural data, decoding EEG signals into structured 3D representations remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we propose a novel framework that translates EEG recordings into 3D object reconstructions by leveraging neural decoding techniques and generative models. Our approach involves training an EEG encoder to extract spatiotemporal visual features, fine-tuning a large language model to interpret these features into descriptive multimodal outputs, and leveraging generative 3D Gaussians with layout-guided control to synthesize the final 3D structures. Experiments demonstrate that our model captures salient geometric and semantic features, paving the way for applications in brain-computer interfaces (BCIs), virtual reality, and neuroprosthetics.Our code is available in https://github.com/sddwwww/Mind2Matter.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Mind2Matter: Creating 3D Models from EEG Signals" introduces a novel framework for reconstructing 3D objects from EEG recordings.  The framework consists of two stages. First, a trained EEG encoder extracts spatiotemporal features from the EEG signals, which are then fed into a fine-tuned large language model (LLM) to generate a textual description of the scene.  Second, this textual description is used to generate a 3D scene using generative 3D Gaussians with layout-guided control.  The paper demonstrates that the model can capture geometric and semantic features from EEG signals and produce plausible 3D reconstructions, opening possibilities for brain-computer interfaces, virtual reality, and neuroprosthetics.  The code is made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper introduces a novel combination of techniques for EEG-based 3D reconstruction. The two-stage approach using an EEG encoder, LLM for scene description, and 3D Gaussian splatting is a unique pipeline. Most previous work either focuses on fMRI or only produces 2D images from EEG. The layout-guided control for 3D Gaussian generation based on textual descriptions is also a valuable contribution. The adaptive margin mechanism on the contrastive loss is potentially interesting for cross-modal alignment.
*   **Weaknesses:**  While the integration is novel, the individual components (EEG encoders, LLMs, 3D Gaussians) are established technologies. The primary novelty lies in the specific architecture and training process for this particular task.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:**  Successfully demonstrating 3D reconstruction from EEG signals is significant because EEG is cheaper, more portable, and has better temporal resolution than fMRI. This opens up possibilities for real-time applications. The ability to translate brain activity into understandable visual representations has strong potential impact for BCIs, VR, and neuroprosthetics.
*   **Weaknesses:**  The quantitative results, while showing improvement over baselines, are still modest. The 3D reconstructions are likely not high-fidelity or precisely controllable in the same way as methods that use direct manipulation or richer input data. The dataset used in the experiment is relatively small.

**Strengths and Weaknesses Breakdown**
* **Strengths:**
    * Novel integration of EEG decoding, LLM for text interpretation, and 3D generation
    * Two-stage appraoch to reconstruction that enhances the spatial and semantic context, producing a coherent and robust model.
    * Addresses cost and mobility limitations of fMRI-based reconstructions
* **Weaknesses:**
    * Limited spatial resolution of EEG presents challenges in generating high-fidelity 3D models
    * Potential errors from EEG signal extraction can propagate from the textual analysis phase to the final 3D rendering.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to stimulate further research in EEG-based 3D reconstruction.  The open-source code will allow others to build upon this work.  Improvements in EEG encoding, LLM-based scene understanding, and 3D generative models could significantly improve the quality and applicability of this type of system.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** because while the paper presents a novel and potentially impactful approach, it also has certain limitations. The novelty lies primarily in the specific combination of techniques rather than groundbreaking advances in any single area. The quantitative results, while positive, suggest there is much room for improvement. Furthermore, the limited scale of the dataset and the qualitative results showcasing some artifacts indicate areas for future refinement. Overall, this is a promising initial study with significant potential to advance the field but requires more mature investigation before it could be considered a truly outstanding contribution.

### R-Meshfusion: Reinforcement Learning Powered Sparse-View Mesh Reconstruction with Diffusion Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11946v1)
- **Authors**: Haoyang Wang, Liming Liu, Peiheng Wang, Junlin Hao, Jiangkai Wu, Xinggong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Mesh reconstruction from multi-view images is a fundamental problem in computer vision, but its performance degrades significantly under sparse-view conditions, especially in unseen regions where no ground-truth observations are available. While recent advances in diffusion models have demonstrated strong capabilities in synthesizing novel views from limited inputs, their outputs often suffer from visual artifacts and lack 3D consistency, posing challenges for reliable mesh optimization. In this paper, we propose a novel framework that leverages diffusion models to enhance sparse-view mesh reconstruction in a principled and reliable manner. To address the instability of diffusion outputs, we propose a Consensus Diffusion Module that filters unreliable generations via interquartile range (IQR) analysis and performs variance-aware image fusion to produce robust pseudo-supervision. Building on this, we design an online reinforcement learning strategy based on the Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) to adaptively select the most informative viewpoints for enhancement, guided by diffusion loss. Finally, the fused images are used to jointly supervise a NeRF-based model alongside sparse-view ground truth, ensuring consistency across both geometry and appearance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves significant improvements in both geometric quality and rendering quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the R-Meshfusion paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces R-Meshfusion, a novel framework for sparse-view mesh reconstruction.  It tackles the challenges of reconstructing high-fidelity 3D meshes from limited viewpoints, particularly in unseen regions, by leveraging the strengths of diffusion models and reinforcement learning. The key contributions include:

1.  **Consensus Diffusion Module:** Filters unreliable diffusion outputs using interquartile range analysis and performs variance-aware image fusion to create robust pseudo-supervision.
2.  **UCB-based Viewpoint Selection:**  An online reinforcement learning strategy based on the Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) adaptively selects informative viewpoints to guide training, guided by diffusion loss.
3.  **Joint NeRF-based Optimization:** Integrates the synthesized views into a NeRF-based training pipeline, jointly optimizing geometry and appearance while enforcing multi-view consistency.

The authors demonstrate improved geometric and rendering quality compared to existing methods through extensive experiments.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:*

The paper exhibits reasonable novelty in its approach. While using diffusion models and NeRFs individually isn't new, the way R-Meshfusion combines these components with the Consensus Diffusion Module and the UCB-based viewpoint selection is a novel contribution. The Consensus Diffusion Module is a valuable addition to address the instability of diffusion-based reconstruction techniques. The online RL strategy to select viewpoints in this context appears original.
The use of diffusion to generate additional views for sparse reconstruction is novel, as is the UCB viewpoint selection strategy. The approach of having a consensus diffusion module coupled with UCB is good to manage the randomness of diffusion outputs. This allows them to better use the knowledge they have.

*Significance:*

The significance of this work lies in addressing the practical problem of mesh reconstruction from sparse views. Many real-world applications suffer from limited or constrained viewpoints, and R-Meshfusion offers a potential solution to overcome these limitations. The improvements in both geometric and rendering quality are tangible, suggesting the framework could impact areas like virtual reality, robotics, and cultural heritage preservation.

*Strengths:*

*   **Principled Framework:** The integration of diffusion priors and reinforcement learning is well-motivated and grounded in solid theoretical principles.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The framework addresses multiple challenges, from diffusion instability to viewpoint selection and 3D consistency.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The authors provide convincing experimental results demonstrating improvements over existing methods.
*   **Well Written and Organized:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to understand the methodology and contributions.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:**  The method depends on a pre-trained conditioned diffusion model, which might limit its adaptability to different datasets or scenarios. It would be better if the entire architecture was trained end to end.
*   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models can be computationally expensive, and the Consensus Diffusion Module, which involves generating multiple diffusion outputs, might add to the overall cost. The paper lacks the specific runtime measurements.
*   **Limited View-Dependent Effects:** It admits to have limited view dependent effects, and inability to capture some effects like transparency.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence future research in sparse-view mesh reconstruction and diffusion-model-based 3D reconstruction. The Consensus Diffusion Module and the UCB-based viewpoint selection strategy could be adopted or adapted by other researchers in the field. The work demonstrates the feasibility of combining diffusion models with reinforcement learning for improved 3D reconstruction.

*Justification for Score:*

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of sparse-view mesh reconstruction. The technical approach is sound, and the experimental results are compelling.  The weaknesses mentioned do not outweigh the strengths of the paper. The approach fills an important gap in 3D reconstruction, addressing the need for robust performance under sparse-view conditions. It presents a good combination of UCB and diffusion models to produce better reconstructions.

### Novel-view X-ray Projection Synthesis through Geometry-Integrated Deep Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11953v1)
- **Authors**: Daiqi Liu, Fuxin Fan, Andreas Maier
- **Abstract**: X-ray imaging plays a crucial role in the medical field, providing essential insights into the internal anatomy of patients for diagnostics, image-guided procedures, and clinical decision-making. Traditional techniques often require multiple X-ray projections from various angles to obtain a comprehensive view, leading to increased radiation exposure and more complex clinical processes. This paper explores an innovative approach using the DL-GIPS model, which synthesizes X-ray projections from new viewpoints by leveraging a single existing projection. The model strategically manipulates geometry and texture features extracted from an initial projection to match new viewing angles. It then synthesizes the final projection by merging these modified geometry features with consistent texture information through an advanced image generation process. We demonstrate the effectiveness and broad applicability of the DL-GIPS framework through lung imaging examples, highlighting its potential to revolutionize stereoscopic and volumetric imaging by minimizing the need for extensive data acquisition.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Novel-View X-Ray Projection Synthesis Through Geometry-Integrated Deep Learning":

**Summary:**

This paper presents the DL-GIPS (Geometry-Integrated Projection Synthesis) framework for generating novel-view X-ray projections from a single or multiple existing projections.  The core idea involves disentangling geometry and texture features from the input X-ray, transforming the geometry features according to the desired target view angle, and then generating the new X-ray image by combining the transformed geometry with the original texture features using deep learning techniques. The model utilizes separate encoders for geometry and texture, a 3D feature refinement module to address sparsity issues, and adversarial training to enhance realism. The authors validated the framework on a lung imaging dataset (LIDC-IDRI), demonstrating its effectiveness in both one-to-one and multi-to-multi projection synthesis scenarios, and show it outperforms a U-Net baseline. The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevance:** X-ray imaging is a vital medical tool, and reducing radiation exposure through view synthesis is a significant goal.
    *   **Clear Approach:** The separation of geometry and texture features, and the use of geometric transformations to achieve view changes, is a sound approach that aligns well with the physics of X-ray imaging.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The paper presents quantitative results using standard metrics (MAE, RMSE, SSIM, PSNR) and compares against a U-Net baseline, providing evidence of the method's performance.
    *   **Public Code:** Releasing the code enhances reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon the work.
    *   **Addresses Sparsity:** Including a 3D feature refinement model is a good step to reduce artifacts from single-view inputs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The core idea of disentangling features and transforming geometry isn't entirely novel *in the broader field of image synthesis*. The innovation lies in applying this approach to X-ray imaging, where the physics and data characteristics are unique.  However, previous work exists in X-ray view synthesis. The improvements to the DL-GIPS from the original paper is not stated beyond "reproducing and optimizing" the network.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While LIDC-IDRI is a well-known dataset, more substantial clinical datasets would strengthen the validation.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The high inference time of DL-GIPS compared to UNet (a factor of ~14x) is a significant limitation for real-world clinical applicability. The authors acknowledge this issue but don't offer a solution in the paper. This drastically limits the applications in which this can be used.
    *   **Lack of In-depth Analysis:** The paper presents results but lacks a deeper analysis of the failure cases. Where does the method struggle? What specific anatomical structures are problematic?  Visual inspection of the results in Figures 2 and 3 show that the DL-GIPS results still do not contain the same level of quality as the ground truth.
    *   **Limited Clinical Validation:** The paper doesn't explore the clinical utility of the synthesized images. Are radiologists able to accurately diagnose conditions using the synthesized images?
    *   **Baseline Choice:** Comparing to U-Net is not optimal because U-Net is a segmentation network and not designed for view synthesis. A more appropriate view synthesis baseline would have better served the paper.
*   **Significance:** The paper presents a promising technique for X-ray view synthesis, which *could* contribute to reducing radiation exposure and improving clinical workflows. However, the practical significance is limited by the computational cost and lack of clinical validation. If the computational cost is drastically reduced and a comprehensive clinical validation conducted, the work could become highly influential.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to inspire further research in the area of X-ray view synthesis, particularly focusing on improving the computational efficiency and clinical utility of deep learning models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has some merit in that it addresses a relevant problem and presents a reasonable approach. However, the limited novelty, computational cost, lack of strong clinical validation, and reliance on a simple baseline prevents a high score. Given that the core concept has been explored in other image domains, the contribution is incremental rather than groundbreaking.
