### Selective Demonstration Retrieval for Improved Implicit Hate Speech Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12082v1)
- **Authors**: Yumin Kim, Hwanhee Lee
- **Abstract**: Hate speech detection is a crucial area of research in natural language processing, essential for ensuring online community safety. However, detecting implicit hate speech, where harmful intent is conveyed in subtle or indirect ways, remains a major challenge. Unlike explicit hate speech, implicit expressions often depend on context, cultural subtleties, and hidden biases, making them more challenging to identify consistently. Additionally, the interpretation of such speech is influenced by external knowledge and demographic biases, resulting in varied detection results across different language models. Furthermore, Large Language Models often show heightened sensitivity to toxic language and references to vulnerable groups, which can lead to misclassifications. This over-sensitivity results in false positives (incorrectly identifying harmless statements as hateful) and false negatives (failing to detect genuinely harmful content). Addressing these issues requires methods that not only improve detection precision but also reduce model biases and enhance robustness. To address these challenges, we propose a novel method, which utilizes in-context learning without requiring model fine-tuning. By adaptively retrieving demonstrations that focus on similar groups or those with the highest similarity scores, our approach enhances contextual comprehension. Experimental results show that our method outperforms current state-of-the-art techniques. Implementation details and code are available at TBD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of detecting implicit hate speech, which is subtle and often context-dependent, making it difficult for even advanced language models to identify consistently. The authors propose a novel method called Adaptive Retrieval-based In-context Learning (ARIIHA). ARIIHA adaptively retrieves demonstrations for in-context learning, without requiring model fine-tuning. It enhances contextual comprehension by prioritizing demonstrations focusing on similar target groups or those with high similarity scores. The method dynamically determines the optimal retrieval strategy using thresholds derived from similarity scores and the model's reliance on shortcut cues. Experiments on the Implicit Hate Corpus (IHC) dataset show that ARIIHA outperforms existing state-of-the-art techniques. Ablation and case studies further demonstrate the approach's effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the adaptive retrieval mechanism that combines target-group prioritization with similarity-based retrieval, dynamically selecting the best approach based on threshold testing. This adaptive approach addresses the limitations of purely similarity-based methods and those overly focused on target groups. The inclusion of shortcut cue detection for thresholding is also a unique aspect. It is not clear, however, the novelty of leveraging BM25 in this scenario.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a crucial problem: the detection of implicit hate speech, which is both pervasive and difficult to address. Over-sensitivity and dataset subjectivity are well-known challenges. The proposed method demonstrates promising results, achieving higher accuracy than existing methods in a few-shot setting without fine-tuning, which is appealing. However, the dataset used, the IHC, may not be as well-known or broadly used as other hate speech datasets (e.g., HateXplain), which limits the immediate impact of the results.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Adaptive Approach:** The core idea of adaptively selecting demonstrations is well-motivated and addresses a genuine problem.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct ablation studies and case studies to analyze the contribution of individual components.
    *   **Improved Performance:** The method consistently outperforms the baselines, showing that the adaptive retrieval mechanism is effective.
    *   **Mitigation of Over-Sensitivity:** By adapting the retrieval strategy, the approach avoids being overly sensitive to toxic words, reducing false positives.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Choice:**  While the IHC dataset is relevant, its relative obscurity compared to other datasets makes it harder to directly compare the results with many existing studies.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Claim:** The experiments are limited to a single dataset. The generalizability of the method to other datasets and languages is not established.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The adaptive selection mechanism likely incurs additional computational overhead.  This is not clearly discussed or quantified. While the method avoids *fine-tuning*, retrieval can still be expensive, especially for larger datasets.
    *   **Dependence on Target Group Prediction:**  The target-prioritized retrieval relies on accurate target group prediction. Errors in this stage could significantly affect the performance, as the paper acknowledges. More discussion of the impact of this prediction step is needed.
    *   **BM25 Novelty:** The paper did not clearly address how using BM25 in this specific situation is considered novel when BM25 is a decades-old algorithm.
    *   **Limited Discussion on the Shortcut Detection:** More elaboration on the methodology to extract and detect these shortcut cues would be interesting.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and adaptable hate speech detection systems. The adaptive retrieval mechanism could be a valuable addition to existing in-context learning frameworks. However, the limitations above restrict the immediate practical impact.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to address the challenges of implicit hate speech detection. The adaptive retrieval mechanism is a significant contribution. However, the limitations related to dataset choice, a need for more generalizability analyses, dependence on target group prediction accuracy, and the limited discussion of computational cost and BM25 limit the overall impact and justifies a score of 6.5. A higher score would require evaluation on more widely used datasets, analysis of computational efficiency, and more detailed explanation around the shortcut cue detection process.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Reasoning-Based AI for Startup Evaluation (R.A.I.S.E.): A Memory-Augmented, Multi-Step Decision Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12090v1)
- **Authors**: Jack Preuveneers, Joseph Ternasky, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: We present a novel framework that bridges the gap between the interpretability of decision trees and the advanced reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to predict startup success. Our approach leverages chain-of-thought prompting to generate detailed reasoning logs, which are subsequently distilled into structured, human-understandable logical rules. The pipeline integrates multiple enhancements - efficient data ingestion, a two-step refinement process, ensemble candidate sampling, simulated reinforcement learning scoring, and persistent memory - to ensure both stable decision-making and transparent output. Experimental evaluations on curated startup datasets demonstrate that our combined pipeline improves precision by 54% from 0.225 to 0.346 and accuracy by 50% from 0.46 to 0.70 compared to a standalone OpenAI o3 model. Notably, our model achieves over 2x the precision of a random classifier (16%). By combining state-of-the-art AI reasoning with explicit rule-based explanations, our method not only augments traditional decision-making processes but also facilitates expert intervention and continuous policy refinement. This work lays the foundation for the implementation of interpretable LLM-powered decision frameworks in high-stakes investment environments and other domains that require transparent and data-driven insights.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R.A.I.S.E. (Reasoning-Based AI for Startup Evaluation), a novel framework that aims to combine the interpretability of decision trees with the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) for predicting startup success.  The framework uses chain-of-thought prompting to generate reasoning logs, distills these logs into structured rules, and incorporates several enhancements, including efficient data ingestion, a two-step refinement process, ensemble candidate sampling, simulated reinforcement learning scoring, and persistent memory. The authors claim the framework improves prediction accuracy and provides human-understandable explanations, facilitating expert intervention and policy refinement.  They present experimental results on startup datasets comparing their approach to a standalone OpenAI model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining LLM reasoning with explicit rule extraction is interesting, but not entirely novel. Prior work, as acknowledged in the literature review (e.g., GPTREE), has explored similar hybrid approaches. The novelty lies primarily in the *specific combination* of enhancements, such as the simulated RL scoring and persistent conversational memory, and their application to the startup evaluation domain. The authors also emphasize modular design for the frameworks.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of the work is significant if it delivers on its promise of interpretable and accurate startup evaluation.  This could have practical implications for venture capital firms and other investors. However, the significance is somewhat limited by the scale of the evaluation. The experiments are performed on a balanced dataset with a relatively small test set (60 founders). While this is sufficient for comparing the different model variants, it's not enough to convincingly demonstrate the real-world applicability of the framework. While the increased performance compared to simple LLM model seems promising, more significant testing with real-world, larger and more heterogeneous data set with diverse founders is required. Furthermore, the improvement of the precision is noteworthy, but still relatively low. Furthermore, there is only a limited set of examples included for the 40 mini, indicating that the main strength of the framework is its ability to work well with more advanced LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The framework addresses a real-world problem with potential economic value.
    *   The integration of multiple enhancements is well-described.
    *   The emphasis on interpretability and explainability is crucial in the context of high-stakes investment decisions.
    *   The modular design facilitates future extensions and improvements.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited in scale and diversity.
    *   The performance improvements, while present, aren't drastically large in some cases.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the limitations of each enhancement and the trade-offs involved.
    *   Lack of comparison with traditional machine learning models (e.g., logistic regression or random forest using carefully engineered features) makes it difficult to assess the true advantage of LLM-based approach.
    *   The specific prompts and configurations used for the LLMs are not provided for reproducibility and external verification.
    *   Cost and token use remains a challenging factor.
    *   There are only limited details on how the RL-based scoring actually works.
    *   A more thorough discussion on why the individual components where chosen this way.
    *   Details on the failure modes.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on interpretable AI for high-stakes decision-making. It may inspire the development of similar hybrid frameworks in other domains, incorporating domain expertise and iterative refinement. The emphasis on modularity and explainability could also encourage the adoption of more transparent AI systems.

*   **Score:** 6/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution by exploring a practical application of LLMs with a focus on interpretability. However, the impact is tempered by the incremental novelty (given existing hybrid approaches), limited experimental evaluation, and lack of comparison with strong baseline models. The performance improvement of the proposed model is decent, but not extraordinary, although it might be due to the nature of the current LLM model. While the paper demonstrates a sound concept, its significance is partially limited by these factors. The current implementation of RL scoring could be extended further to improve the performance of the model further. Future research could explore these gaps and make the framework more robust and widely applicable.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Gauging Overprecision in LLMs: An Empirical Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12098v1)
- **Authors**: Adil Bahaj, Hamed Rahimi, Mohamed Chetouani, Mounir Ghogho
- **Abstract**: Recently, overconfidence in large language models (LLMs) has garnered considerable attention due to its fundamental importance in quantifying the trustworthiness of LLM generation. However, existing approaches prompt the \textit{black box LLMs} to produce their confidence (\textit{verbalized confidence}), which can be subject to many biases and hallucinations. Inspired by a different aspect of overconfidence in cognitive science called \textit{overprecision}, we designed a framework for its study in black box LLMs. This framework contains three main phases: 1) generation, 2) refinement and 3) evaluation. In the generation phase we prompt the LLM to generate answers to numerical questions in the form of intervals with a certain level of confidence. This confidence level is imposed in the prompt and not required for the LLM to generate as in previous approaches. We use various prompting techniques and use the same prompt multiple times to gauge the effects of randomness in the generation process. In the refinement phase, answers from the previous phase are refined to generate better answers. The LLM answers are evaluated and studied in the evaluation phase to understand its internal workings. This study allowed us to gain various insights into LLM overprecision: 1) LLMs are highly uncalibrated for numerical tasks 2) {\color{blue}there is no correlation between the length of the interval and the imposed confidence level, which can be symptomatic of a a) lack of understanding of the concept of confidence or b) inability to adjust self-confidence by following instructions}, {\color{blue}3)} LLM numerical precision differs depending on the task, scale of answer and prompting technique {\color{blue}4) Refinement of answers doesn't improve precision in most cases}. We believe this study offers new perspectives on LLM overconfidence and serves as a strong baseline for overprecision in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Gauging Overprecision in LLMs: An Empirical Study" investigates overprecision, a form of overconfidence characterized by unwarranted certainty in the accuracy of knowledge or beliefs, in large language models (LLMs). The authors introduce a framework to study this phenomenon by prompting LLMs to generate numerical intervals with imposed confidence levels, refining these intervals through aggregation and self-refinement techniques, and then evaluating the results using cognitive science-inspired metrics. Key findings include: LLMs are poorly calibrated for numerical answers; there's a lack of correlation between interval length and imposed confidence levels; numerical precision varies based on task, answer scale, and prompting technique; and refinement strategies offer limited precision gains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to studying overconfidence in LLMs. While previous work has focused on overestimation (eliciting LLM's own confidence), this study examines *overprecision* by *imposing* confidence levels and evaluating how well the LLM adheres to those constraints when generating numerical intervals. This is a valuable shift in perspective. The framework design itself, with its generation, refinement, and evaluation phases, is well-structured and allows for systematic investigation.

*   **Significance:** Quantifying the trustworthiness of LLMs is crucial as they are increasingly deployed in various applications. Overprecision can lead to potentially harmful outcomes in areas such as financial analysis, medicine, and education. By introducing a method to gauge overprecision, the authors offer a means to better understand and potentially mitigate this risk. The paper also provides a strong baseline for future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Definition and Focus:** The paper clearly defines overprecision and justifies its importance within the context of LLMs.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** The proposed framework is methodical and allows for a systematic analysis of LLM behavior.
    *   **Cognitive Science Inspiration:** Drawing inspiration from cognitive science adds a theoretical grounding to the research.
    *   **Empirical Rigor:** The experiments are well-defined and utilize multiple datasets and models. The inclusion of self-refinement is interesting as well.
    *   **Thoughtful Analysis:** The authors provide insightful interpretations of their findings, considering the potential reasons behind the observed behaviors. They are also careful to address potential caveats like the inherent limitations of the prompts.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Scope:** The study primarily focuses on GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 (technically GPT-4o-Mini), which, while widely used, may not be representative of all LLMs. Including more models would strengthen the generalizability of the findings. While one can appreciate the reasoning to select those models as widely used and reliable, many open-source LLMs now exist that could provide greater generalizability in the observations.
    *   **Potential Prompting Biases:** While the authors acknowledge the potential for prompting biases, exploring alternative prompting strategies and mitigating their effects could further enhance the robustness of the results. A larger set of prompting configurations beyond CoT could have more general applicability. The nature of the hints, even as acknowledged in the appendix, provides a signal and potentially invalidates the study. This is a huge concern.
    *   **Limited Refinement Strategies:** The refinement techniques explored (aggregation and self-refinement) show limited gains, but further exploration of other refinement methods might be beneficial. The negative self-refinement results raise questions about the nature of this process in LLMs.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper is innovative and valuable, some limitations bring down the overall impact. The novel framework for evaluating LLM overprecision is an impactful shift from the focus on overestimation. The experimental approach, which is clearly inspired by cognitive science, offers valuable insight. However, the limited scope (models, the nature of the hints, a few refinements) somewhat tempers the overall result. Further exploration of other models and hint types, along with the self-refinement component, could increase the usefulness.

### Generalized Visual Relation Detection with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12100v1)
- **Authors**: Kaifeng Gao, Siqi Chen, Hanwang Zhang, Jun Xiao, Yueting Zhuang, Qianru Sun
- **Abstract**: Visual relation detection (VRD) aims to identify relationships (or interactions) between object pairs in an image. Although recent VRD models have achieved impressive performance, they are all restricted to pre-defined relation categories, while failing to consider the semantic ambiguity characteristic of visual relations. Unlike objects, the appearance of visual relations is always subtle and can be described by multiple predicate words from different perspectives, e.g., ``ride'' can be depicted as ``race'' and ``sit on'', from the sports and spatial position views, respectively. To this end, we propose to model visual relations as continuous embeddings, and design diffusion models to achieve generalized VRD in a conditional generative manner, termed Diff-VRD. We model the diffusion process in a latent space and generate all possible relations in the image as an embedding sequence. During the generation, the visual and text embeddings of subject-object pairs serve as conditional signals and are injected via cross-attention. After the generation, we design a subsequent matching stage to assign the relation words to subject-object pairs by considering their semantic similarities. Benefiting from the diffusion-based generative process, our Diff-VRD is able to generate visual relations beyond the pre-defined category labels of datasets. To properly evaluate this generalized VRD task, we introduce two evaluation metrics, i.e., text-to-image retrieval and SPICE PR Curve inspired by image captioning. Extensive experiments in both human-object interaction (HOI) detection and scene graph generation (SGG) benchmarks attest to the superiority and effectiveness of Diff-VRD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diff-VRD, a novel approach to visual relation detection (VRD) that leverages diffusion models to address the limitations of existing methods.  Unlike traditional VRD models that are restricted to pre-defined relation categories and struggle with semantic ambiguity, Diff-VRD models relations as continuous embeddings and generates them in a conditional generative manner. This allows the model to identify relations beyond the training categories and capture the subtle nuances of visual interactions.  The method uses a Transformer decoder to model the diffusion process, injecting subject-object pair information via cross-attention. A matching stage then assigns relation words to subject-object pairs based on semantic similarities. To properly evaluate the generalized VRD task, the paper introduces two new evaluation metrics: text-to-image retrieval and SPICE PR Curve. Experiments on HOI and SGG benchmarks demonstrate the superiority and effectiveness of Diff-VRD.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its application of diffusion models to the task of visual relation detection. While diffusion models have gained traction in other areas like image generation and text generation, their use in VRD, especially to handle semantic ambiguity and generalized relation detection, appears novel. The introduction of new evaluation metrics (T2I Retrieval and SPICE PR curve), is also a valuable contribution, as it addresses the limitation of traditional VRD evaluation metrics that are ill-suited for evaluating open-vocabulary and generalized settings. A diffusion-based generative model to detect **any** interaction (as the authors state) is conceptually interesting.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is multifaceted. First, it directly addresses a critical limitation of existing VRD systems – their inability to generalize to unseen relations and handle the inherent semantic ambiguity of relations. By modeling relations as continuous embeddings and using diffusion models to generate them, Diff-VRD opens up avenues for building more robust and adaptable VRD systems. The proposed evaluation metrics contribute to the field by enabling a more comprehensive evaluation of VRD models, considering both accuracy and the ability to identify reasonable but unannotated relations. Furthermore, a post-processing method to existing approaches is valuable as it offers an easy and readily applicable method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Generative Approach:** Using a generative diffusion model allows Diff-VRD to escape the constraints of predefined relation categories, enabling it to identify a wider range of relationships.
    *   **Semantic Ambiguity Handling:** The method explicitly addresses the semantic ambiguity of visual relations, which is a significant challenge in the VRD task.
    *   **New Evaluation Metrics:**  The introduction of T2I retrieval and SPICE PR curve provides a more appropriate way to evaluate generalized VRD models.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of Diff-VRD on standard benchmarks, showing improved performance compared to existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Visual and Text Embeddings:** The method relies heavily on pre-trained CLIP embeddings, which may introduce biases or limitations inherited from the CLIP model itself. The reliance on textual descriptions of relations, even in the generative phase, may still constrain the model's ability to generate completely novel or unexpected relations.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are generally computationally expensive, which could be a barrier to wider adoption. While the paper doesn't directly address this, practical deployment might require exploring techniques to optimize the diffusion process.
    *   **Complexity:** the model involves diffusion, CLIP, and transformers, increasing the overall complexity.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of VRD. It provides a fresh perspective and a novel approach to address the challenges of relation detection. The introduced evaluation metrics could become standard practice for evaluating generalized VRD models. The diffusion modeling of visual relations could inspire new research directions in related areas, such as scene understanding and human-computer interaction. The proposed post-processing is readily applicable to many existing systems, increasing its utility to the community.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8**. The paper demonstrates significant novelty in its approach to visual relation detection, leveraging diffusion models to address limitations of existing methods. The introduction of new evaluation metrics is a valuable contribution. The results on standard benchmarks are promising, showcasing the effectiveness of Diff-VRD. However, the reliance on visual-language models, the computational cost of diffusion models, and reliance on external encoders slightly temper the overall assessment. These limitations provide avenues for future research and improvement.

- **Score**: 8/10

### Entropy-Guided Watermarking for LLMs: A Test-Time Framework for Robust and Traceable Text Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12108v1)
- **Authors**: Shizhan Cai, Liang Ding, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has intensified concerns about content traceability and potential misuse. Existing watermarking schemes for sampled text often face trade-offs between maintaining text quality and ensuring robust detection against various attacks. To address these issues, we propose a novel watermarking scheme that improves both detectability and text quality by introducing a cumulative watermark entropy threshold. Our approach is compatible with and generalizes existing sampling functions, enhancing adaptability. Experimental results across multiple LLMs show that our scheme significantly outperforms existing methods, achieving over 80\% improvements on widely-used datasets, e.g., MATH and GSM8K, while maintaining high detection accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel entropy-guided watermarking scheme for Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve both detectability and text quality.  The core idea is to use a cumulative watermark entropy threshold; the text is watermarked only if its entropy exceeds this threshold. This is achieved by utilizing previous tokens as a seed to generate a key.  The method is designed to be compatible with existing sampling functions, enhancing adaptability.  Experimental results across multiple LLMs show that the proposed scheme outperforms existing methods in terms of long-answer question answering tasks while maintaining high detection accuracy and robustness to paraphrase attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the introduction of the entropy threshold concept to guide the watermarking process.  Existing methods often apply watermarks indiscriminately, which can negatively impact text quality. Using entropy as a gating mechanism is a reasonable approach to balance quality and detectability.  Adapting binary sampling via a new mapping is another novel contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the practical challenges associated with watermarking LLMs.  Many current techniques sacrifice text quality for detectability or are easily defeated by adversarial attacks like paraphrasing. The proposed method addresses these issues, potentially leading to more reliable and usable watermarking solutions. Demonstrating improvements in question-answering tasks is especially relevant, as these are crucial applications of LLMs.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong empirical results:** The paper provides solid experimental evidence across multiple LLMs and datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing watermarking techniques.  The performance gains on long-answer QA tasks are particularly noteworthy.
    * **Robustness:** The demonstrated robustness against paraphrase attacks is a major strength. Many existing watermarking schemes are brittle and easily circumvented by simple text modifications.
    * **Theoretical grounding:** The paper provides some theoretical justification for the proposed approach, including a proof of indistinguishability.
    * **Compatibility:** Claiming compatibility with existing sampling functions is useful for promoting the adoption of the method.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited ablation studies:** The paper could benefit from more detailed ablation studies to understand the impact of different components of the proposed scheme (e.g., the specific entropy function used, the threshold value, the choice of sampling function). Further analysis breaking down the performance in individual sub-categories of the benchmarked datasets would also improve the validity of the claims.
    * **Complex algorithm:** The description of the binary sampling adaptation, using Huffman encoding, is complex and difficult to follow. More clarity in explaining the practical implementation would be beneficial.
    * **Dependence on GPT-4 for scoring:**  Using GPT-4 to assess text quality is a common practice, but it introduces a potential bias. A human evaluation of text quality could strengthen the results. The use of SimCSE alleviates this concern somewhat, but it does not remove it completely.
    * **Limited analysis of larger models:**  While the paper discusses generalization to larger models, such as the Llama-3-1-8B model, further experiments with even larger models (e.g., models with 70B+ parameters) would improve the generalizability of the findings.

* **Impact:** If validated and adopted, this work could contribute to more responsible use of LLMs by allowing owners to detect misuse and protect against unauthorized distillation. The improved balance between text quality and detectability is a key factor that might encourage broader adoption.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a good contribution to the field of LLM watermarking. The novel entropy-guided approach shows significant promise in improving both text quality and robustness compared to prior methods. The experimental evaluation is reasonably thorough, and the theoretical justification adds weight to the claims. However, the weaknesses in algorithmic complexity and limited analysis do detract somewhat from the overall impact.

### A Diffusion-Based Framework for Terrain-Aware Remote Sensing Image Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12112v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenyu Yu, Mohd Yamani Inda Idris, Pei Wang
- **Abstract**: Remote sensing imagery is essential for environmental monitoring, agricultural management, and disaster response. However, data loss due to cloud cover, sensor failures, or incomplete acquisition-especially in high-resolution and high-frequency tasks-severely limits satellite imagery's effectiveness. Traditional interpolation methods struggle with large missing areas and complex structures. Remote sensing imagery consists of multiple bands, each with distinct meanings, and ensuring consistency across bands is critical to avoid anomalies in the combined images. This paper proposes SatelliteMaker, a diffusion-based method that reconstructs missing data across varying levels of data loss while maintaining spatial, spectral, and temporal consistency. We also propose Digital Elevation Model (DEM) as a conditioning input and use tailored prompts to generate realistic images, making diffusion models applicable to quantitative remote sensing tasks. Additionally, we propose a VGG-Adapter module based on Distribution Loss, which reduces distribution discrepancy and ensures style consistency. Extensive experiments show that SatelliteMaker achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SatelliteMaker, a diffusion-based framework designed for terrain-aware remote sensing image reconstruction. It tackles challenges such as cloud occlusion, missing data, and distribution discrepancies common in satellite imagery.  SatelliteMaker leverages diffusion models, Digital Elevation Models (DEMs) as conditional inputs, and tailored prompts to generate realistic images.  A VGG-Adapter module with a Distribution Loss is proposed to reduce distribution discrepancy and ensure style consistency across spectral bands.  The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of SatelliteMaker through experiments on Landsat-8 and EarthNet2021 datasets, showing state-of-the-art performance in various tasks like filling spatial gaps and temporal data completion.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a relevant problem:** Remote sensing image reconstruction is crucial for various applications, and the paper tackles key challenges such as cloud cover and data loss.
*   **Integration of DEMs:** Using DEMs as conditional inputs is a valuable contribution.  It enhances spatial consistency and accuracy, which is vital for quantitative remote sensing tasks.
*   **VGG-Adapter for style consistency:** The introduction of the VGG-Adapter module and Distribution Loss is a strong point. This helps to address the crucial issue of spectral consistency that often plagues generative models in remote sensing. Preserving color and textural characteristics is of paramount importance for accurate reconstruction, and the paper provides an intelligent solution.
*   **Strong experimental results:** The paper presents thorough experimental results on two datasets, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance compared to several other methods. The ablation study is also useful in verifying the importance of each module.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation Metrics:** The paper utilizes a comprehensive set of evaluation metrics, which supports the rigor of the reported findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Core Diffusion Architecture:** While the *application* of diffusion models to remote sensing is valuable, the core diffusion architecture itself doesn't seem to have significant modifications. It appears that the paper mainly focuses on the conditioning and loss functions. Although these components can enhance performance, the conceptual novelty of the core method might be limited.
*   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are known to be computationally expensive. The paper doesn't provide a detailed analysis of the computational cost of SatelliteMaker compared to other methods, which is a critical factor for real-world deployment.
*   **Dependency on High-Quality DEMs:** The reliance on DEM data as a conditioning input could be a limitation. The accuracy and availability of DEMs vary across different regions. The paper doesn't discuss how SatelliteMaker performs in areas where DEM data is low-quality or unavailable. This aspect could weaken the real-world applicability of the method.
*   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the paper performs well in image completion, the focus is relatively limited. Expanding the scope to tasks like super-resolution or change detection would demonstrate the generalizability of the framework further.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to make a significant impact on the remote sensing community.  It combines a state-of-the-art generative technique (diffusion models) with domain-specific knowledge (DEMs, spectral consistency) to address a critical problem. While the core diffusion architecture may not be entirely novel, the way it is applied and adapted to remote sensing data is definitely a valuable advancement. The improvements in image quality and accuracy can lead to better environmental monitoring, agricultural management, and disaster response capabilities.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of remote sensing by offering an effective diffusion-based framework for image reconstruction.  The use of DEMs and the VGG-Adapter addresses key challenges. While the conceptual novelty of the core diffusion approach may be limited, the practical benefits and the thorough experimental validation justify a positive, but not exceptional, evaluation.

### Clarifying Ambiguities: on the Role of Ambiguity Types in Prompting Methods for Clarification Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12113v1)
- **Authors**: Anfu Tang, Laure Soulier, Vincent Guigue
- **Abstract**: In information retrieval (IR), providing appropriate clarifications to better understand users' information needs is crucial for building a proactive search-oriented dialogue system. Due to the strong in-context learning ability of large language models (LLMs), recent studies investigate prompting methods to generate clarifications using few-shot or Chain of Thought (CoT) prompts. However, vanilla CoT prompting does not distinguish the characteristics of different information needs, making it difficult to understand how LLMs resolve ambiguities in user queries. In this work, we focus on the concept of ambiguity for clarification, seeking to model and integrate ambiguities in the clarification process. To this end, we comprehensively study the impact of prompting schemes based on reasoning and ambiguity for clarification. The idea is to enhance the reasoning abilities of LLMs by limiting CoT to predict first ambiguity types that can be interpreted as instructions to clarify, then correspondingly generate clarifications. We name this new prompting scheme Ambiguity Type-Chain of Thought (AT-CoT). Experiments are conducted on various datasets containing human-annotated clarifying questions to compare AT-CoT with multiple baselines. We also perform user simulations to implicitly measure the quality of generated clarifications under various IR scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the problem of generating clarifying questions in information retrieval (IR) to resolve ambiguity in user queries. The authors argue that current prompting methods for large language models (LLMs), particularly Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, don't adequately distinguish between different types of ambiguity.  To address this, they propose AMBIGUITY TYPE-CHAIN OF THOUGHT (AT-CoT), a prompting scheme that integrates ambiguity types (ATs) into the reasoning process. AT-CoT prompts LLMs to first predict the ambiguity type underlying a query and then generate clarifications accordingly.  They define a new action-based AT taxonomy (Semantic, Generalize, Specify). They evaluate AT-CoT on several datasets containing human-annotated clarifying questions, comparing it to standard prompting, AT-standard prompting (informing LLMs of ATs without forcing prediction), and vanilla CoT prompting. Experiments include intrinsic evaluation (clarification generation) and extrinsic evaluation (IR performance via user simulation). The results show that AT-CoT consistently outperforms baselines.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of an explicit ambiguity type taxonomy into the CoT prompting framework for clarification generation.  While CoT prompting and clarification generation are not new, the structured approach of explicitly incorporating and reasoning about ATs is a significant contribution. The proposed action-based ambiguity taxonomy is also a novel element and seems well-suited for guiding LLM behavior in generating clarifications.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work is threefold: 1) It offers a more structured and interpretable approach to clarification generation, moving beyond free-form reasoning. 2)  The improved clarification generation, demonstrated through both intrinsic and extrinsic evaluations, has the potential to improve the effectiveness of interactive search systems. 3) By explicitly modeling ambiguity, the work provides insights into how LLMs can be better leveraged to understand and resolve user information needs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem and approach:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing CoT methods and presents a well-motivated solution.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experiments are thorough, covering multiple datasets and evaluation tasks (clarification generation and IR). The inclusion of user simulation is valuable for assessing real-world impact.
    *   **Detailed analysis:**  The analysis of the predicted ambiguity types and their impact on performance provides deeper insights into the workings of AT-CoT.
    *   **Consistent improvements:** AT-CoT consistently outperforms baselines across various datasets and evaluation metrics.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **AT Taxonomy limitations:** The three-type AT taxonomy, while action-oriented, might be considered somewhat simplistic.  A more fine-grained or hierarchical taxonomy could potentially lead to even better performance. The authors acknowledge the exploration of AT granularity as a future work.
    *   **User Simulation Assumptions:** As with all user simulations, the validity of the results depends on the realism of the assumptions made about user behavior. The authors acknowledge these limitations and assume users are cooperative with no intent shifts occur.
    *   **Model Scale:**  The work utilizes Llama-3-8B model which might limit the generalization of the results. Although the authors mention the exploration of different model scales as future work, the absence of it is a weakness.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with other Taxonomy-Based Methods:** The comparison with other taxonomy-based methods could provide deeper insight into the improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in clarification generation and interactive IR. The AT-CoT framework could be extended with more sophisticated AT taxonomies or combined with other techniques for user intent modeling. The findings could also inform the design of more effective interactive search systems. The explicit modeling of ambiguity also provides a way to analyze failure cases and improve the robustness of clarification generation systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to improving clarification generation in IR by integrating ambiguity types into the CoT prompting framework.  The experiments are comprehensive, and the results demonstrate consistent improvements over existing methods. While the AT taxonomy is relatively simple and the user simulation involves assumptions, the work represents a significant step forward in structured clarification generation and provides valuable insights for future research. The novelty is significant, but the simplicity of the AT taxonomy prevents a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Anti-Aesthetics: Protecting Facial Privacy against Customized Text-to-Image Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12129v1)
- **Authors**: Songping Wang, Yueming Lyu, Shiqi Liu, Ning Li, Tong Tong, Hao Sun, Caifeng Shan
- **Abstract**: The rise of customized diffusion models has spurred a boom in personalized visual content creation, but also poses risks of malicious misuse, severely threatening personal privacy and copyright protection. Some studies show that the aesthetic properties of images are highly positively correlated with human perception of image quality. Inspired by this, we approach the problem from a novel and intriguing aesthetic perspective to degrade the generation quality of maliciously customized models, thereby achieving better protection of facial identity. Specifically, we propose a Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) framework to fully explore aesthetic cues, which consists of two key branches: 1) Global Anti-Aesthetics: By establishing a global anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a global anti-aesthetic loss, it can degrade the overall aesthetics of the generated content; 2) Local Anti-Aesthetics: A local anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and a local anti-aesthetic loss are designed to guide adversarial perturbations to disrupt local facial identity. By seamlessly integrating both branches, our HAA effectively achieves the goal of anti-aesthetics from a global to a local level during customized generation. Extensive experiments show that HAA outperforms existing SOTA methods largely in identity removal, providing a powerful tool for protecting facial privacy and copyright.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Anti-Aesthetics: Protecting Facial Privacy against Customized Text-to-Image Synthesis."

**Summary**

The paper proposes a novel approach called Hierarchical Anti-Aesthetic (HAA) to protect facial privacy against malicious misuse of customized text-to-image diffusion models. HAA aims to degrade the generation quality of customized models by manipulating the aesthetic properties of generated images. The framework has two key components: (1) *Global Anti-Aesthetics*, which degrades the overall aesthetic quality using an anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and loss; and (2) *Local Anti-Aesthetics*, which disrupts local facial identity using adversarial perturbations guided by a local anti-aesthetic reward mechanism and loss. By integrating both global and local strategies, HAA aims to prevent the reconstruction of recognizable facial features, safeguarding privacy and copyright. The authors demonstrate through extensive experiments that HAA outperforms existing methods in identity removal.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using anti-aesthetics as a means to protect facial privacy is relatively novel. Existing methods primarily focus on directly perturbing the image to prevent accurate reconstruction or feature extraction, often without considering the aesthetic implications of these perturbations. Framing the problem within an aesthetic context offers a new perspective. The hierarchical approach, combining global and local aesthetic considerations, adds another layer of novelty. However, the individual components (adversarial attacks, reward mechanisms) are not entirely novel; their combination and application to this specific problem are.

*   **Significance:** Facial privacy is a significant and growing concern, particularly with the rise of powerful generative models that can be easily customized for malicious purposes (e.g., deepfakes). The paper tackles a very relevant problem. If the proposed HAA method effectively prevents identity leakage, it would be a valuable contribution to the field. The experiments seem thorough, comparing against several SOTA baselines and evaluating with multiple datasets and prompts. A potentially important practical advantage highlighted is that the method aims to add "subtle" noise that has a minimal impact to the overall use of the image.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Formulation:** Addresses a critical and timely problem.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of anti-aesthetics is a unique framing.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The hierarchical approach combines global and local anti-aesthetic mechanisms.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** Evaluated against multiple baselines, datasets, and metrics.
    *   **Practical Potential:** Aims to add subtle noise which can still be used for other purposes.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Component Novelty:** While the overall framework is novel, the individual components (adversarial attacks, reward mechanisms) are well-established. The paper's novelty lies more in the combination and application of these existing techniques than in the invention of new ones.
    *   **Subjectivity of Aesthetics:** The concept of "aesthetics" itself is somewhat subjective. While the paper uses a reward model based on human preferences, the effectiveness of the method might depend on the specific characteristics of this reward model and how well it captures diverse aesthetic sensibilities. Different training sets for reward models could have significantly different results.
    *   **Generalization to Unknown Attacks:** The robustness of the method against future, more sophisticated attacks is unclear. Adversarial defenses often prove vulnerable to new attack strategies. How well will it work when adversaries specifically target anti-aesthetic measures?

*   **Potential Influence:** If the proposed HAA framework is proven to be robust and generalizable, it could influence the development of future privacy-preserving methods for generative models. It might also inspire more research into the relationship between aesthetics and human perception in the context of AI safety and security. Furthermore, the research may influence copyright protection schemes.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a significant problem. While the individual components are not groundbreaking, the framework's conceptual novelty, the systematic combination of these components, and the experimental validation justify a reasonably high score. However, the inherent subjectivity of aesthetics, the potential for the method to be circumvented by future attacks, and the relatively incremental nature of the underlying techniques prevent it from achieving a higher score. The study does address important limitations, however, with robustness tests that demonstrate effective counter attacks. The method shows good signs of scalability, and this makes it a very valuable research paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multilingual Contextualization of Large Language Models for Document-Level Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12140v1)
- **Authors**: Miguel Moura Ramos, Patrick Fernandes, Sweta Agrawal, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance in sentence-level machine translation, but scaling to document-level translation remains challenging, particularly in modeling long-range dependencies and discourse phenomena across sentences and paragraphs. In this work, we propose a method to improve LLM-based long-document translation through targeted fine-tuning on high-quality document-level data, which we curate and introduce as DocBlocks. Our approach supports multiple translation paradigms, including direct document-to-document and chunk-level translation, by integrating instructions both with and without surrounding context. This enables models to better capture cross-sentence dependencies while maintaining strong sentence-level translation performance. Experimental results show that incorporating multiple translation paradigms improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based methods.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces DOCBLOCKS, a new multilingual, document-level parallel dataset designed for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve their performance on document-level machine translation (MT). The authors propose a fine-tuning approach that leverages DOCBLOCKS in multiple translation paradigms (direct document-to-document and chunk-level translation) with and without surrounding context integrated as instructions.  They demonstrate through experimentation that this approach improves document-level translation quality and inference speed compared to prompting and agent-based methods, and show the efficacy of the method across different LLM architectures. They also present ablation studies assessing different components of DOCBLOCKS, like filtering, MRD2D, and CAPT and show the effects of balancing sentence and document-level data in DOCBLOCKS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: improving the document-level translation capabilities of LLMs. While LLMs have shown promise in sentence-level MT, scaling to document-level poses significant challenges, particularly in capturing long-range dependencies and discourse phenomena.

*   **Novelty:** The DOCBLOCKS dataset is a novel contribution. The creation of a high-quality, filtered, document-level MT dataset addresses a recognized gap in resources. The curated approach, focusing on full documents and contextual segments from trusted parallel corpora (news, TED talks, literature, parliamentary proceedings), distinguishes it from existing datasets that primarily focus on isolated sentence pairs or limited context windows. The incorporation of multiple training paradigms (MRD2D and CAPT) seems valuable.

*   **Significance:** The reported results indicate improved performance compared to prompting and agent-based methods, which suggests a viable alternative approach. The ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of data curation and training strategies. Showing this benefit on different LLM architectures helps to showcase the general applicability of the method.

*   **Limitations and Questions:**
    * The reliance on automatic metrics like BLEU and COMET, even with document-level adaptations, is a potential weakness. While LTCR is also reported, it may not fully capture the nuances of coherence, consistency, and style in document-level translation, so the experiments may benefit from a human evaluation component, which should be part of future work.
    * The specific details of the data curation process, especially the thresholding used with Bicleaner and CometKiwi-23, might benefit from further justification and sensitivity analysis.
    * The experiments are limited to a set of specific language pairs (including GuoFeng and IWSLT), and a more comprehensive evaluation across diverse language families and domains would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    * While the authors claim improvements in inference speed, the evaluation of throughput lacks detailed analysis of the computational cost associated with fine-tuning.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field, primarily through the introduction of the DOCBLOCKS dataset and the demonstrated effectiveness of fine-tuning LLMs using this resource. While the experimental evaluation relies heavily on automatic metrics and lacks exhaustive analysis across different datasets/languages, the results are promising and suggest that the proposed approach has the potential to advance the state-of-the-art in document-level MT. The ablation studies provide important insights into the impact of different data curation strategies, and the comparison against prompting and agent-based methods provides a strong comparative basis for the introduced method. Considering the limitations regarding evaluation and generalizability, this warrants a positive, but not exceptionally high, score.
