### Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12177v1)
- **Authors**: Victor Manuel Hernandez Lopez, Jaime E. Cuellar
- **Abstract**: This article analyzes the Hamas-Israel controversy through 253,925 Spanish-language YouTube comments posted between October 2023 and January 2024, following the October 7 attack that escalated the conflict. Adopting an interdisciplinary approach, the study combines the analysis of controversies from Science and Technology Studies (STS) with advanced computational methodologies, specifically Natural Language Processing (NLP) using the BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) model. Using this approach, the comments were automatically classified into seven categories, reflecting pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, anti- Palestinian, anti-Israeli positions, among others. The results show a predominance of pro- Palestinian comments, although pro-Israeli and anti-Palestinian comments received more "likes." This study also applies the agenda-setting theory to demonstrate how media coverage significantly influences public perception, observing a notable shift in public opinion, transitioning from a pro- Palestinian stance to a more critical position towards Israel. This work highlights the importance of combining social science perspectives with technological tools in the analysis of controversies, presenting a methodological innovation by integrating computational analysis with critical social theories to address complex public opinion phenomena and media narratives.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Mapping Controversies Using Artificial Intelligence: An Analysis of the Hamas-Israel Conflict on YouTube" analyzes Spanish-language YouTube comments (253,925) posted between October 2023 and January 2024 regarding the Hamas-Israel conflict. It employs a mixed-methods approach, combining Science and Technology Studies (STS) analysis of controversies with Natural Language Processing (NLP) using the BERT model. The comments are classified into seven categories reflecting different positions (pro-Palestinian, pro-Israeli, etc.). The study also incorporates agenda-setting theory to understand how media coverage shapes public perception.  Results indicate a prevalence of pro-Palestinian comments initially, but a shift towards an anti-Israel stance later on, aligning with evolving media narratives.  The paper argues for the importance of combining social science perspectives with computational tools for analyzing controversies and presents a methodological innovation in integrating computational analysis with critical social theories.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Interdisciplinary Approach:** The paper effectively bridges STS and NLP, offering a more nuanced understanding of the controversy than either approach could achieve alone. The integration of agenda-setting theory provides a valuable framework for interpreting the observed shifts in public opinion.
    *   **Methodological Innovation:** The paper integrates computational analysis with critical social theories to explore complex phenomena.
    *   **Real-world Relevance:**  The analysis of the Hamas-Israel conflict is highly topical and relevant, providing insights into the online discourse surrounding a significant geopolitical event.
    *   **Empirical Foundation:**  The large dataset of YouTube comments provides a solid empirical basis for the analysis.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper clearly outlines its methodology, findings, and conclusions. The use of visualizations (graphs) aids in understanding the data.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The study focuses solely on Spanish-language YouTube comments. This limits the generalizability of the findings to other languages, platforms, or cultural contexts.
    *   **Potential Bias in BERT:** While acknowledged, the potential for bias in the BERT model, stemming from its training data, needs further scrutiny.  The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of how potential biases were mitigated or accounted for.
    *   **Oversimplification of Categories:**  The classification of comments into seven fixed categories may oversimplify the complex and nuanced opinions expressed online. The study acknowledges the limitations in classifying "Anti-Hamas" sentiments but doesn't fully address the broader issue of capturing the full spectrum of views.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Qualitative Analysis:** While the paper mentions a qualitative component, the discussion primarily focuses on quantitative findings. A more in-depth qualitative analysis of a subset of comments could provide richer insights and validate the automated classification.
    *   **Novelty and Significance**: The methodology of combining computational techniques and social science theory is a common and well established technique. Furthermore, the results are not particularly new given there has been significant analysis of the conflict.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The application of BERT for analyzing political discourse on the Hamas-Israel conflict is of value.
    *   The integration of agenda-setting theory in the context of online discussions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable analysis of online discourse surrounding a significant geopolitical event, using a methodologically sound and interdisciplinary approach. However, limitations in the scope, potential biases, and depth of qualitative analysis somewhat detract from its overall impact. Given that similar techniques have been deployed, this paper provides a good application of a well established technique. Therefore, the paper is moderately novel.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Trusting CHATGPT: how minor tweaks in the prompts lead to major differences in sentiment classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12180v1)
- **Authors**: Jaime E. Cuellar, Oscar Moreno-Martinez, Paula Sofia Torres-Rodriguez, Jaime Andres Pavlich-Mariscal, Andres Felipe Mican-Castiblanco, Juan Guillermo Torres-Hurtado
- **Abstract**: One fundamental question for the social sciences today is: how much can we trust highly complex predictive models like ChatGPT? This study tests the hypothesis that subtle changes in the structure of prompts do not produce significant variations in the classification results of sentiment polarity analysis generated by the Large Language Model GPT-4o mini. Using a dataset of 100.000 comments in Spanish on four Latin American presidents, the model classified the comments as positive, negative, or neutral on 10 occasions, varying the prompts slightly each time. The experimental methodology included exploratory and confirmatory analyses to identify significant discrepancies among classifications. The results reveal that even minor modifications to prompts such as lexical, syntactic, or modal changes, or even their lack of structure impact the classifications. In certain cases, the model produced inconsistent responses, such as mixing categories, providing unsolicited explanations, or using languages other than Spanish. Statistical analysis using Chi-square tests confirmed significant differences in most comparisons between prompts, except in one case where linguistic structures were highly similar. These findings challenge the robustness and trust of Large Language Models for classification tasks, highlighting their vulnerability to variations in instructions. Moreover, it was evident that the lack of structured grammar in prompts increases the frequency of hallucinations. The discussion underscores that trust in Large Language Models is based not only on technical performance but also on the social and institutional relationships underpinning their use.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-4o mini, when used for sentiment analysis. It tests the hypothesis that subtle variations in prompt structure significantly affect the results of sentiment classification. The authors analyze 100,000 YouTube comments in Spanish related to four Latin American presidents, using ten different prompts that vary in lexical choice, syntax, modality, and structure. They employ both exploratory (PCA, Levenshtein distance, coincidence matrix) and confirmatory (Chi-squared test) statistical methods to identify significant discrepancies in the classifications generated by the different prompts. The study finds that even minor modifications in prompts can significantly alter the sentiment classification results, questioning the robustness and reliability of LLMs for such tasks. The paper also notes the tendency for prompts with poor grammatical structure to produce more "hallucinations" or inconsistent responses.  The discussion emphasizes the social and institutional context of trust in LLMs, arguing that confidence isn't solely based on technical performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic examination of prompt sensitivity in sentiment analysis for a specific LLM (GPT-4o mini) and language (Spanish), using a relatively large dataset of real-world comments. While prior research has explored prompt engineering and LLM robustness, this study provides a detailed empirical analysis of subtle prompt variations and their impact on a practical task within a specific linguistic context. However, there's nothing drastically new in its broad approach (testing prompt variations).

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its implications for the responsible use of LLMs in social sciences and related fields. By demonstrating the vulnerability of sentiment analysis to minor prompt variations, the authors raise concerns about the reliability and interpretability of findings derived from these models. The observation regarding the correlation between poor grammar in prompts and increased "hallucinations" is also practically significant, offering insights into how to better design prompts for LLMs. The discussion section touches on the very pertinent and critical issue about the social and institutional trust placed on LLMs beyond just their technical capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The study uses a large dataset of real-world comments, increasing the ecological validity of the findings.
    *   The experimental design is well-structured, with clear variations in prompt construction.
    *   The combination of exploratory and confirmatory statistical methods provides a robust analysis.
    *   The discussion explores the broader implications for trust and reliance on LLMs in research and decision-making.
    *   The exploration goes beyond purely technical dimensions, bringing in perspectives from STS regarding the social trust placed in such models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study is limited to one LLM (GPT-4o mini) and one language (Spanish). While this provides depth, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other LLMs and languages. It would strengthen the study to compare to a different LLM architecture.
    *   The reliance on YouTube comments means that the dataset may be subject to biases inherent in that platform's user base and content moderation policies.
    *   While the study investigates *statistical* significance, it doesn't delve deeply into the *practical* significance of the observed variations in sentiment classification. For instance, does a 5% difference in positive sentiment classification actually *matter* in a real-world application?

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence researchers and practitioners who use LLMs for sentiment analysis, encouraging them to be more cautious about prompt design and to validate their results using multiple prompts or other methods.  It contributes to the ongoing conversation about the limitations and biases of LLMs and the need for responsible AI development and deployment. It also emphasizes that the social trust placed in LLMs can be outsized compared to the robustness and reliability of their technical capabilities.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid empirical contribution to the field of LLM research, coupled with its relevant discussion about trust and responsibility. It's not a groundbreaking study that revolutionizes the field, but a systematic investigation that builds on existing knowledge and provides valuable practical insights. Its relative limitations in generalizability (single LLM and language) and its relatively limited engagement with the *practical* significance of the sentiment differences prevent a higher score. But the paper makes a robust contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SALAD: Improving Robustness and Generalization through Contrastive Learning with Structure-Aware and LLM-Driven Augmented Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12185v1)
- **Authors**: Suyoung Bae, Hyojun Kim, YunSeok Choi, Jee-Hyong Lee
- **Abstract**: In various natural language processing (NLP) tasks, fine-tuning Pre-trained Language Models (PLMs) often leads to the issue of spurious correlations, which negatively impacts performance, particularly when dealing with out-of-distribution data. To address this problem, we propose SALAD}(Structure Aware and LLM-driven Augmented Data), a novel approach designed to enhance model robustness and generalization by generating structure-aware and counterfactually augmented data for contrastive learning. Our method leverages a tagging-based approach to generate structure-aware positive samples and utilizes large language models (LLMs) to generate counterfactual negative samples with diverse sentence patterns. By applying contrastive learning, SALAD enables the model to focus on learning the structural relationships between key sentence components while minimizing reliance on spurious correlations. We validate our approach through experiments on three tasks: Sentiment Classification, Sexism Detection, and Natural Language Inference. The results demonstrate that SALAD not only improves model robustness and performance across different environments but also enhances generalization to out-of-distribution datasets and cross-domain scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SALAD: Improving Robustness and Generalization through Contrastive Learning with Structure-Aware and LLM-Driven Augmented Data":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SALAD, a novel approach to enhance the robustness and generalization ability of pre-trained language models (PLMs) when fine-tuned for NLP tasks.  SALAD tackles the problem of spurious correlations by generating structure-aware and counterfactually augmented data for contrastive learning. The method utilizes POS tagging to identify structural elements of sentences to generate positive samples by masking less-important tokens. It employs Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate negative samples with varied sentence patterns. Contrastive learning then encourages the model to learn structural relationships between sentence components while minimizing reliance on spurious correlations.  The authors evaluate SALAD on sentiment classification, sexism detection, and natural language inference tasks, demonstrating improved performance and generalization compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of structure-aware data generation (using POS tagging) and LLM-driven counterfactual data augmentation within a contrastive learning framework. While individual components like data augmentation and contrastive learning are not new, the specific integration and application to mitigate spurious correlations, especially using LLMs for counterfactual generation with structural awareness, represents a distinct contribution. The LLM method focuses on simple prompts while focusing on generating datasets by leveraging POS tag information to alter the labels on words. It's less complex than many retrieval-based methods but is effective in enhancing model performance across three tasks.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in NLP: the vulnerability of models to spurious correlations and the resulting poor performance on out-of-distribution data. By demonstrating that SALAD improves robustness and generalization, the paper offers a valuable technique for developing more reliable NLP systems. The results across several tasks (sentiment, sexism detection, NLI) and datasets (including cross-domain evaluation) strengthens the significance of the findings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper conducts extensive experiments across multiple tasks, datasets (including in-domain, out-of-distribution, and cross-domain scenarios), and baselines. This comprehensive evaluation provides strong evidence supporting the effectiveness of SALAD.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies help isolate the contributions of different components of SALAD (structure-aware augmentation, LLM-driven augmentation, contrastive learning).
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The method is well-described, and the implementation details provided are sufficient for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependence:** The method relies on the quality of LLM-generated counterfactual data. While the experiments show good results, the performance of SALAD may be sensitive to the choice of LLM and the prompt engineering used for counterfactual generation. This could be explored more.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The method involves several hyperparameters, such as the scaling factor *k* for selecting [UNK] tokens and the loss weighting parameter *A*. There is no comprehensive section dedicated to the optimization of these hyperparameters, rather a claim of them being tuned per data set.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   SALAD offers a practical approach for improving the robustness and generalization of NLP models, which can benefit a wide range of applications.
    *   The combination of structure-aware and LLM-driven augmentation could inspire further research into more sophisticated data augmentation techniques for addressing spurious correlations.
    *   The method provides a framework for effectively utilizing LLMs for data augmentation, potentially reducing the need for manual annotation.

**Justification:**

SALAD's combination of structure-aware generation and LLM-driven data augmentation makes the study valuable to the NLP community. Addressing the problems of shortcut learning with a robust, automated method with few resources involved, is a significant development.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### What Do Large Language Models Know? Tacit Knowledge as a Potential Causal-Explanatory Structure
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12187v1)
- **Authors**: Céline Budding
- **Abstract**: It is sometimes assumed that Large Language Models (LLMs) know language, or for example that they know that Paris is the capital of France. But what -- if anything -- do LLMs actually know? In this paper, I argue that LLMs can acquire tacit knowledge as defined by Martin Davies (1990). Whereas Davies himself denies that neural networks can acquire tacit knowledge, I demonstrate that certain architectural features of LLMs satisfy the constraints of semantic description, syntactic structure, and causal systematicity. Thus, tacit knowledge may serve as a conceptual framework for describing, explaining, and intervening on LLMs and their behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the possibility that Large Language Models (LLMs) possess tacit knowledge, as defined by Martin Davies. The author argues that while LLMs are trained on next-word prediction and do not have explicit, pre-programmed rules, they can acquire a form of implicit knowledge through their architectural features, specifically the embedding layer.  The paper refines Davies's original constraints for attributing tacit knowledge, particularly addressing the 'syntactic structure' requirement which Davies himself argued was problematic for connectionist networks. It proposes a weaker constraint that acknowledges the role of embeddings in grouping semantically similar inputs. The paper analyzes recent technical literature, focusing on studies that edit factual associations in LLMs, to provide preliminary evidence that LLMs can meet these modified constraints and thus, potentially possess tacit knowledge. The author also acknowledges potential limitations and areas for further research, particularly concerning the reliability of intervention-based methods for identifying causal common factors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of a relatively old philosophical concept (tacit knowledge) to the contemporary field of LLMs and interpretability. While the notion of LLMs "knowing" things has been discussed, framing it through the lens of Davies's account of tacit knowledge offers a more structured and nuanced perspective. The paper also offers a useful critique of the original Davies constraints and provides useful adaptations. However, the connection between tacit knowledge, as originally defined, and LLMs seems tenuous; there are some fundamental disconnects, which the paper attempts to address, but not entirely successfully.
*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to be significant for several reasons. First, it provides a conceptual framework for thinking about what LLMs "learn" beyond mere statistical correlations. This could guide research on mechanistic interpretability, allowing for a more rigorous understanding of the internal causal processes. Second, by linking to the philosophical literature, it connects the AI field to broader theoretical discussions about knowledge and representation. The interventionist approaches outlined potentially offer a method for validating and understanding internal structure within LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates Davies's concept of tacit knowledge and its relevance to the discussion of knowledge in LLMs.
    *   The refinement of the syntactic structure constraint is insightful and necessary for applying the concept to LLMs' architectures.
    *   The analysis of empirical research on fact editing provides concrete examples of how the proposed framework can be applied.
    *   The authors acknowledge limitations and offer possible areas for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The empirical evidence presented is somewhat thin. The reliance on Meng et al. (2022) as the main source of support exposes the paper to the criticisms leveled against that work (e.g., the concerns raised by Hase et al. (2023)).
    *   The notion of "causal common factors" within the distributed representations of LLMs remains somewhat abstract. More concrete examples of how these factors could be identified and manipulated would strengthen the argument. It would be necessary to link abstract factors to model parameters.
    *   The paper doesn't address, with full satisfactory depth, a deeper question: whether the conceptual toolkit around "knowledge" is truly applicable to LLMs, even under this relaxed notion of tacit knowledge.
    *   The link between the philosophical concept of tacit knowledge and its application to LLMs might be considered somewhat loose. The author attempts to bridge this gap, but the connections may not fully convince all readers.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The framework could encourage researchers to move beyond simple input-output analysis of LLMs and delve deeper into their internal causal structures.
    *   It may stimulate further research on developing intervention techniques that can reliably identify and manipulate the representations underlying LLMs' "knowledge."
    *   It provides a conceptual vocabulary for the interpretability field to assess the success of different interpretability methods.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a useful contribution by applying a philosophical framework to the interpretation of LLMs. It refines the definition to better adapt it to the architecture and properties of transformer models. It also provides some testable, empirical claims about interpretability and causal structure. However, some conceptual questions remain somewhat open and the supporting evidence is limited. Thus, it is novel, but incremental.

### d1: Scaling Reasoning in Diffusion Large Language Models via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12216v1)
- **Authors**: Siyan Zhao, Devaansh Gupta, Qinqing Zheng, Aditya Grover
- **Abstract**: Recent large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong reasoning capabilities that benefits from online reinforcement learning (RL). These capabilities have primarily been demonstrated within the left-to-right autoregressive (AR) generation paradigm. In contrast, non-autoregressive paradigms based on diffusion generate text in a coarse-to-fine manner. Although recent diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) have achieved competitive language modeling performance compared to their AR counterparts, it remains unclear if dLLMs can also leverage recent advances in LLM reasoning. To this end, we propose d1, a framework to adapt pre-trained masked dLLMs into reasoning models via a combination of supervised finetuning (SFT) and RL. Specifically, we develop and extend techniques to improve reasoning in pretrained dLLMs: (a) we utilize a masked SFT technique to distill knowledge and instill self-improvement behavior directly from existing datasets, and (b) we introduce a novel critic-free, policy-gradient based RL algorithm called diffu-GRPO. Through empirical studies, we investigate the performance of different post-training recipes on multiple mathematical and logical reasoning benchmarks. We find that d1 yields the best performance and significantly improves performance of a state-of-the-art dLLM.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces d1, a two-stage framework for improving the reasoning capabilities of masked diffusion large language models (dLLMs). The first stage uses supervised finetuning (SFT) on high-quality reasoning traces. The second stage employs a novel policy gradient reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm called diffu-GRPO, specifically designed for dLLMs.  diffu-GRPO uses an efficient one-step estimation of log-probabilities, leveraging random prompt masking as a form of regularization. The authors empirically evaluate d1 using LLaDA-8B-Instruct as the base model on mathematical and logical reasoning benchmarks, showing that d1 outperforms base LLaDA, SFT-only, and diffu-GRPO-only models.  The paper also provides ablation studies and qualitative analyses of the generated text.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Gap:** The paper tackles an important and relatively unexplored area: how to effectively apply reinforcement learning to improve reasoning in dLLMs. While AR models have seen significant progress in RL-based reasoning, dLLMs present unique challenges due to their non-autoregressive nature.
    *   **Novel Algorithm (diffu-GRPO):** The diffu-GRPO algorithm seems to be a genuinely novel contribution, tailored to the dLLM paradigm. The use of random prompt masking for efficient log-probability estimation and regularization is a clever technique to address the computational complexities inherent in dLLMs. It helps alleviate the computation needed in policy optimization.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The empirical evaluation is reasonably thorough, with comparisons against several baselines (base model, SFT, and RL-only). The inclusion of ablation studies helps to understand the contributions of different components of the d1 framework. Qualitative analysis sheds light on "aha moments" and how reasoning traces differ between models.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Solution:** The paper does a solid job of clearly outlining the challenges associated with applying RL to dLLMs and then presenting a solution that directly addresses those challenges.
    *   **Code will be available.** The authors have indicated that code will be made available.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement?** While the results show consistent improvements, the *magnitude* of the improvements on some tasks (GSM8K and MATH500) is somewhat modest. This raises the question of whether the complexity of the d1 framework justifies the limited gains, especially given the computational cost of RL. While the improvement is good, it can be described as incremental.
    *   **Limited Generality/Specific Model:** The study is primarily focused on LLaDA-8B-Instruct. It's unclear how well d1 would generalize to other dLLM architectures or larger models. Further experimentation on a variety of models would be helpful.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Although the diffu-GRPO method aims to improve computational efficiency, RL is still inherently more computationally expensive than SFT. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the actual computational resources required for training d1 compared to other approaches.
    *   **Synthetic Data:** The reliance on synthetic data for Countdown and Sudoku is a potential concern, as it might not fully reflect the complexities of real-world reasoning tasks. This reliance is acknowledged.
    *   **Reward Hacking?** The reward function in RL is often a critical component. Although mentioned, there is only limited details on the reward function and the possibility of reward hacking that could skew the outcome.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The *novelty* primarily comes from the diffu-GRPO algorithm and its adaptation to the specific characteristics of dLLMs. This approach to apply the randomized masking, efficient log-likelihood estimation and RL training procedure to masked diffusion models.
    *   The *significance* lies in opening up the possibility of effectively leveraging RL to improve reasoning in dLLMs. This could lead to further advancements in this emerging paradigm of language modeling, especially considering the potential efficiency advantages of dLLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**. This is a solid paper that makes a non-trivial contribution to the field. It addresses a relevant problem, proposes a novel algorithm, and provides empirical evidence of its effectiveness. The weaknesses relate to the incremental improvement and the limited generality of the study. However, it's a significant step forward in applying RL to dLLMs and has the potential to influence future research in this area. The novelty of the algorithm and the comprehensive experiments are enough to push it to a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Coding-Prior Guided Diffusion Network for Video Deblurring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12222v1)
- **Authors**: Yike Liu, Jianhui Zhang, Haipeng Li, Shuaicheng Liu, Bing Zeng
- **Abstract**: While recent video deblurring methods have advanced significantly, they often overlook two valuable prior information: (1) motion vectors (MVs) and coding residuals (CRs) from video codecs, which provide efficient inter-frame alignment cues, and (2) the rich real-world knowledge embedded in pre-trained diffusion generative models. We present CPGDNet, a novel two-stage framework that effectively leverages both coding priors and generative diffusion priors for high-quality deblurring. First, our coding-prior feature propagation (CPFP) module utilizes MVs for efficient frame alignment and CRs to generate attention masks, addressing motion inaccuracies and texture variations. Second, a coding-prior controlled generation (CPC) module network integrates coding priors into a pretrained diffusion model, guiding it to enhance critical regions and synthesize realistic details. Experiments demonstrate our method achieves state-of-the-art perceptual quality with up to 30% improvement in IQA metrics. Both the code and the codingprior-augmented dataset will be open-sourced.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the video deblurring paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CPGD-Net, a novel two-stage framework for video deblurring that leverages both coding priors (motion vectors and coding residuals) and generative diffusion priors. The first stage, the coding-prior feature propagation (CPFP) module, uses motion vectors for efficient frame alignment and coding residuals to generate attention masks, addressing motion inaccuracies and texture variations. The second stage, a coding-prior controlled generation (CPC) module, integrates coding priors into a pre-trained diffusion model, guiding it to enhance critical regions and synthesize realistic details. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art perceptual quality with significant improvements in IQA metrics. The authors plan to open-source the code and a coding-prior-augmented dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *integration* of coding priors (MVs and residuals directly from video codecs) with diffusion models for video deblurring. Prior work in video deblurring has largely overlooked these readily available and efficient motion cues. While the use of diffusion models for restoration tasks is not new, the combination with coding priors to *guide* the diffusion process *specifically* for video deblurring, particularly through attention mechanisms, is a distinct contribution. A weaker novelty is the CPFA/CPC structure - though useful, this can be regarded as a thoughtful use of the components rather than true novelty.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance is multifold:
    *   **Efficiency:** Using MVs avoids the computationally expensive optical flow estimation step that many other deblurring methods require. This makes the method potentially more practical for real-world applications.
    *   **Performance:** The claimed state-of-the-art (SOTA) perceptual quality improvements, backed by IQA metrics, suggest the method effectively addresses the blur artifacts and reconstructs details, which are key issues in deblurring.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The creation and release of a coding-prior-augmented dataset will be a valuable resource for the research community, enabling further exploration of these techniques.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation for utilizing coding priors.
    *   Well-structured two-stage framework with logical components (CPFP and CPC).
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation with quantitative (PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, NIQE, MUSIQ) and qualitative results on standard datasets.
    *   Ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of individual components and design choices.
    *   Open-sourcing code and dataset promotes reproducibility and further research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reduction in PSNR and SSIM, while explained, may raise concerns about the method's ability to accurately recover pixel-level details, even if perceptual quality is enhanced. Further discussion about the trade-offs would be beneficial.
    *   The paper could have provided more discussion on the limitations and potential failure cases of the method. For instance, the reliance on MVs from a specific codec might limit its generalizability to videos encoded with different compression standards. What about the cases when the objects move too fast to be captured by the motion vector precision?
    *   The description of the CPFA and CPC modules are quite technical.
    *   The complexity of the method is evident. While efficient compared to optical flow-based methods, the framework involves several networks and a diffusion model, making it potentially challenging to deploy on resource-constrained devices.

* **Impact:** If the claims are substantiated and the open-sourced code is well-implemented and user-friendly, the paper could significantly influence future video deblurring research by popularizing the use of coding priors and providing a solid foundation for building upon diffusion-based approaches. The improved perceptual quality could also have a practical impact on applications like video surveillance, archival restoration, and video conferencing.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the weaknesses mentioned, the paper presents a compelling and original approach to video deblurring. The integration of coding priors with diffusion models is a genuinely novel idea, backed by solid experimental results and a commitment to open-source resources. There are some minor weaknesses to be overcome. The trade-off between PSNR/SSIM and perceptual quality requires further discussion. Therefore, after critical evaluation, the paper deserves a score of **8**. This score acknowledges the novelty and significance of the core ideas, balanced against the need for further refinement and a more thorough discussion of limitations.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Watermarking Needs Input Repetition Masking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12229v1)
- **Authors**: David Khachaturov, Robert Mullins, Ilia Shumailov, Sumanth Dathathri
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) raised concerns over potential misuse, such as for spreading misinformation. In response two counter measures emerged: machine learning-based detectors that predict if text is synthetic, and LLM watermarking, which subtly marks generated text for identification and attribution. Meanwhile, humans are known to adjust language to their conversational partners both syntactically and lexically. By implication, it is possible that humans or unwatermarked LLMs could unintentionally mimic properties of LLM generated text, making counter measures unreliable. In this work we investigate the extent to which such conversational adaptation happens. We call the concept $\textit{mimicry}$ and demonstrate that both humans and LLMs end up mimicking, including the watermarking signal even in seemingly improbable settings. This challenges current academic assumptions and suggests that for long-term watermarking to be reliable, the likelihood of false positives needs to be significantly lower, while longer word sequences should be used for seeding watermarking mechanisms.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces the concept of "watermark mimicry" in large language models (LLMs), demonstrating that both humans and other LLMs can unintentionally mimic watermarking patterns through extended conversations. The authors show that unwatermarked LLMs, when interacting with watermarked LLMs, start producing watermarked outputs, challenging the long-term effectiveness of current LLM watermarking techniques. They also investigate the impact of ngram length and temperature on the mimicry effect. The experiments suggest that larger models (e.g., GPT-4o) are less susceptible to this mimicry.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a previously underexplored concept, *watermark mimicry*, which is a significant contribution. While prior work mentioned learning from watermarked text, this paper focuses on in-context adaptation and its implications for watermarking. The demonstration of this mimicry both in LLMs and in humans communicating with LLMs provides valuable insights.
*   **Significance:** The findings raise serious concerns about the robustness and long-term reliability of current LLM watermarking schemes. If watermarks can be unintentionally replicated by non-watermarked sources, the utility of watermarking for attribution and misuse detection is significantly diminished. This suggests that alternative watermarking approaches or different detection mechanisms are needed to account for the mimicry effect.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and the concept is clearly defined.
    *   The experiments are well-designed, considering different watermarking schemes, model sizes, and conversational settings.
    *   The investigation of both LLM-LLM and human-LLM interactions strengthens the findings.
    *   The discussion of limitations and future research directions is valuable.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is primarily limited to two popular watermarking schemes (Kirchenbauer et al. and Aaronson and Kirchner). It's unclear whether the observed mimicry is generalizable to all watermarking techniques.
    *   The experiments are limited to models of size 7B and 13B, potentially impacting the conclusions about larger models. While experiments with GPT-4o are presented, they do not show the mimicry effect, but this analysis appears to not have as thorough investigation for potential causes. A more systematic assessment and analysis on cause would strengthen claim.
    *   The explanation for the lack of mimicry in larger models remains speculative. While the authors suggest that larger models rely on broader contextual understanding, further investigation is needed to confirm this hypothesis. The role of training data on this phenomenon deserves further exploration.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the types of linguistic features that are being mimicked. Understanding the specific patterns that are being unintentionally replicated would inform the design of more robust watermarking schemes.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM watermarking and misuse detection. It highlights a critical vulnerability that must be addressed to ensure the reliable attribution of AI-generated content. The introduction of the concept of mimicry opens up new research directions for studying linguistic adaptation and its impact on AI safety. However, the degree to which this influence materializes will depend on the impact it has on the next steps in the field of LLM watermarking.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper's novelty lies in identifying and demonstrating the *watermark mimicry* phenomenon. The experiments, while limited in scope, provide strong evidence that this effect exists and can undermine current watermarking techniques. The paper's significance stems from the challenge it poses to the effectiveness of existing watermarking strategies for LLMs.

However, the paper has limitations in terms of the breadth of watermarking schemes considered, the depth of the analysis on larger models, and the granularity of the linguistic feature analysis. Moreover, the lack of a clear solution to mitigate or circumvent the phenomenon, to improve the overall detection rates across architectures.

Considering these factors, a score of 7 reflects the paper's valuable contribution to the field and its potential influence, balanced against the limitations of the study.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MOS: Towards Effective Smart Contract Vulnerability Detection through Mixture-of-Experts Tuning of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12234v1)
- **Authors**: Hang Yuan, Lei Yu, Zhirong Huang, Jingyuan Zhang, Junyi Lu, Shiqi Cheng, Li Yang, Fengjun Zhang, Jiajia Ma, Chun Zuo
- **Abstract**: Smart contract vulnerabilities pose significant security risks to blockchain systems, potentially leading to severe financial losses. Existing methods face several limitations: (1) Program analysis-based approaches rely on predefined patterns, lacking flexibility for new vulnerability types; (2) Deep learning-based methods lack explanations; (3) Large language model-based approaches suffer from high false positives. We propose MOS, a smart contract vulnerability detection framework based on mixture-of-experts tuning (MOE-Tuning) of large language models. First, we conduct continual pre-training on a large-scale smart contract dataset to provide domain-enhanced initialization. Second, we construct a high-quality MOE-Tuning dataset through a multi-stage pipeline combining LLM generation and expert verification for reliable explanations. Third, we design a vulnerability-aware routing mechanism that activates the most relevant expert networks by analyzing code features and their matching degree with experts. Finally, we extend the feed-forward layers into multiple parallel expert networks, each specializing in specific vulnerability patterns. We employ a dual-objective loss function: one for optimizing detection and explanation performance, and another for ensuring reasonable distribution of vulnerability types to experts through entropy calculation. Experiments show that MOS significantly outperforms existing methods with average improvements of 6.32% in F1 score and 4.80% in accuracy. The vulnerability explanations achieve positive ratings (scores of 3-4 on a 4-point scale) of 82.96%, 85.21% and 94.58% for correctness, completeness, and conciseness through human and LLM evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MOS: Towards Effective Smart Contract Vulnerability Detection through Mixture-of-Experts Tuning of Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes MOS, a novel framework for smart contract vulnerability detection that leverages Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs).  MOS aims to address limitations of existing methods, including reliance on predefined patterns in program analysis, lack of explanation in deep learning approaches, and high false positive rates in LLM-based methods.  The framework involves several key components: (1) continual pre-training on a smart contract dataset, (2) construction of a high-quality MOE-Tuning dataset via LLM generation and expert verification, (3) a vulnerability-aware routing mechanism to activate relevant expert networks based on code features, and (4) a specialized mixture of experts network with parallel expert networks dedicated to specific vulnerability patterns.  A dual-objective loss function is used to optimize detection and explanation performance while ensuring balanced expert load distribution.  Experiments demonstrate that MOS outperforms state-of-the-art methods in detection accuracy and provides high-quality vulnerability explanations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its holistic approach to smart contract vulnerability detection, combining several innovative techniques.
    *   **MoE Tuning for Vulnerability Detection:**  While MoE has been explored in other NLP tasks, its application to *specifically detect vulnerabilities* in smart contracts is a significant contribution.  The design of the expert network, routing mechanism, and specialized training data tailored for different vulnerability types is well-thought-out.
    *   **Vulnerability-Aware Routing:**  The dynamically selects expert networks based on code analysis is a smart way to move beyond rigid pattern-matching and create adaptability.
    *   **Explanation Generation:** The combination of LLM generation, expert review, and LLM evaluation for creating a high-quality explanation dataset is a strength. This addresses a key weakness of many deep learning-based approaches.
    *  **Dual-Objective Loss:** The Dual-Objective Loss function, which balances vulnerability detection and explanation capabilities, shows that the authors have thought about both aspects of the model during the training phase.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem with significant real-world implications: smart contract security. The proposed framework represents a substantial improvement over existing methods, as demonstrated by the experimental results. By improving vulnerability detection and providing reliable explanations, MOS could significantly enhance the security of blockchain systems and reduce financial losses. However, note that the baseline's have been taken from a single paper which means the SOTA comparison is limited. This reduces the impact of the work on the community.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** MOS tackles multiple limitations of existing techniques, resulting in a well-rounded solution.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate significant improvements in both detection accuracy and explanation quality across multiple vulnerability types.  The use of both human and LLM evaluation for explanations adds credibility.
    *   **Well-Defined Components:**  The paper clearly describes each component of the framework and its contribution to the overall performance.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** These are designed well to show the importance of each phase of the training for the overall vulnerability identification and explanation capabilities.
    *  **Error Analysis:** The discussion of detection limitations provides valuable insights into the challenges that remain and directions for future research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The high computational requirements of the MoE framework may limit its widespread adoption, especially for smaller organizations or individual developers. A deeper analysis and strategies for reducing the computational burden would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Validation:**  The external validity could be strengthened by evaluating the framework on a wider range of real-world smart contracts and deployment scenarios, with focus on the scalability. Also, the current SOTA comparison relies on taking baseline results from a single paper. This can introduce biases and limit the community impact.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** While the paper acknowledges the limitations of its prompt engineering approach, it doesn't delve into specific alternative strategies or a comprehensive comparison of different prompting techniques.
    *  **Code and Dataset availability:** While the code is available, the link is simply a placeholder. Making the code and dataset available will greatly benefit the community to build on this work.

* **Potential Influence:** If the computational costs can be addressed, MOS has the potential to become a widely used tool for smart contract vulnerability detection. The emphasis on explanation is particularly valuable for developers and auditors, enabling them to better understand and address security risks. Further research building on MOS could lead to more robust and explainable AI-powered security solutions for blockchain systems.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

MOS presents a significant advancement in smart contract vulnerability detection by combining MoE tuning, vulnerability-aware routing, and high-quality explanation generation. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating substantial improvements over existing methods. The approach addresses key limitations in the field and has the potential to significantly improve the security of blockchain systems. However, the high computational cost and limitations in validation and baseline comparison prevent it from achieving a higher score. Further work to address these weaknesses could solidify its position as a leading solution in this critical area.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Cobra: Efficient Line Art COlorization with BRoAder References
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12240v1)
- **Authors**: Junhao Zhuang, Lingen Li, Xuan Ju, Zhaoyang Zhang, Chun Yuan, Ying Shan
- **Abstract**: The comic production industry requires reference-based line art colorization with high accuracy, efficiency, contextual consistency, and flexible control. A comic page often involves diverse characters, objects, and backgrounds, which complicates the coloring process. Despite advancements in diffusion models for image generation, their application in line art colorization remains limited, facing challenges related to handling extensive reference images, time-consuming inference, and flexible control. We investigate the necessity of extensive contextual image guidance on the quality of line art colorization. To address these challenges, we introduce Cobra, an efficient and versatile method that supports color hints and utilizes over 200 reference images while maintaining low latency. Central to Cobra is a Causal Sparse DiT architecture, which leverages specially designed positional encodings, causal sparse attention, and Key-Value Cache to effectively manage long-context references and ensure color identity consistency. Results demonstrate that Cobra achieves accurate line art colorization through extensive contextual reference, significantly enhancing inference speed and interactivity, thereby meeting critical industrial demands. We release our codes and models on our project page: https://zhuang2002.github.io/Cobra/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper abstract:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Cobra," a novel framework for reference-based comic line art colorization. Cobra addresses challenges in accuracy, efficiency, contextual consistency, and flexible control, which are often problematic in the comic production industry. The framework integrates extensive contextual references (over 200 images), color hints, and a Causal Sparse DiT architecture to maintain low latency while ensuring color identity consistency. The Causal Sparse DiT incorporates specifically designed positional encodings, causal sparse attention, and Key-Value caching.  The authors introduce Cobra-bench, a benchmark dataset, and claim Cobra achieves state-of-the-art performance in image quality, color ID accuracy, and inference efficiency. Codes and models are available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the architectural innovations that enable efficient processing of a large number of reference images for line art colorization. Specifically, the Causal Sparse DiT architecture with localized reusable positional encodings, causal sparse attention, and KV-Cache seem to be the core novelties. The paper emphasizes the practical impact on industrial comic production. Combining long-context references with color hints and achieving low latency is also a contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a practical problem in the comic industry and potentially improving the workflow of artists. Successfully demonstrating efficient handling of many references *while* maintaining ID consistency is a substantial achievement. The Cobra-bench dataset could also be a useful contribution to the community.  If the claims of state-of-the-art performance are substantiated, this could have significant impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defined problem in a practical application domain.
    *   Architectural innovations (Causal Sparse DiT) addressing specific challenges.
    *   Demonstration of scalability in handling a large number of reference images.
    *   Introduction of a new benchmark dataset (Cobra-bench).
    * Code and model release encourages reproducibility and adoption.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The abstract lacks detailed technical explanations of the Causal Sparse DiT architecture. While the high-level ideas are presented, a deeper understanding requires examination of the full paper.
    *   While the claim of state-of-the-art is made, there is a lack of concrete quantitative comparison with several contemporary methods. The value of qualitative examples might be limited.
    *   The paper has a potential weakness with a failure to generalize if the characters are different, as described in the limitations.
    *   The abstract relies heavily on claims (high precision, efficiency, and flexible usability) without providing sufficient evidence within the limited text.

* **Missing Details**: The abstract does not include any discussion of training loss functions used or any information on the network's training complexity. The discussion of dataset augmentation techniques also requires more details.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the architectural innovations are robust and translate to other tasks, the influence could extend beyond comic colorization.  The Cobra-bench dataset has the potential to influence future research in this area. However, the specialized nature of the application could limit broader influence.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper addresses a practical problem with seemingly effective architectural innovations. The ability to handle numerous references *efficiently* is a significant contribution. The inclusion of the new Cobra-bench dataset enhances the value. However, the limited details in the abstract and the potential for over-claiming require caution.  The specific limitations identified also temper the overall assessment. Given the promising results, a score is assigned with the anticipation of greater evidence substantiation in the full paper.

Score: 7
